POULTRY PEN,

HINTS AND INTERESTING FACTS AND CHATS FOR AMATEURS.

GO ON BREEDING.

In face of the present price of meat every poultry-keeper should set to work and see that he raises more chicks this year than last. The old argument about the food being dear should not deter us from this endeavour, because by so doing we are helping to provide for the needs of the community. We cannot all fight, neither can we make munitions of war, but we can produce some class of food which will -relieve the demand upon -the general store of the

country. Do not you realise that by growing an extra bushel of potatoes, if only for your own use, that it relieves the call upon the general stock of the country, and also that by producing your own eggs this will help to save a demand upon what another is producing. Many breeders will have finished hatching by this month in the ordinary way, but it is

essential that everyone should go on breeding 1 for another six weeks or two months. If from good laying strains, and the pullets are kept on the intensive principle, there is no reason why these should not be laying right through the winter. Every egg will be wanted, and we must each one do our part to increase the supply to meet an increasing demand both for ourselves and our wounded defenders. All those birds which will not come in for laying will do for eating, and I feel sure that before next Christmas there must be a big shortage of meat, which can be helped out if we produce another million or two chickens.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
BITS FOR BEGINNERS.

If the chicken is to grow up and be profitable as an, adult it must have ample room for exer- cise. Sq also must there be plenty of fresh air in the sleeping houses. Common-sense housing plans are all-important in chicken-rearing. The wise poultry-keeper will very carefully overhaul his young stock, and separate the culls from the rest. Market the culls as early as possible, to allow the other stock all the room possible. All tarring and whitewashing should be at- tended to.

Overcrowding must i all cases be strictly avoided.

Take care of the early-hatched cockerels; they will be required as breeders nest season. Confined birds must be given plenty of green food just now. - -

Dispose of any adult stock not required for next season.—" The Poultry World."

THE EVILS OF FOUL GROUND.

There is nothing which causes disaster among young chickens so greatly as foul ground. It has a very bad effect upon fowls of all kinds and all ages, but particularly upon chickens during the first few months of their lives. The same plot, if possible, should not be used for rear- ing two consec.utive years. If space is very limited, it is better in the long run only to rear half the number of birds, so that fresh ground can be used each season. It is customary to confine chickens, at any rate during the first few weeks of their lives, in small runs, and when this is the case it is very necessary indeed to remove the run every day, or at least every alternate day. Foul ground is an almost cer- tain forerunner of disease, and many of the complaints froni which chickens suffer are directly brought about through inattention to this...matter. Not only does tainted soil bring about disease, but it retards growth and stunts &velopment. It also means that the soil over which chickens are running is devoid, or almost devoid, of animal and vegetable growth, two extremely important items in the diet of young chickens:—Middlesex in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

LABOUR AND HARVEST.

Alleged Apathy Amongst Farmers Regarding Shortage.

At the monthly meeting of the Chamber of Agriculture, in London, on Tuesday, Mr. Ankers Simmons (Berks and Oxon) dealt with a resolution concerning the present shortage of labour. They were now on the verge of the hay harvests, he said, and labour was urgently needed for harvesting the crops. A resolution to the effect that the Council should urge the Board of Agriculture to appeal to the War Office to send drafts of men accustomed to farm work, under the control of a non-commissioned officer, to assist the farmers was carried unanimously. One of the suggestions made was that those Territorials who had not volunteered for foreign service should be utilised. "After using a pitchfork for several weeks," added the speaker, "they would be in splendid form for the bayonet." , Several "of the members complained of the apathy among the farmers in various counties on the subject of labour shortage. They had been circularised by the thousand but only a very small proportion of replies were sent in. It seemed to be very necessary to educate the farmers generally in regard to this urgent matter.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR WORKERS FOR LANCASHIRE FARMS.

The Lancashire Farmers' Association, repre- senting over 3,000 members, at their meeting at Preston on Saturday afternoon, considered the difficulties arising from the shortage of labour on farms and what steps were being taken to alleviate them.

Mr. Fitzherbert Brockholes, who presided, said Lady Derby had been approached by mem- bers of the Labour Exchange for Lancashire under the scheme for getting women to work on farms. The Lancashire Education Committee had decided to allow children under certain restrictions to be absent from school to help in agricultural work. He suggested the appoint- ment of a special representative committee to work with the representatives of similar societies, with power to take the necessary steps and draw upon the Society's funds to the ex- tent of £5O.

The secretary read a letter from the secretary of the Liverpool Municipal Officers' Guild offering to devote a week of their holidays to essisting fatmers to get in the hay harvest. It was purely volunteer work. A resolution was passed appointing the com- mittee as suggested by Mr. Brockholes.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
AGRICULTURAL AREA

Decreasing, But Less Rapidly.

Returns just issued show that in 1914 27,114,404 acres were under crops and grass on June 4th. This is 15,378 acres less than in 1913, and the smallest total returned since 1877. The average annual decrease between 1891 and 1913 has been nearly 40,000 acres, and this decrease is the smallest, with the exception of that in the year 1906, since the agricultural area began to decline twenty-two years ago.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
WASTED FRUIT.

There is a good deal of truth in the conten- tion that this country under-estimates the value of the home-grown fruit crops. We are so accustomed to have our needs supplied from external sources says "The Times," that our own productions have been treated as supple- mentary to the consignments from abroad in- stead of being regarded as the principal con- tributions. The nation cannot afford this year to permit waste in any class of food. There is probably a larger proportion of waste in fruit than in any other commodity. Every season we read of fruit being left ungathered because of over-stocked markets. There can be no ex- cuse for such a state of things in the coming harvests. The more perishable, or soft, fruits have to be marketed within so short a period that current •requirements are often exceeded and prices fail in proportion. The only means of avoiding the loss of wholesome fruit that is a familiar feature of the business is to devise a scheme for preserving the surpluslor future use. It should not be impossible to orpnise projects whereby the soft, stoned, and hard fruits not required for immediate consumption could be conserved as jam or bottled fruit.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

ROOM GARDENING FOR THE WOUNDED. There is nothing that delights a sick person more than the watching of some green growing thing. Convalescence, especially for a badly wounded man, is often a dreary business, but the days may be rendered much brighter if those in charge of the invalids start a room garden. Of course, many kinds of things may' be cultivated in an ordinary room, but on the present occasion the writer wishes to draw at- tention to a few very simple ideas, all of which are easy to carry out. In every case there is a novel feature which will make it of special in- terest. A great many interesting things may be done with a packet of grass seed. A pretty object for hanging in a window can be formed as follows :—Get a good-sized sponge, and arrange to hang it up by looping two pieces of string round it. Moisten it well, and scatter the grass seed thickly on it. Keep it damp, and in a week or so it will be one of the prettiest green objects imaginable. —" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
MULCHING EARLY PEAS.

The first early peas are generally given the warmest corner of the garden, the idea being to secure a few pickings as early as possible. This being so, it is necessary if the weather is very dry to give a good mulch of manure along each side of the row and water freely to encourage the pods to fill and sivell off. If this assistance is not forthcoming the secondary blooms often fail to set. In the case of the second early peas we have found it a good plan to pinch out the points of the bine, this encouraging the formation of bloom. These, if the weather is dry, should be encouraged in the same way as advised above for the first early varieties.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FOXGLOVE.

The foxgloves are such stately plants when well grown that it is surprising they are not more often met with in gardens. There is now such a numerous array of varied coloured forms —none perhaps more striking than the pure white form—that if a suitable position is selected one can make a fine feature with theni alone in the garden. The present is a good time to sow the seeds for flowering next year. This can be done in boxes or in well-tilled soil in the open air. It is well to move the seedlings when strong enough to good ground, setting them at a distance of 15 inches apart, transferring them to their permanent quarters in October.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
SOOT IN THE GARDEN.

Not only as a fertiliser to various kitchen garden crops, but as an antidote to insect pests, soot is very useful. In country districts it is of great value, and if more were used the various crops would be benefited. If peas which are only a few inches high and taking on a sickly yellow look were given a dressing of soot, hoeing this in previous to drawing the soil up to the plants before staking, they would quickly assume a healthier hue. We remember some years ago having seen a field which had been heavily dressed • with soot previous to sowing oats. The owner could not procure at the time sufficient soot to dress the whole field, and when the oats were fit for cutting one could see at a glance the part which had not received a dress- ing of soot, the straw in this portion being much shorter while the heads were not nearly so large and well filled.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====

===== 0002601_19150612_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
A DESOLATED TOWN.

In Blazing Ruins, But Still Huns

Shelled It.

Private James Gilbert, an employe at Bewsey Forge, who joined the 2nd South Lancs. lat September, writing to his wife. Mrs. J. Gilbert, 51, Pierpoint-street, Bewsey-road, says : "At the time of writing, June sth, I am in the best of ,health. We are quartered in dug- outs just outside a big Belgian town, which had previously been shelled by the Germans. There is scarcely a house or shop in the place which has not been hit, and many of them have been burned to the ground. This desolated town presents a sight which I shall never for- get. I had read in the papers about the burn- ing of the beautiful cathedral here, and we saw the ruins for ourselves, as we passed through the town. The inhabitants of the town had fled, leaving all their belongings, and the ruins had been on fire for five or six weeks when we got there. Yet the Germans had kept on shelling them. . . . I have visited the graves of Lieutenant Walker and Private Hayes. . . I am 'in the pink.'"

===== 0002601_19150612_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PICNIC!

A.S.C. Driver Tells Of His Experiences

Driver Reginald Wilkes, of the Supply Section of the A.S.C., whose home is in Thewlis-street, and who is attached to the Seventh Infantry Brigade, "somewhere in France," writing recently to a friend in Warrington, says: "We are expecting to move to a place where the terrible gas which is being used by the enemy is at the thickest. . . . I have been lucky, in a sense, for I have got a job which does not entail any actual fighting with a rifle, but I have been very frequently under shell fire—have seen the shells burst a matter of yards only away from me. . . ." He adds that in the solitude of the night watches, amid all the roar and horrors of war, the truth and the reality of the lessons of his Bible Class days come home to him with a force never realised in the piping days of peace. One takes the most serious aspect of things when it is a case of "Alive to-day, and, maybe,.dead to-morrow !" "I am writing this letter," he concludes, "in the loft of a cowshed, and the surroundings, to say nothing of the aroma, are not over-pleasant, but, of course, I did not come out here expecting a picnic, and in consequence things do not seem so bad as, I suppose, they really are."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HUMORIST IN THE TRENCHES.

Cheery Letter From A Corporal Of

The Cheshires.

Corporal A. G. Ball, of the Ist Cheshire Regiment, writing from "No. 2 Dug-out Field" to Mr. W. Watkins, 2, Heath-street, Stockton Heath, and who was previously employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros., makes some amusing references to the dangers which he is facing. " Midnight Excursions to the Firing-Line."

"I thank you," he says, "for the papers. We are enjoying forty-eight hours' rest in the sup- port trenches, .behind the firing-line. We have midnight excursions to the firing-line, by way of recreation. On those excursions we take food and ammunition to the boys who are keeping their eyes on the Huns. You would hardly believe how nice it is to wake up in the morn- ing and hear the birds singing—and the bullets whistling, the aeroplanes humming and the shells screaming. There are several varieties of German shells—the common or garden; the hourly service; the shell that is not satisfied unless it gets a whole street or half a dozen shops, and which specialises in churches and chapels; the whizz-bang and the shell which we have dubbed this-way-or-that—because you don't know whether it is going or coming back. Thon there is the German Sausage—quite the latest—which is the mine-searcher among shells, and the stuffing of which consists of geglinite, dynamite, turpinite, lyddite, cordite—and good- nite (beg pardon, night) if it happens to hit you. I was forgetting the Black Maria—the Hun's educated she'll, which is loaded with Kultur Handcuffs and, if it hits you, handcuffs you and takes you prisoner. It is not a violent shell at all ;.in fact it is quite gentle. Last, but not least, there are the Kaiser Stink-bombs, direct from the Royal Gasworks. There is a wide variety of methods from which death may be had, for, in addition to the foregoing, any of the following will be found effective : , Bullets, hand-grenades, rifle-grenades, bombs, dynamite explosions, poisoning, bayonets—or, if you are unlucky enough to be taken prisoner, starvation!

===== 0002601_19150612_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
The G-Hun Machine Man's Fate.

"There is also a G-Hun machine man in our immediate vicinity who can step the Ashton break-down with any Lancashire step-dancer. He had a brother, but we put him out of action the other day, and hope to do the same with this tiresome fellow before long. . . . I expect the war is making it pretty hard for the work- jag people at home to get along, but, like us, they will have to grin and bear it. . . . The B-hoys are in the best of spirits—not Lloyd George's. We are all on the tack' now—

THE SPIRIT OF LOVE.

Archdeacon Of Chester On What Is Essential In Christian Work.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING OF NEW ST. HILDA'S MISSION.

The new building of St. Hilda's Mission, built on the site of the old structure, in Slater- street„Latchford, was opened on Thursday night, Ni-hen a sermon was delivered by the Rev. W. L. Paige Cox, archdeacon of Chester, and vicar of Hoylake. The church was crowded. St. Hilda's Mission, which is an auxiliary of St. James' Church, Latchford, has been in existence about fourteen years, but the work was for a long time carried on in a wooden building. Nearly eleven months ago the work of building a stone and brickwork structure was commenced, the old building being demolighed •one half at a time, so that worship could still go on while the change was being made. The present building is a considerable improvement on the old one. It cost £1,200 to build, and not a penny of debt has been accuraulated. There will be a small additional call on the efforts of the congregation, in order to raise funds for furnishing. The next stage in the life of the Mission, which is doing, and has been doing for years, a splendid work, will be the building of a proper church alongside the new building. When this more ambitious task is completed, the present building will be used as a school. The church building is aapable of seating from a hundred and fifty to two hundred people. The builders are Messrs. W. and A. Ashton, of Bridge Foot, Warrington. There were present at the opening service, in addition to the Archdeacon, the following local clergy: The Rev. H. B. Firth (vicar), the Rev. C. F. Waller (curate), the Rev. H. H. Corsble (a former curate of the parish, but now vicar of Thornton-le-Moor); the Rev. H. V. Pigot (rector Of Grappenhall), the Rev. L. J. T. Darwall (vicar of Walton, Warriniton), the Rev. G; C. Briggs (vicar of Christ Church, Latchford), Rev. and Hon. J. E, Cross '(vicar of Stratton) and the Rev. John Gunner. Mr. H. Pevitt was at the organ. A CHURCH OF LIVING SOULS.

The Archdeacon took as his text: "And are built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets, Jesus Christ himself being the chief corner stone in whom all the building fitly Framed, together groweth unto an holy temple in the /Lord: In whom we also are builded to- gether for an habitation of God through the Spirit."—(Ephesians ii., 20, 21, 22.) The world was God's temple. It was filled with the presence of God. It furnished many and varied evidences of God's power and wisdom. Never were we more conscious of His presence ,and.work in the universe than at this time of

===== 0002601_19150612_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
IF GERMANS WOULD FIGHT FAIR!

Warrington " Terrier" Says It Would

Not Be Half So Bad Then.

Private Harry Leather, of the Warrington Territorials, who was employed by Messrs. Rylands' Brothers, and lived with his sister, Mrs. Holt, at 39, Birchall-street, has written an interesting letter to his brother, Mr. Thomas Leather, 58, Birchall-street, in which he ex- presses his desire to have a fair fight with the Germans, and his confidence in the result. Private Leather, a member of Wycliffe P.S.A., was keenly interested in billiard § and football. He was the son of the late Mr. Thos. Leather, of Crossley-street, who was employed by Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons. In the course of his letter Private Leather says:— "It is a very hard life out here, but we have to make the best of it, and I don't suppose it will do those of us who have the luck to pull through any harm. It will make us appreciate the more the value of a good home, and we shall not want to ramble far away from it after this lot." _ _ _

Referring to an impending move in whicli'the Territorials were to take part, the writer says that they fully intended if need be to hold the line to the last man. . . . " The Germans," he continues, "keep On using gases. It would not be half so bad if they would come out and fight fair. All the boys are eager to have a 'do ' at them: in fact, we -are all keen and in the best of spirits. In fact, if you came into our hut you would hardly think that there was a war on. We have all sorts of fun—it's a regular pantomime at times." The writer goes on to talk of the popularity of "The Examiner" in the Territorial trenches "as we all like to read of •the old town."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
BUSY BANK HOLIDAY.

Warrington Cinema Manager Tells Of

"A 28-Hour Day."

Writing to a friend in Manchester, Sergeant C. S. Lyon—who is well-known to cinema patrons in Warrington, having managed one of the local picture halls prior to joining the Royal Field Artillery—gives an interesting account- of how his battery—the 118th—spent Whit Monday at the Front.

"I was lying in my blanket close to the gun" he says, 'at about two a.m. when I heard heavy rifle fire on our front, and began to smell traces of gas (chlorine), so I nipped across to the telephone dug-out to make sure that the tele- phonist was awake. 'heavy attack with gas in our zone was the message Nnich greeted us, and while further particulars were coming along the battery got going. The gas began coming so strong that we could hardly stick it. We were all nearly choked, and our eyes were in a terrible state.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
"The Fun Really Began."

"At about 6 a.m. the fun reariy began. The Germans shelled us with gas shells—a high- explosive shell with liquid chlorine enclosed in a lead bottle inside the shell. The heat of the bursting charge releases and vaporises the chlorine, and if you miss the splinters you get the gas. The men flopped out all over the place, but we got them up on to a roof to recover, as the gas, being heavy, lies close to the ground. "At about 9 a.m. telephone communication stopped. Linesmen sent out to straighten things out find observation station blown to pieces, officer wounded, and a general mix-up. First line trenches hang on well, but gas has driven all supports out of reserve trenches, and our orders are to keep up our fire. So we dig into it again, gas or no gas, with reduced detachments, till 3 p.m.. when the weight of attack is off, and we shift our fire to the back of the German lines to prevent them massing for another attack.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
A Whitsuntide Holiday (!)

"At 7 p.m. there are only two sergeants avail- able for duty, so we toss and I lose, and go on with two men per gun for the night. Orders to stop firing come at 4.15 a.m., and having posted sentries and reported all correct, I get inside the blanket again at 4.30 a.m.—a Whit- suntide holiday of 281 hours. "The London tramway drivers would have found it more exciting than their day's outing to "Amstead 'Eath ' whilst they are waiting for their rise.' When I think of men striking for another penny an hour it makes me nearly as sick as the Germans' gas. And our gunners are doing this for is. Bd.d day. We must have done something of a record shoot. This gas is terrible stuff. People at home cannot realise it, or there would be no qualms about handing it back. It's like a blight on the land, kills insects, bleaches the grass, and withers the leaves on the trees, so you can imagine it is pleasant stuff to face. Luckily water is an excellent antidote— 'drink gallons of water and then be as sick as a cat' is the best remedy."

the year. A church composed of living souls was the ideal church, represented in ideal figure and form by'temples made with hands. The disciples of the Lord, of which company they all formed a part, constituted a large building "fitly framed together." The preacher went on to congratulate his hearers on their excellent achievement in build- ing the church at a cost of £1,200 and opening it without a debt existing. "Owe no man any- thing," was a good rule, and it ought to be regarded in Christian communities. They had done a splendid thing, and every one concerned would look back on it with satisfaction. A further sum would be necessary for the fur- nishing of the church. Drawing In And Binding Together.

As he came along, he saw many people in the streets. How many of them were worshippers in the House of God? If they did not worship God what a terrible privation and loss it meant to them; how terribla it must be for them to do their duty; on what a low plane their lives must be lived; and when trouble came, as it did so woefully often in these days, how terrible for them if they could not draw comfort, as they who worshipped God drew comfort, from the knowledge of the love of Christ! There was much to be done in that way, outside the church. By working earnestly and praying fervently many of those people at present out- side the church might be brought in. But it was not merely drawing people in that was wanted; what was equally necessary was to bind them together in love. It was love that cemented the building, and that repaired the. rift; it was that that brought people, together after differences.

A Great Christian Principle.

"Make up your minds," the Archdeacon exhorted, "that, you will live on the best of terms with all your fellow Christians and all with whom you have to do. If anyone has done you a personal injury, don't take it too much to heart; do not show too much resentment. One of the greatest principles of Christianity is that of being kind one to another, tender- hearted, 'forgiving one another, even as God, for Christ's sake, has forgiven you.' There is no better way in which you can help the, church than by displaying that spirit always. The work is done, not by us, but by the Spirit working in us. It is often hindered by man's indifference, opposition and apathy, but it is always at work—this Spirit—in a community. If we severally and individually yield ourselves as willing instruments of the Heavenly Spirit, and, walking in the Spirit, try so to live in an atmosphere of brayer and holy thought, then what is 'base and sinful in us will always be kept under, and we shall speak, and think, and act in accordance with the will of God. May He bless the preaching of the Word in this Place, and your work here and your private lives and inmost souls, and may this church become in the highest sense, a habitation for God through the Spirit."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
Splendid Service Rendered At Whiteeross Hospital. OVER A HUNDRED CHEERFUL " TOMNIIES."

"One of the most up-to-date, light, airy .and best equipped of the many temporary military hospitals I have been in so far !" That was the description applied to the Whitecross Hospital, Warrington, by an Army medico who recently inspected the building. And even the tyro in these matters who has been privileged to pay the bright, cheerily-cleanly wards a visit can see at a glance that the medico but stated a fact, and added no flattery. The Whitecross Hospital is, indeed, a model of what such places should be, and many a poor warrior broken on the field of battle will have cause to remember it, and the kindly, gentle ladies who minisfer to the maimed and sick therein, with feelings of gratitude. The hos- pital is the latest and up-to-datest addition to the local Poor Law Institution, but it has never yet been used for the purpose for which it is primarily intended—the succouring of the 4ndigent sick. Instead, so soon as it was built, it was placed at the disposal of the mili- tary authorities, who were—and are; so terrible is the total of victims of the war—on the look out all over the country for buildings that could be utilised for nursing back to health and strength the thousands of wounded who con- stitute part of the price of glory in the great campaign.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
PATIENTS FROM ALL PARTS OF THE WAR AREA.

The military authorities gladly availed them- selves of the offer, and a special committee of the Board of Guardians set themselves to work to furnish it in double quick time, and that they did their work well is attested by the fact that the building now lacks nothing in .the way of the furnishings and appurtenances that' can possibly be required to minister to the comfort of the patients who are being hurried into it from several " somewheres " in France and Belgium, and even from the farther away Dardanelles, where "Tommy Atkins" has been busy giving the Terrible Turk a salutary lesson that should make him regret that he ever put his finger into the very-hot pie.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ADMINISTRATIVE COMMITTEE.

The committee of the Guardians who have been given special powers to administer what one may call the business side of the hospital, consists of the Chairman of the Board (Mr. A. J. Jolley), the Vice-Chairman (Mr. W. Furness) and Mr. W. C. Brooks, who have the able and willing assistance of the Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) and the Master (Mr. Loxham). One of the first things decided upon when the military authorities accepted the offer of the hOspital was that for the time being if should be named in a manner that should not offend the susceptibilities of any "Tommy" who might be nursed back to health therein. And so after much cogitation it was decided to call it the Whitecross Hospital and, though it is doubtful whether it will continue to be known by that name when it is devoted to the purpose for which it was built, it is certain that it will mwer be known, officially at any rate, as the Workhouse Infirmary—a forbid4ing title which, at the behest of the Local Government Board. is falling into desuetude in all the more enlightened Unions in the country.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
ROOM FOR 200 PATIENTS.

Tip to yesterday there were 116 military patients in the Whitecross Hospital, a few of whom only arrived there a few days before. They are a wonderfully plucky and cheery set of fellows, prodigal with their smiles of gratitude for the ladies who tend to their wants and their hurts, and profuse in their expressions of thanks to the doctors who so assiduously attend to their welfare. The hospital is not yet full; beds have been provided for between 190 and 200 patients.

THE SUPERINTENDENT AND HER STAFF. In the person of Miss Enterton, the " Tommies " have a cheery, kindly and ex- tremely practical matron—superintendent-nurse is her official style and title, but she's "matron" to all her soldier charges. Under her work about twenty nurses, some of whom are attached to the regular staff and others of whom—volun- teers—come in for a few hours a day and give a much-appreciated helping hand. Some of the auxiliary nurses are attached to the local Red Cross detachments. The institution being an acknowledged training school for nurses, under the Local Government Board regulations, the administrative body are able to utilise the ser- vices of young ladies desirous of training for the noble profession of nursing.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
MANY REGIMENTS REPRESENTED.

The hospital stands separate and apart from the " House " of which it is eventually to form an integral part. It is a not unimposing build- ing, three storeys in height, and it is equipped with all the latest conveniences from the sani- tary and hygienic point of view. The patients belong to a large variety of regiments; indeed, they would seem to represent the bigger Part of the infantry regiments in the Army, as well as the artillery and some of the best known cavalry regiments. There are Englishmen, Irishmen, Scotclunen, Welshmen, Canadians, Australians and New Zealanders, as well as a Belgian and a Frenchman.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DOCTORS.

Dr. Hodgkinson, one of the best known and most skilful practitioners in the town, is the medical and surgical superintendent, and he is ably assisted in his good work by Dr. Jagger, the resident doctor. Since the hospital was opened some half-doien delicate operations have been performed, all of them successfully. Relatives and personar friends of the patients are at liberty to visit them any day, And, seeing that in the great majority of instances, a visit must necessitate a railway journey—and some- times ,a very long one at that—no harsh and in- flexible rule is, we understand, laid down with regard to visiting hours, within reason.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
GIFTS THAT WILL BE WELCOMED.

As. to matters of finance, the War Office will pay a sum which will cover the maintenance charges in connection with the hospital, but the capital charges—the interest on and redemp- tion of the loan for building, equipping, &c.— will fall on the usual shoulders; or, should not one say purses; viz., those of the ratepayers. Whilst the War Office will pay for the care and attention bestowed on the wounded soldiers, and for their physic and food, there will still be opportunities for local admirers of the gallant fellows ,to show that admiration in practical form. For instance, as everybody knows, " Tommy " loves his "fag" or his pipe; ,ergo. gifts of cigarettes and pipes and the wherewithal to charge the latter will be much appreciated. Again, " Tommy " is fond 'of writing home, or to his best girl, and, in order to do so, he requires paper, envelopes and postage stamps, and gifts of either, or all, or, in the alternative, money to buy the same, • will be esteemed. There are other things for which the money of the generous and long-pursed is also required. For instance, to-day it is proposed to take a number of the convalescent patient's on a trip to Pen- keth, and to give them a good time. . Again, the men are rather hard up for games with which to while away the tedious hours during which they are confined to the wards.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
CAN YOU LEND A MOTOR CAR?

The committee of the hospital are much in- debted to a number of motor car owners in the district, who have taken the patients out for long and interesting rides. At the same time it is .hoped that more owners will come forward and place their cars, for an odd afternoon now and then, at We disposal of the wounded war- riors.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
CONCERTS FOR THE PATIENTS.

Local singers are seeing to it that the patients in the hospital have plenty of music, quite a number of concerts of various descrip- tions having been given in the largest ward, greatly to the delight of the men. One of the most enjoyable of the concerts was' that given a few clays ago by Senor Angel Blanco, the celebrated Spanish violinist, Mrs. J. Sephton, and the Brooklyn Quartet—Messrs. Sephton (tenor), Crowe (alto), Dutton (baritone), and Hopkins (bass), with Mr. A. E. Trow at the piano.,

===== 0002601_19150612_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. T. T. JAMES' "ENCOURAGING" FAREWELL

Large Congregations To Wish Their Pastor God-Speed.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
NEED FOR THE HOPEFUL SPIRIT.

Last Sunday, at both morning and evening services at the Wycliffe Congregational Church, there were large congregations to hear the Rev. T. T. James' parting words on the eve of his going to the Front. The seryices were bright, and though naturally there was a sorrowful note, a spirit of " encouragement " prevailed, and much was done in the way of fortifying those before whom further sacrifices lie. It was intercession Sunday, and prayers were said for those belonging to the Church who are on active service.

In the morning the Boy,' Brigade attended in full foee.

DAVID'S EXAMPLE.

The Rev. T. T. James, who at the evening service preached from I. Samuel, xxi., 6—." David encouraged himself in the Lord his God "—said the Church would always be in his thoughts when away, and he asked his people to remember those at the Front in their prayers. He wished, he said, to speak a word of encouragement—there was nothing we needed at the present time more than a hopeful spirit. He meant the hope that sprang from a realisation of the facts, not from empty light- headed or selfish or callous indifference. He thought for the most part we realised the gravity of the crisis, not only for our own country, but for the world. One of the needs of the hour, was, having faced the whole of the facts, to speak and to receive the word of encouragement.

"And so," said Mr. James, "we turn to this word for a moment. David ' encouraged ' him- self in the Lord his God. This word is taken out of one of the Old Testament stories of con- flict. David found himself in a desperate situation. These Old Testament pictures of battle are grim and terrible enough, though I do not think they exceed in the way of horror and barbarity some of the things that are taking place in our modern civilisation to-day. Anyhow, the story says that in this hour of tremendous difficulty David ' encouraged ' him- self in the Lord his God. He talked to his own soul as-it were and said that new strength must be acquired by a reliance and dependence upon God.

Church. We talk of the chilling and arresting effect of this war 'upon the Church. but these days what a busy place the Church ought to be, for with her is the message of CCllsdation and comfort, the light that shineth in eark- ness, and the hearts and homes of England Ore needing these unspeakable gifts as they never did before. We are to be encouraged because out of the very burden of tragedy God has fashioned for us a new and magnificent oPPar. tunity of service in Ms name." Mrs. Smallshaw sang the solo "Abide with me," and suitable hymns were sung through- out, Mr. Payton being at the organ.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
DO OUR IDEALS JUSTIFY ENCOURAGE-

MENT?

"You will let me say first that we need such encouragement as a nation. And therefore it is as well to see what it means. For David it meant first of all getting himself into line with the will of God; it meant wanting the things that God wanted; it meant supreme anxiety about God's purposes being achieved. It may be as well to put this quite frankly to our- selves. If the object of this conflict in which we are engaged remains what we set it down to be at the first; if it is the defence of the weak against the strong that we have under- taken, if it is a crushed and mutilated nation whose ruined homes we have set out to restore, and whose shattered towns we are determined to see re-built, if it is the crushing of the spirit which declares might to be right that we are bent upon, if it is the overthrow of the idea that militarism and materialism are the supreme things in the world that we /strive for, if the ideals of justice and truth and righteous- ness remain our supreme motive and ideal, then we may encourage ourselves in the Lord our God. But if in the minds of any of us these ideals have •become obscured by any lower motives, if in our conception this is merely. a war as to whether England or Germany shall have the biggest markets in the world, then frankly I don't think that God is particularly interested in the matter. If it becomes a ques- tion of whether England can out-Prussianise the Prussian, so that in materially conquering Germany we succeed in imposing upon ourselves the very spirit of militarism against which we set out to fight, then frankly the less we ask God about it the better. But if we have kept our hearts clean in His sight, if we see to it that it is not mere bitterness or racial animosity, or material ambition which lie behind us, but the desire to see the triumph of the best and truest ideals, we may find encouragement in the changeless God, and be heartened and strengthened by our faith in Him.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
WONDERFUL OPPORTUNITY FOR THE CHURCH.

"And you will let me say that we need the note of encouragement in the Church. I do not know whether I can describe the feeling of despondency with which , I regarded this war at the first at it affected the Church. In those first dark days it seemed as though there were nothing to look fotward to except -arrested progress, work destroyed, activities benumbed. It was only as the days went by that it became clear to me that out of the very tragedy of those days God was providing a new and wonderful oppor- tunity for the Church. Yet the fact of it ought to be patent to every one of us. Think for a moment of the young fellows who have answered the call in this hour, and of the way in which the Church may hear an insistent' claim upon her to watch over them, and let them know that they are dear to her heart. They will becoming back soon, these lads, many of them will be coming back please God,/ and we want to hear them say: In the hour when we were away, facing cruel dangers by land and sea, in battle and in storm, the Church of Christ never let us pass from her memory, and the Church prayed for us, and the Church sent us a message now and then, and the Church taught us that she loves us as her own sons What an opportunity is here for the Church to claim for God the manhood of our country, and to weave about the men who are away the chains of affection which will ensure their loyalty and devotion in the coming days. " And,with the Church is the gift of healiag. We say that we have the message which puts the healing touch on broken hearts. Never was there a greater field for its exercise than there is at this very moment; never were there such countless homes where sorrow and anxiety and care need to be met by the Divine secret. which should be the supreme possAssion of the

Born in the year 1889, at Orihuela, Spain, Senor Angel Blanco commenced his musical career at the age of five. A pupil of Sara,sate, the renowned Spanish virtuoso, at the age of seven he was playing at classical concerts. At the age of ten he was the principal musician at two special concerts given before the old Royal Family of Spain. Then, for a period of nearly three years, he studied under Cesar Thompson, .of Brussels, the acknowledged premier violin tutor in the world.

At the age of seven he commenced a tour of Europe, the tour, with various short intervals, lasting fourteen years, during which period he visited most of the principal cities, being everywhere received with great applause. Dur- ing this tour he was frequently spoken of as the Youthful Sarasate." Senor Blanco is now touring the country as a music-hall artiste, and during the period of the war has, on numerous occasions, played gratuitously at concerts in aid of hospitals, &c. Another pleasant entertainment was provided recently by Mr. Jowett—who acted as solo pianist and provided the accompaniments—and a party consisting of Miss Whitmore (soprano), Miss Wardle (contralto), Mr. Walker (teaor), Mr. A. Davis (bass), Mrs. A. Landon (vill in), Private Neville t Coldstream Guards), and Driver Rosenberg (R.F.4.).

EARNEST, MANLY CHRISTIAN.

Mr. Eccles, on behalf of the deacons and members of the Church, at the conclusion 01 the communion service to which some 200 or 300 of the congregation remained, addressing Mr. James, said : "I should like to say to you, Mr. James, how deeply we appreciate the work you have done since you came amongst us over two years ago. We recognise to the full the high service Yon have been able to render in the church from Sunday to Sunday by the earnest, manlY. Christian character of your discourses; the in: terest you have taken in the Sunday school an° in all that concerns its welfare; in the Y.P.S.: the P.S.A., the Boys' Brigade, and last, but not least, the Cricket Club, showing them there as elsewhere how to play the game.' In all that you have been able to accomplish we associate, as I feel sure you would like us to, your good wife, Mrs. James.

"The Church willingly granted you an ex- tended 'leave to do work for which we believe you are eminently fitted. It will not by any means be a restful holiday, but we pray that God's blessing may attend your ministrations; that you may bring comfort and peace into the lives gf those of our fellow-countrymen, wile, it ma f be, will be called upon to lay down their lives in the cause of 'right against might that you may be kept safely whilst travelling by land and sea; that you may be preserved from all harm -whiLst ministering to thie wounded and the dying on the battlefield, an," that in due time we may be privileged to Wei- come you back amongst us in health and io strength, re-consecrated to the service of the Master and -determined to work, if possible, more zealously for Him and His cause in the future than even in the past. "May God bless you and keep you and cause His Face to shine upon you and be merciful unto you."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. DUKES' GIBE

11 Labour Leaders In Military Uniform

The delegates who attended the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, held in the Socialist Hall, Bridge Foot, on Tuesday evening, passed a. 'resolution declar- ing their strong opposition to compulsory mili- tary service, "believing that it would be a violation of that form of civic freedom which was one of the chief heritages" of the British race, that it would constitute a menace to the progress of the nation and that it was un- called for in view of the enormous number of enlistments since the war began. Further, the delegates, by their resolution, called upon Par- liament to offer the utmost opposition to the attempt 'which was being made in certain quar- ters to impose upon this country the very yoke which had proved one of the chief curses of Prussian militarism.

The Chairman (Mrs Hamblett) moved the resolution, and said that,. personally, he would have preferred a stronger one.

Mr.-C. Plinston seconded.

A Delegate observed that once compulsory military service was agreed to in this country it would be a case of "good-bye to trade union- ism." (Hear, hear.) The Chairman: It would be a step that would be fatal to the liberty of every trade unionist.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
OPPOSED TO COMPULSION.

Mr. C. Dukes, the secretary of the Council, said he was entirely opposed to any form of military compulsion; it was matter about which the 'individual had the moral right to consult himself only. He did not think that he would submit to being coerced even if a con- scription law was passed. If, he added, the people who called themselves leaders of Labour would leave military matters .to people who did understand them, and not deck themselves out in uniforms which they did not even know how to wear, but would look after the interests of the workers instead, the country would be in a much better state than it was at present. There was a class in the country who would love to bring every man under the domination of mili- tarism, in order that they might be in a posi- tion to do exactly what they were the first to denounce the Prussian Junkers for having done during the last quarter of a century. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150612_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
THE I.L.P. MANIFESTO.

The appeal referred to in the Trades Coun- cil's discussion was issued by the National Council of the Independent Labour Party, and is entitled "An appeal to the organised workers of Great Britain on 'The Peril of Con- scription.'" The appeal contains the follow- ing "The unscrupulous raising of food prices, the inordinate profits of financiers and contractors, the denunciation of the workers as selfish, un- patriotic, and drunken, and the demand for the setting aside of trade union and Factory Act restrictions all go to show the temper and the aim that is behind this attempt compulsorily to militarize the working class.

"It is -regrettably true that since the out- break of the present war the -principle of voluntary enlistment has been seriously in- fringed by capitalist pressure on the wage- earners. Workmen have been discharged from their work or otherwise intimidated in order to force them to join the Army. Enlist or starve' is but another variant of the relentless formula of class oppression. Only the wage- workers are terrorized in this way. _ _

"But the adoption of conscription would not lessen but aggravate the hardship of the workers. They would be swept into the Army wholesale, without any regard whatever to their convictions or their family obligations. Tens of thousands of workers who for good and sufficient reasons believe they are discharging loyally their duties as patriots and citizens in this crisis by remaining at their industrial posts would be press-ganged into the Army and Navy, not because they are really needed there, but simply in order to establish conscription." Trades Councils, Trades Unions, Labour Party and Socialist bodies are asked to secure the adoption of a resolution against compul- sory military service and to forward copies to the Prime Minister, the Secretary of State for War, Mr. Arthur Henderson, and members of Parliament.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKING - MEN MAGISTRATES.

Trades Council Calls For Their

Appointment.

At a meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, held on Tuesday evening, the question of the appointment of working men to local magisterial benches was discussed at some length. The matter arose out of the read- ing by the Secretary (Mr. Charles Dukes) of a circular letter from the National Council of the Independent Labour Party, which expressed the view that strong ground for complaint existed in many districts, owing to the fact that the Benches were composed entirely of representa- tives of the Middle and upper classes, and in- quired how matters stood in Warrington. The letter further opined that working class repre- sentatives should be appointed on county as .well as borough benches. Mr. Charles Dukes said that Warrington was one of the, few towns where they had no ad- visory committee to assist in the matter of the appointment of local magistrates. From what they saw of the local magistracy from time to time, he felt almost inclined Ao say that the Labour movement wanted on the Bench some men who were as biased in favour of the mem- bers of the class from which they came, as exist- ing magistrates were in favour of their class. (Hear, hear.)

ONLY FAIR PLAY AND JUSTICE WANTED.' The Chairman (Mr. Hamblett) said it was due largely to the apathy of the workers themselves that there were no real Labour representatives on the local Bench. (Hear, hear.) In asking for Labour magistrates, they were not asking for justices who would stretch the law for the convenience of the workers; all they wanted were men who would stand up for fair play and justice, and who really had some knowledge of the classes with whom they were called upon to deal.

It was decided to supply the I.L.P. with the information for which they asked.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
BR Fl Isil nIPERIAL COL NCH, OF' COMMERCE.

Alderman Bennett Advocates An

Imperial Parliament.

Alderman Bennett attended the first annual meeting of the British Imperial Council of Commerce in London on Wednesday of last week, and, speaking on the report, said that, gratifying as it was in many ways, he noticed that, as yet, only fifty of the British Chambers out of something like a hUndred had actually joined the Council. He also noticed that the Manchester and Oldham Chambers still held aloof, and he• hoped that, in view of old con- troversies, there was not anything sinister in this fact. (Laughter.) The Chairman had told' them that the motto of the Council was "Union for Commerce, Union for Defence." He thought it ought to be made still more comprehensive, and to read, "Union for Com- merce, Consultation and Defence." He trusted that, when they came to discuss the question of commercial union in the future they would approach it in a leas pedantic spirit than here- tofore. (Hear, hear.) And the desirability of consulting with the Colonies, tremendously important before this awful war, became im- perative now. Personally, he thought a good beginning might be made by the inclusion of colonial representatives in the Imperial Cab- inet for the discussion of Imperial affairs. But that was only a -temporary expedient, and he shared the opinion so eloquently expressed, the other day, in London by the new Colonial Secretary, Mr. Bonar Law, that the only ulti- .mate solution was to be found in the creation of a true Imperial Parliament. That was a goal worthy of all their efforts and the best that all of them could give. (Cheers.)

IN THE Counirs.

WHY HE INAS ABSENT.

Soldier NI ho Said He Got Drunk And Lost His Pass.

Michael Sheriden, of the Labour Section of the Army Service Corps, was charged at Mon- day's Police Court with being an absentee from his.regiment, and it was stated that at 5.30 in the morning he was seen by a poliee officer at Bank Quay Station, and asked for his pass. He was unable to inoduce one, and when the officer inquired why, tendered the explanation that, he got drunk in London the night before and lost it. The excuse did not suffice, and, when taken to the Police Station he admitted that he was an absentee.

Prisoner told the Bench that he was very sorry and, the Chief Constable having stated that an escort was on its way to fetch him back, he was ordered- to be handed over to them upon their arrival.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Dili .NK "IN THE VAULTS."

Police Officers' Discovery " When The

Sleeper (Was) Awakened."

Charged with havink been drunk on licensed premises on Saturday, Wm. Wheldon, who has no fixed abode, but stays at common lodging- houses, was at Monday's Borough Police Court, fined 95., or ordered to go to prison for seven days. The evidence was to the effect that Detective-Sergeant. Reddington and another officer went into the vaults of the Lion Hotel at 8.50 on Saturday night and there found the prisoner, in a drunken condition and fast asleep on a bench in front of the bar. Witness called a barman's attention to him. The barman went across to him, and experienced some diffi- culty in waking him up. Eventually he• was aroused and put outside, whereupon he called the barman a pig, and used bad language. He was locked up.

Prisoner said he must admit that he was drunk, but added that it was his first offence. Ire had been working for two years at the Vulcan Iron Works.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST THEIR BEARINGS.

Predicamenis Of Two Saturday Nigit

" Drunks."

John 13ligh. who said he had been working at a wireworks in the, town, was charged at Monday's Borough• Police Court with having been helplessly drunk in "the wee 'sma hoors " of Sunday morning. _ _

The evidence 1%---as to the effect that when patrolling a passage at the rear of Arpley- street, a constable heard a man apparently talking to himself, and when he went to in- vestigate:found the prisoner lying flat on his back. He took the prisoner to the Police Station. Prisoner: I couldn't find my way back to the town, so I laid down for safety and fell asleep on „the ground.

The Chief Constable: He has sufficient money on him to pay a fine according to scale. The Bench imposed a penalty of 9s. or seven days.

A similar eXplanation as to why he was found where he was found, was tendered at the same Court by Win. Clooney, against whom the charge was also one of drunkenness. At 1.25 on Sunday morning a constable was at the rear of Bridge-street and chanced to examine the backyard of an unoccupied shop. There he found the prisoner fast asleep in a shed. He woke him up and found that he was drunk and incapable. As he was unable to give a satisfactory explanation of himself he was, with assistance, conveyed to the Police Station.

"I lost mv way on Saturday night and couldn't get hack into the town," said the prisoner. "so I turned in there." A similar penalty was imposed.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
TICKETLESS AND TROUBLESOME.

Raih'ay Constable Assaulted At Central Station.

Before the Borough magistrates on Tuesday,' Harry Macmillan, of 594, Liverpool-road, Irlam, was charged with obstructing and assaulting P.C. Thomas Clarke, of the Cheshire Lines Railway Police, whilst in the execution of his duty at the Central Station, Warring- ton, on Monday, June 7th.—He pleaded guilty. P.C. Clarke deposed that on the arrival of the 8.30 ex-Liverpool express at the Central Station on Monday evening he was informed that pri- soner had refused to produce a ticket or to give his name and address. Witness went to the compartment in which the man was seated and himself asked to see the ticket, but prisoner said " What's it got to do with you?" Witness then asked his name and address, and, as he refused that also, he was invited to step out of the carriage.

A Blow in the Face.

Thereupon he struck witness in the face and became very violent. With assistance witness ejected him from the compartment and got him to the Police Station. He appeared to have had a drop of drink, but he was far from being drunk. - - - - -

Macmillan told the Bench that he had no recollection of the affair and the Chief Con- stable said that he was quite unknown to the local police. He was fined 20s. .

===== 0002601_19150612_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED IMPUDENT AMOR- CYCLE THEFT.

Man Who Is Also Said To Be Deserter Is Remanded.

A young man giving the name of Victor Edward Claremont, and said to have ne occupation, and to be of no settled abode; stood in the dock at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning to answer charges of having stolen a Lewis motor-cycle, valued at EH, the property of the L. and N.-W. Railway Co., and of being a deserter from the Army Service Corps since November 27th.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
How He Is Said To Have Got the Cycle.

The Chief Constable said, that the man enlisted in the Black Watch a few days ago, and on Monday was Orli a ticket to go to the North of Scotland to join that regiment. When he arrived at Crewe Station he apparently saw a motor-bicycle, and he directed a porter to place it in the train. Arriving at Warring- ton, he alighted with the cycle and went to a shop in Winwick-road and obtained some petrol. The shopkeeper, noticing that the man had little or no knowledge of the working of the, machine, had his suspicions aroused and called on a special constable, and between, them they set a trap • for the prisoner. They fillea the carburetter with petrol and then turned the tap off so' that the man 'would not be able to go more than a ,hundred yards. To account for his ignorance of the mechanism of the cycle, the prisoner said he ;had only been out with i three weeks; and added that he wanted to get back to Crewe.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
Said He Had Forgotten His Licence.

At length Mr. Dendy, the special constable, said to him "I have had a telephone message, and I have to ask you to produce your licence." Prisoner replied that he had forgotten to bring it with him. Mr. Dendy then. said "You must come to the Police Office." On the way there Mr. Dendy met a pcilice officer and handed prisoner over to him. There were further inquiries to be made, .and the -Chief Constable asked that prisoner might be remanded for a week.

Prisoner: I would like to be sent back to the Army.

The Chief Constable: They probably won't want to receive him.

The remand asked for was granted.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
A SILLY QUESTION.

'roper Who Asked Policeman What He

Had To Do With It!

At Monday's Borough Police Court, Louis Yellop, of no permanent address, was ordered a month's imprisonment, with hard labour, for having been drunk and disorderly in Butter- market-street on Saturday, an offence to which he pleaded guilty. The evidence was to the effect that at 8.35 on Saturday night an officer saw the. man in a drunken state in Buttermarket-street and heard him make use of most disgraceful language. When the constable remonstrated with him he asked "What the you?"

- The Chief Constable said this was the man's third appearance for drunkenness in a month, and as recently as May sth he was awarded 14 days' hard labour.

The Bench now ordered the prisoner a month in goal and he left the dock murmuring thanks.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
DRUNKEN MAN'S NASTY FALL.

Constable Renders First-Aid And Then

Locks Him Up.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday morning, Albert Egan, a stranger to the town, was charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly on Bank Quay Station on Saturday. A police officer said that at 2.45 on Saturday afternoon he was called to the railway station and there found the prisoner causing a dis- turbance on the down-platform and obviously drunk. He was bleeding copiously from a wound in his forehead, having slipped down on the platform. Witness took him to a waiting- room and assisted to dress his injury and then conveyed him to the police station, where he was locked up.

Blamed It On To A Clay Pipe.

Prisoner told the Bench that 'it was all because of an old clay pipe"; it `got him under," and caused him to fall down and hurt his head, after which he remembered no more.

The Chief Constable: I yave heard all sorts of excuses put forward, but I don't think I have ever heard the old clay pipe tale told before. The Chief added that this was the fifth time the prisoner had been charged with drunkenness during the past twelve months. He was a stranger to the town, and, according to the scale, which the magistrates had drawn up, he was liable to a penalty of 335. 9d. Prisoner: Oh, gentlemen! Give me just a chance and I'll never come here again. The Chief Constable: He was here only a few days before this. A fine of 30s. (or 21 days' hard labour) was imposed.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLED THE RHUBARB.

Prisoner Who Said He Did Not Steal "In A Sense."

Charged with stealing rhubarb to the value of twopence from a garden adjacent to the Warrington Cemetery, an aged man, named James Stockton, of Helsby-street, told the magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Monday that he did not intend to steal the vegetable, adding " I thought it would be all right, being used to the ground, and, please, this is the first offence in all my lifetime."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
Spotted by a "Special."'

The Chief Constable outlined the facts. The rhubarb,, he said, belonged to a Mr. Goodyear, who was employed in the Cemetery, and it was growing in the garden of the house which was allowed him, as part of his remuneration. At about 6.55 on Saturday evening Mr. Mason—a schoolmaster, who is also a special constable— drew the attention of a policeman—P.C. Barr— to the fact that man was in a bed of rhubarb in the garden. The constable went to the spot and saw the prisoner climbing a wall, with a number of sticks of rhubarb under his arm. The constable taxed him with stealing it and he replied "No; Mr. Goodyear, the owner, has told me I can take some rhubarb when I like." Thereupon the officer took the prisoner to Mr. Goodyear's house, and that gentleman said that there was no truth at all in the statement. Prisoner then remarked: "I did not steal it, in a sense. I was told that I could take some, when there was enough, but, as a matter of fact, I took this 'on my own."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
Felt Compelled to Prosecute.

Mr. Goodyear told the Bench that the man had no right at all to take the rhubarb. Be- cause of previous losses he had sustained from his garden he felt compelled to prosecute in this case.

Prisoner was ordered to pay a fine of 65., or to go to prison for seven days.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
A SOLDIER'S "HOME."

Husband At The Front; Wife In Prison For Child-Neglect.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning, Ann Stark, the wife of a soldier on active service, was again brought before the Court on a summons for neglecting her chil- dren, Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, appearing to conduct the prosecution on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
Woman Given Chances.

Mr. Browne reminded the Bench that the case first came before them on April 13th, and. was then adjourned for four weeks, on defend- ant promising to mend her ways. She did aim- prove for a time, and the case was adjourned for a further four weeks in order that she might improve still further, but she had behaved, no better whatever. He , (Mr. Browne) had three independent witnesses who would tell the Court that they had done all they could to keep the woman sober and away from drink, and to get her to conduct herself properly, as a soldier's wife should in the absence of her husband. But, these well- intentioned people had failed. Had Been Drinking Very Badly.

The first. of the three witnesses referred to by Mr. Browne—whose names, at the request of the Bench, are suppressed—was a married woman of 25, who declared herself unable to read the oath, a statement which caused the Chairman (Councillor Roberts) to remark: "What! Only twenty-five and can't read! What school did you go tor' Witness: I only went to school when little. Mother was very bad and I was the oldest girl and I had to stay at home. The Chairman: At what age did you leave school?

Witness: About thirteen, sir. I can't read so very well; anyhow, I can't read big words like those on the card.

[The words "on the card" are :—" The evidence I shall give to this Court shall be the truth, the whole truth and nothing buf the truth; so help me God !"] All the Neighbours Had Done Their Best.

In answer to Mr. Browne, the witness said she Was a neighbour of the defendant's, and took an interest in her case, and had done her best by talking to her, and in other ways, to get, her to go on better. Since the last adjourn- ment, however, the defendant had been drink- ing very badly; indeed she was a disgrace to the street in which they both lived. Men visited the house. All the neighbours have done their best for her.

Defendant admitted that "she had a glass now and then," and said that the witness sent her in a bottle of 'beer only a few days ago. . • Witness: Yes, because I thought she was behaving herself. • The Chairman: So you thought the way to help her to behave herself was to send her in beer!

The' second witness called said she lived close to the defendant, and had tried to do her best for her, but she did not seem to reform at all. Witness had sent her and the children food,

and that despite the fact that the defendant was drawing 235. a week. "Men came to the front and the back," and witness had, herself, "chucked two out at twelve o'clock at night." Defendant: The two men brought food for the children. She added that her children got plenty of food.

Witness: I don't know so much; seeing that they come begging, for food. Defendant, she added, had actually sent her four-year-old girl for bottles of beer.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
" A Disgrace to the Place."

Another neighbour said she had also done her best to try and reform the defendant, who was a disgrace to the place, and wanted moving. Witness's boys had found bottles of beer hidden _ _

in defendant's ash:tub, awaiting the time when she could take them into the house without being seen. Last Saturday night witness saw a man in the house, gnd he was only one of dozens, some of whom actually belonged to the neighbourhood. Men had stayed the week-end with defendant, whose children had actually noticed the fact. Defendant's conduct was scandalous, and had got the street a bad name.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
" No Hope of Improvement."

Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., said he visited the house the previous day and found the bedding filthy and ,the house, gener- ally, dirty, whilst it was obvious that the woman had been drinking. There was faod on the table, and the youngest child was eating from. a plate with its fingers. The other two children , were out. On May 26th, witness visited the house at 10.45 at night, and found the woman out. anti the children in bed, whilst the house was in the same filthy, dirty con- dition. Next morning he went round at 9.30 o'clock and found the children unwashed and, the woman recovering from the effects of drink. There was not a scrap of food in the house. Witness had previously cautioned the woman about her drinking habits: he had seen her "crying drunk." He paid further visits to the house on May 31st and Monday-of this week,' and from what he saw there was no hope Of improvement. When the woman drew the separation allowance the children got plenty of food for a day or two, but at the end of the week there was never anything in the house.

Only Two Gills of Beer a Day. The woman told the Bench that, she paid 4s. 9d. a week in rent, and on top of that she had to pay insurance and instalments for furniture procured on the hire-purchase system. As to her drinking, why she, gener- ally, only had a gill in the morning and another at night, for sapper. Inspector Wainwright: When I visited the house on the 27th ult., I found that the woman had even pawned the ashpan, although she ()illy drew her money the previous Tuesday. In sentencing the woman to two months' im- prisonment, the Chairman said that she had had two opportunities and had shown no im- provement. Seeing that her husband had nobly gone to the Front, to fight for his country, the least she could have done would have been to try to behave decently to his chil- dren whilst he was away. The woman was led to the cells crying piteously for her children.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMAN'S ADMISSIONS.

Concerned In Theft From Salvation Army Officer.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning, before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair) and Messrs. J. Rymer Young and W. T. Williams, Martha Adamson, a youngish woman of respectable appearance, was charged with, between April 29th and May 13th, obtaining by means of false pretences, from Ada Clara Ward, food and lodging to the value of <V.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
Traced to Liverpool and Arrested.

The Chief Constable said it was necessary to ask for an adjournment for seven days. The prosecutrix, Mrs. Ada ,Clara Ward, resided with her husband at 9, Hamilton-street. Prisoner called at Mrs. Ward's house on April 29th and was taken in and given board and lodging. At the time she was working in a laundry in Man- chester-road. It appeared, however, that she left that laundry and then she went to her landlady and told her that she had got em- ployment at Messrs. Crosfield's, and, that statement being believed, she was allowed to remain on. She left the house at 8.40 each morning, to give colour to the story that she had obtained a job, and that went on until May 13th, when she absconded. The matter was reported to the police and a warrant was issued, and on Monday the prisoner was traced to Liverpool and there arrested. Since her arrest it had been ascertained that she was supposed to be mixed up in the larceny of £5 from the house of a Salvation Army Officer whom she had been in the habit of visiting. She admitted the false pretences and also that she had a portion of the ~-05 that was stolen. Prisoner bad nothing to say and was re- minded for a week.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
OWING TO HIS WIFE.

Gaol For Husband Unless He Pays Up

Arrears.

Charged at Wednesday's Borough Police Court with being £1 3s. in arrears under a maintenance order granted to his wife Ellen, Edward Mee, of 16, Manley-street, was ordered to pay up or go to prison ,for a month, the Chief Constable stating that if he made an attempt to pay up the police would not press the commitment.

Prisoner admitted that he owed the money, and said he could not pay because he had been out of work.

The wife said that after the separation order was granted her husband got regular work, but could not keep it, as he stopped away drinking. The week he left he drew a week's pay which the firm had kept in hand, but she got none of it. • The Chief Constable said that the order was made on October 23rd of last year, and under it the husband was ordered to pay ss. a week. He had been before the Court Several times for various offences. He told the wife that he was not going to work any more; he was going to rest his bones for the future.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
A DEAR GAME.

Street Football Costs Four Youths

A Sovereign.

Four youths were summoned at Thursday's Police Court for having played football in the street at School-brow. Their explanktion was that the game started on private property, but the ball happened to go into the street just as the constable came on the scene, and they ran after it.

The Chief Constable said it appeared that the game was Waged partly on private property and partly in the street. The worst feature of the case was, according to the officer who caught the lads, that they were shouting and swearing.

They -were ordered to pay ss. each, the Chair- man (Mr. J. Rymer Young) remarking: ''Very dear football that, isn't it?"

===== 0002601_19150612_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A COSTLY MOTOR RIDE.

'three Breaches of Rye-Law Cost Warringlonian 70s. ,

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday,. John Cunningham, of Froghall-lane, Warring- ton, was summoned for riding a motor-cycle without a light, at 11.45 at night on May, 30th. rie was further summoned for not having a rear red light, and also for failing to stop when called upon to do so by a Constable.—He was represented by Mr. Guy Williams, solicitor to the Automobile Association, and denied the first and third offeuces, but admitted the second.

The police evidence was to the effect that a constable .saw the, defendant driving a. motor- cycle in Chester-road, Sutton, without either head or rear lights, and blew his whistle and nabbed his lanip, but defendant took no notice.

A little further along the road, however, he had to stop owing to engine trouble. There was a side-car attached to the motor-cycle.

Defendant said he neither saw nor heard the constable, and that he did not notice that his head light was out—he had been riding with it turned down in order to eke out his supply of carbide, which had run short—until a passenger in the side-car called his attention to the fact. It was then that he stopped, and not because h., had engine trouble. For the first offence defendant was fined 55., for the second one, a like amount, and for the

third—which the Chairman (Sir Fredk. Not- man) said was a most serious matter-60s.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY AFTERNOON GAMBLING.

Boys and Young Men Before 'file Magistrates.

Sixteen defendants—whose ages ranged from young manhood down to schoolboy-years, and two of whom wore the khaki uniform cif the Warrington Territorials—were summoned at Thursday's Police Court for playing the gambling game of "Head It!" in a public pas- sage at the rear of Jockey-street, on Sunday, May 9th. Gambling of this form, it was stated in evidence, seemed to be growing chronic in the neighbourhood, taking place three times a week, or more. On the Sunday in question the noise and the occasional swearing of the players, so annoyed one of the neighbours that she emptied potato peelings over the wall on to them. The gambling" said one of the wit- nesses "is a perfect nuisance, particularly on Sundays." One of the younger boys denied that he took any part in the gambling, and said that he went to Sunday school on the afternoon that if was alleged to have taken place, whilst another said that he enlisted the day before and went down to the Police Station, to see if the doctor would pass him, just about the time the game was in progress.

Five of the defendants were discharged for want of evidence, and one was let off because he had "joined up." Of the remainder, those above 16 years of age were fined 10s., and those below that age ss.

1110ROLS AND INTOXICATED.

Rejected Soldier In The Dock.

Before Mr. J. Rymer Young and other magis- trates, at the Borough Police Court on Thurs- day morning, John Broadhurst, a labourer, of no settled abode, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Buttermarket-street at 10.30 the previous night. The evidence showed that when he was first noticed by the constable who arrested him he was standing at the top of Academy-street, talking to passers-by, especially those of the fair sex. He stopped two women and they had a job to get past him. Then he went into the roadway 'and approached three other women and got in between them and said something insulting to them. When arrested he became very violent, and it took a sergeant and two constables to get him to the lock-up. He had a quart bottle, of beer in his pocket. The Chief Constable said the man had an Insurance card in his possession, which showe& that he had only worked one week out of 21. Presumably he had been in the Army part of the time, however, and had been discharged in consequence of being not likely to become an efficient soldier. It was the. R.A.M.C. that he joined.

The Chairman: British soldiers don't gener- ally insult women.

The penalty was 13s. or 14 days.

NO HOME AND NO MONEY-AND

DRUNK!

Pliliht Of A Wizened Old Woman.

A diminutive old woman giving the name of Ann Shaw, was charged at Thursday's Borough Police Court—before Messrs. J. Rymer- Young (in the chair) and A. J. Jolley and Dr. Naden— with having been drunk and incapable in Mer- sey-street the previous evening. A constable said he saw the old woman stag- gering about the footpath and obviously too drunk to take care of herself, so he brought her to the Police Station. . The woman, speaking in refined tones, said she had no home and no money. She was extremely sorry for what had happened. She had had rheumatic fever twice and very little intoxicating drink overcame her. Added to the fever, six months ago_ she was run over by a motor car, and that had combined to leave her very weak. The Chief Constable: I should think she was asking for a third dose of rheumatic fever, getting drunk like this. • She was fined 65., with the alternative of seven days' imprisonment.

"I'‘‘o Inebriates Who Chose Footpath As Resting-Place.

He was so drunk that he was un- able to give an account a himself, so was locked UP.
