STEALING BY FINDING.

Man Willi £l3 And Poor Woman's Purse.

Thomas Curley, of 27, Bank-street, a labourer, was charged at the Borough Police Court, yes- terday morning, with feloniously stealing by finding) a purse containing 10s. the pro- perty of Ellen Urey, of The Cottages, Orford, on June 10th. The Chief Constable said that at ten minutes to three on Thursday afternoon, Mrs. Urey,

whose husband was in .the Army, *as sitting on a seat in Bank Park, in company with her brother, when the latter said he Was going to the baths. She said she would go part of the way with him, and they got up and walked off. After walking some distance Mrs. trey found her purse was missing, and went back to the seat on which she had been sitting. She looked about for her purse, but could find no trace of it. The prisoner had been noticed by her, as he sat on a seat near Where she and her brother had been. He was still there, and she went up to him and asked him if he had seen anything of a purse. He replied: "No; I have only got my own." She was not satisfied and went and told her story to a constable, who went with her to the man,- told him that the lady had lost a purse and asked him if he knew anything about it. Prisoner replied in the negative, but the constable was not satisfied with his statement, and invited him to go along to the Police Station. The three started off for the station, but on the way prisoner produced the missing purse and admitted picking it up near the seat where Mrs. trey had been sitting. The prisoner repeated his admission in the dock.

The Chief Constable pointed out that the theft was a particularly mean one. With ,el 3 or £l4 in his own purse at the time, prisoner stole the money of a poor woman whose hus- band had gone to fight for his country. Tte police knew nothing else against the man.

- The Bench (over -which the Mayor presided), fined prisoner £5.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
ZEPPELIN DESTROYED.

.C. For Young Naval Flight

Lieutenant.

The Secretary of the Admiralty reports:— At 3 a.m. on Monday, Flight Sub-Lieutenant R. A. J. Warneford, R.N., attacked a Zeppelin in the air between Ghent and Brus- sels at 6,000 fe,eh. He dropped six bombs, and the airship exploded, fell to the ground, and burnt for a considerable time.

The force of the explosion caused the Moran() monoplane to turn upside down. The pilot succeeded in righting the machine, but had to make a forced landing in the enemy's country. However, he was able to re-start his engine and returned safely to the aerodrome.

TWENTY-EIGHT MEN DIE WITH THEIR AIRCRAFT.

Messages from Amsterdam show that the Zeppelin carried 28 men, who perished with the airship. The airship dropped on an orphan asylum, killing two nurses and two orphans and wound- ing many others. THE KING'S PLEASURE.

•

.8 The Secretary of the Admiralty made the 101 following announcement on Tuesday:— rt, His Majesty the King has sent the following r, telegram to Flight Sub-Lieutenant Warneford : , ..,

I most heartily congratulate you upon your splendid achievement of yesterday, in which you, single-handed, destroyed an enemy Zeppelin. I have much pleasure in conferring upon you the Victoria Cross for this gallant act. GEORGE, R.I.

This must be the quickest grant of the V.C. on record. But it is in keeping with the rapid progress made by Lieutenant Warneford. This young aviator, enlistegl in the Sportsmen's Battalion on January 7th, and a month later was transferred to the naval air service. Fifteen days afterwards he had become so pro-. ficient in flying that he was able to obtain his pilot's certificate. And now, only thirty-six hours after bringing down the Zeppelin with which he fought in the dawn above Belgium, be is awarded the highest distinction conferred on heroes. The whole nation applauds both the award and the King's promptness in making it. Flight Sub-Lieutenant Reginald Alexander John Warneford was born in India, in Cooch Behar, a native State in the north-east corner of Bengal, on October 15th, 1892, and is known to -but a small circle of friends in England. At the instance of General Joffre the French Minister of War has decided to confer the Cross of the Legion of Honour on Flight Sub-Lieuteb- ant Warneford.

c'.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Green's Waterloo.

Mr. Green had been paying four dollars a week for board; his appetite constantly in-. creased. Finally his landlady saw that she must either sell out and quit or raise he boarder's rate. One day, after watching him feverishly devouring plateful after plateful, she plucked up courage, and said Mr. Green, I shall have to raise your board to five dollars."

Mr. Green looked up with a start, then in -a tone of consternation he said:

"Oh, Mrs. Small, don't! It's as much as I can do now to eat four dollars' worth."

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JUNI: 12, Dr.,

THE SAVINGS OF THE PEOPLE.

The present is a time for organisation of our resources and concentration of effort; and perhaps the greatest asset we have, if properly managed, is our wealth. Our resources will have to be husbanded, however, and it is note- worthy that the Germans are boasting, among other things; that their frugality and system Will enable them to beat us in the long run,

even in the field of finance. If they could beat us there the outlook would be dark indeed; but we believe the British people will be able to adapt themselves to the necessities

of the situation and so provide the last silver

bullets that are to claim ,the victory. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has told the people to save all they possibly can, whether in small or large amounts, and so be able to lend to the Govern- ment the sinews of war. He was misunder- stood, butihe has made it clear since, that he wishes all classes to do their utmost accord-

lag to their means, to be ready to place money at the disposal of the Government, which will be a beneficial and-safe method of investment. There was a time when certain people, nsed to decry thrift because it was considered by

them to lower the standard of living, and hence the cost- of living. It was argued, that if the cost of living were reduced it would be a lever in the hands of capitalists whereby wages could be lowered; and the man who saved was looked upon, not as a friend of the worker, but as an enemy to his "class." But class distinctions are rapidly disappearing. The demand for national service, both in tile field, in the workshop, and in the playing ground, has cleared away many ill-conceived notions, and to-day each man, woman and child is imbued with the desire to take his or her place in the State. Economy is demanded because it is vital to the country. Economy of strength, time, re- sources, labour, capital are all more or less necessary to those' who wish to climb addi- tional steps in the march of progress. To the. individual it spells respect and independ- 'ente ; to the State, it is indispensable. The country can find use for whatever may be saved in any of the forms of economy men- tioned. It would be well for everybody to recognise that we are at war; not only the soldiers who are at the Front, not only our sailors upon the sea, but those of us who are left at home. We, as well as they, have a duty to perform to the State, and it is for every one of us. to see that we do not neglect it. That duty was explained very clearly at -the annual meeting of the Trustee Savings Banks Association, held, in Perth last. week (reported elsewhere) a'nd we commendit to the notice of our readers. It is rather significant to note that Sunday was the day chosen for receiving deposits when savings banks were first brought into operation, in view, doubt- less, of the motto of the three directors of the bank, viz.: Upon the first day of the week, let every one of you lay by him in store, as God hath prospered him" There was a quiet ,modesty and simplicity about their intention Which seem hardly commen- surate with the far-reaching effects of ,their aims, and only warm enthusiasts can recog- nise beneath this almost primitive scheme the broail, massive structure which has been erected upon so simple, though solid founda- tion. ,But the pioneers of the movement built better than they knew; and to-day the savings ban'ks of the country are a. tribute to their untiring industry and refreshing energy for the benefit of the Working classes. Writ- ing in an early number of the "Edinburgh Review," Francis Jeffrey said: "It would be difficult, we fear, to convince either the people or their rulers that the spread of savings banks is of far more importance and far more likely to increase the happiness and even the greatness of the nation than the most brilliant success of its arms,_ or the most stupendous improvements of its trade or its agriculture. And yet," he added, " we are persuaded that

it is so."

It was not, however, until the year 1817 that. savings banks, as we now know them, were started on any uniform basis, or that any legislative measures were taken with regard to them. In that year the English Government passed "an Act to encourage the establishment of banks for savings," taking upon themselves the responsibility of their funds. The Warrington Savings Bank was amongst the first of its kind in the kingdom. Within 30 years of its commencement there were 3,05.5 depositors, whilst according to the last .report issued in November, 1914, there were 19,419 depositors with no less a sum than £541,639 7s. standing to their credit. No better proof of the usefulness of such an institution, and the valued place it has always occupied in the lives of the people, csnild Possibly be given. It is pleasing to know that so many citizens have taken advan- tage of the facilities for saving which the hank offers, and the encouragement to become thrifty, to save some portion of one's earn- ings, is a public Service which none can gain- say. It is necessary to have before us always that "it is important that it should get through into the public mind that every man, however small his income, who saved and cut down unnecessary expenditure, was helping the country at this moment." ,As all have a desire to help, we join in the appeal to those who can save' to do so, for it is not only a private duty but a national command.' Several of the banks in Warrington other than the Savings Bank, have special savinffs branches, and every facility exists for assist- ing Mr. LLOYD GEORGE out of the large wages now being earned and the economies that are being effected to gather in—at a paying rate of interest—his all-conquering silver bullets.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 12, 1915.

GREECE WITH ALLIES.

Sir Arthur Crosfield Obtains A Decided

Opinion.

ROUNIANIA ANb BULGARIA.

Sir Arthur .Crosfield informs us that a week ago, he was talking to. one of the best-informed gentlemen in the Anglo-Greek community in London, who spoke freely and confidently of the. probable.. action Of the Balkan States, and particularly Greece, in, relation to the war. Though a naturalised British subject, he keeps himself well versed in the intricacies of. Balkan politics. He and ' all the members of the Anglo-Greek community are furious at the manner in which Venizilos' policy has been, for the time being, stultified, and the action of Greece on the side of the Allies delayed by the King of Greece.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
IMMINENT WAR ELECTION.

' I take it," said Sir 'Arthur, to the gAptle- man in question, "that there is no doubt that Venezilos will have a thumping good majority at the general elections in Greece on June 13th?"

"I anticipate an absolute landslide and Greece's speedy intervention on the side of the Allies afterwards," was the reply. "With that prospect in Greece well under- stood in _Roumania, and Italy's intervention already a fait accompli,' I take it you don't expect that Roumania will lag behind Greece very long?" "certainly not. I should not be greatly sur- prised if she. anticipates Greece and joins the Allies before her. For the rest, all who know Balkan politicians best seem practically certain now that• Bulgaria will act with Roumania."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSTANTINOPLE.

Ilex* is a good deal of pessimistic talk in London just now about the probability of Con- stantinople holding out for many ,a month to come, but Greece has 300,000 men and Rou- mania 700,000 men of good fighting quality. and, in all probability, there will be the Bul- garians to reckon with as well. Taking these factors into consideration, Sir Arthur asked his Anglo-Greek friend If he thought it probable that Constantinople would be able to hold out for more than a couple of manths. He' replied, emphatically • "No, do not believe that Constantinople will."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL SAVINGS.

A Crisis In Which Thrift Is Our Only Salvation,

===== 0002601_19150612_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
LLOYD GEORGE'S MEANING

"Annual income £2O: annual expenditure £l9 19s. 6d.: result—happiness; annual income £2O: annual expenditure £2O Os. 6d.: result— misery."—Mr. Micawber, in " David Copper. field."

The annual meeting of the Trustee Savings Banks Association was held last week at Perth, commencing on Wednesday evening, when the members were accorded a civic welcome by Lord Provost Scott and the magistrates of the city, and terminating on Saturday with a great public °meeting, at which the' Rt. lion. Lord Strathclyde delivered a most eloquent and in- spiring address. The meeting of the Associa- tion synchronised with the celebration of the centenary of the local savings bank, and a most hearty hospitality was extended to the repre- sentatives. Additional interest was also lent to the proceedings, in view of the grave problem whicli confronts the nation, and the appeal to the people to cut down unnecessary expendi- ture. The Warrington, Runcorn and District Savings Bank was represented by Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes (chairman), Josiah Longland (trustee), and William A. Proud (chief actuary). Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes and Mrs. W. A. Proud were also present. The initial function tsok: place in the Work- ing Boys' and Girls' Hall, Tay-street, on Wed- nesday evening, • Lord Provost Scott, addressing the large and interesting ,gathering, said he, in the name of the magistrates and Town Council, and the whole body of citizens they represented, had the. honour as well as the great pleasure of extending to theni a most hearty welcome to the. Fair City of Perth. They (the guests) had come from many distant towns, and he and his fellow magistrates and councillors fondly hoped that the result of these functions, the result of the speeches made at them, would give an impetus to thrift, meaning a big increase in the num- ber of accounts opened at the Savings Banks.

Confidence took the place of panie; men re- turned to the calm and sober ways of every- day life, and ceased to despair of their country. By the end of September the deposits had exceeded the withdrawals, And for the quarter ending the 31st of March last the deposits had exceeded the withdrawals by £4,400,000. That was no 'less than 1:3,000,000 in excess of any similar record for any corresponding period.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MESSAGE OF THE BANKS.

• Sir Charles Addis. London, said that when many public functions had been abandoned or postponed, as being out of keeping with the time when men's minds were occupied with thoughts of war, they were there to show that the Sav- ings Banks of this country had a message to proclaim and a service to perform so intimately connected with the conduct of the war that their trustees would have failed in their duty, if for some mere conventional consideration they would have neglected to make these objects known. There were many good and sound reasons why they should practise economy at all times, and there were some specially cogent reasons why they should do so in time of war. . . . One of the few cheering phases of the war was, stated Mr. William Young, 31. P.; that it had resulted in a very considerable increase in the savings of the people. In Perth alone, he observed that the increase of deposits last year in the bank amounted to about £40,000, and that had been going on all over the country. It was from these savings that the immediate war cost had to be met, so that thrift had been raised by the war from the category of a per- sonal virtue to that of a public duty. When the war was over there might be some renewal of prosperity due to the ravages of the war, but then there was almost bound- to folio* a very considerable collapse, and if the nation was wise it would look ahead, take advantage of that opportunity, and economise for the evil day when it came, so that they at any rate might be able to face it with a minimum of the distress and misery which had invariably followed upon a great war.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
PATRIOTIC OBLIGATION TO SAVE.

But, apart altOgether from the patriotic obligation to save, there was the personal advan- tage to the thrifty working man of the exis- tence of a nest-egg," as it was called, for hard times. The War loans which had been and would be raised offered a sound investment for the working man, so that in helping his country he was 'laying up a competence which would stand him in good stead in the days of depression: At the conclusion of the celebrations a public meeting was held,. when Lord Strathclyde gave a most illuminating address, which we regret lack of space prevents us from giving more fully. The remarks of his Lordship will set" at ease those whose :minds were somewhat per- turbed by misunderstanding the recent utter- ances of the ChanCellor of the Exchequer respecting the savings of the people. Lord, Strathclyde said that a clarion call had been sounded, and a message had gone forth to the , whole nation urging economy, thrift, saving, and husbanding their means as nothing short of a patriotic duty. 'Thrift was their only salvation. It was by thrift alone, practised not merely by this or by that class of the community but by the whole nation, that they should be enabled to surmount and to triumph over the seemingly overwhelming financial difficulties by which they were now confronted. Their needs were colossal. For they were in the midst of a war which, whether they had had regard to the numbers of men engaged, to the extent of the theatre of operations, or to the expenditure of material resources far transcending any past war in the history of the world.

THE CHANCELLOR'S CLARION CALL. And so the Chancellor of the Exchequer told them, with all the responsibility that pertained to his high office, that "from the point of view of the successful conduct of the war, if we are to take our part, not merely in financing our own share, but in helping our Allies to finance theirs, that the national savings should be increased." And a week later, on the 12th of May, the Chancellor told the House of Commons and the country that "the only sayings which will help us substantially are the savings of the people themselves, the sav- ings of ' individuals, of families, and of the man who is getting an, income in any shape or form—these are the savings which will be helpful in prosecuting a great war. . . . The savings of the people at the present moment were vital to the success of this country, and every man who cuts down unnecessary expen- diture in his own sphere is contributing some- thing material and important towards the success of this country at the present moment. . . . . It was important that it should get through into the public mind that every man, however small his income, Who saved and cut down unnecessary expenditure was helping the country att this moment." In sbine quarters, he understood, that appeal to the people to save up by curtailing all needless expenditure was regarded as a threat to raid the Savings Banks, to appropriate the people's savings, to put them all into a War loan, and to pay the owners of the savings not a penny of interest.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL CLASSES TO HELP.

Well, the Chancellor of the Exchequer had disclaimed any such intention. He was not thinking of Bank Savings at all, but of the savings of all classes of the community, high and low. He wanted the amount of money available for investment to swell up to such colossal dimensions that it would be really helpful to the country. If the Chancellor's earnest call was responded to by the people, then, instead of a rush to withdraw money from the Savings Banks, there would be a rush to lodge money in the Savings Banks. Indeed, that seemed_ to have been the result of the war, not quite, but almost from the very first. It was true that there was a sharp run on the Post Office Savings Banks just at the outbreak of hostilities. That was quite natural and exactly what one would have expected. By the end of August the net withdrawals were no less than £.5,000,000 in excess of the deposits. And then there came a remarkable change.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
ABANDON LUXURIES AND SELF- INDULGENCE.

Nothing could more clearly demonstrate the sound condition of the finance, trade, and industry of the country. It rerealed, too, the cheering fact that among the shopkeepers, smaller trader's, and artisans there was not only the desire, but also the ability to make substantial savings. . . . The least thing which a patriot could do who was disabled from entering the- King's service and from lending a direct hand in the manufacture of the munitions of war was to practise thrift and so .aid in husbanding the national resources. They had already proved on many a stricken field that the British soldier was still all-round the finest fighting man in Europe. Let them now demonstrate to the world—not boastfully, but resolutely—that they as a nation, having foresworn luxury and self- indulgence, would bring all their vast material to bear on the task to which they had set their hand—the achievement of a complete and over- whelming victory for right and justice. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19150612_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
SAVE WORK.

Quite As Necessary As Stu kill .114iiie.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Last week your leader dealt with the necessity of conserving the wealth of the country by banking all surpluses, and only buying necessary things. But saving money is only one way in which all can help. Saving work is as necessary as saving moneY ; in, fact, they ultimately mean the same thing. In connection with recruiting, the appeal by the Distributing Trades :Committee issued through the Home -Office to shoppers shows that our statesmen realise the importance of this form of service, and it would be well if every unit in the kingdom did the same. The necessity for saving work in shopping is im- portant in itself and a convenient forni of illustration. But it is only one of many ways in which all can help. In the official appeal named attention is called to many points which, although small, are worth repeating.. We were taught in our early years in the well-known rhyme that " Little drops of water, little grains of sand, make the mighty ocean, and • the beauteous land." If we but think all big things are made up of small units, and this is specially true of an organised society. The efforts made of late to secure more recruits from the distributing trades will be successful (if busi- ness is to be carried on at all) only if the public co-operate so is to reduce the work of the salesman and distributor, and so set would-hp recruits free.. Take, for example, the question of delivery of parcels. Competi- tion has driven shopkeepers into a system of delivering purchases, however small. So much has this custoin grown that it has created a foolish impression that it is had form to carry a parcel, A 'talesman wraps up a purchase, maybe only half a pound in weight, feels he must offer to deliver it because his competitors are willing to do the same, and, he fears to lose custom. And so we have a great amount of unnecessary em- ployment delivering parcels which the buyer could very well carry herself. We cannot afford this pampering. laziness, and waste of effort in war time; There are conspicuous notices in some of the city Co- operative Stores as follows : "Members are requested as far as pos- sible to take small parcels and packages with them after purchase."

Then there is the want of thought of those who leave their shopping till the last moment, and Who perhaps telephone to the salesman they must have something sent up immedi- ately or the purchaser cannot have her lunch, and so the salesman has to send out a special messenger, possibly one who an hour ago has been delivering goods in the same district. Another point detailed in the appeal is the desirability of giving a standing 'order for regular supplies, so that the salesman can deal with it when the work stress is not great. But I need not labour these points, important as they are in their aggregate, effect. Every buyer should think them out for herself, and accept as part of her war ser- vice saving labour for 'the sliopman. The public must learn to be thoughtful, method- ical, and patient. The exacting, capricious, unreasonable shoppers must be induced to change their ways and put their mind, their heart .and their patriotism into their shop- ping.

- English women have during these trying months shown splendid devotion and Sacrifice, many of them have proudly paid the highest price, and are now sad. mourners, but have solaced their grief by engagilig in service and in further sacrifice. ,Perhaps all have not been wise' as for example, those misguided females who have gone about challenging young men, of whose circumstances they know nothing, with reproaches and white feathers. We wionder whether such may not have forgotten the kinds of service already indicated. They and all might find in these, opportunities of helping, far more useful, far more honourable than the methods indicated. Be this as it may, it is well for all carefully to examine in the light of the war the details of daily life, and seek and assuredly find in these one of the best and.truest forms of service. Co-operation in work, to save work, should be the object of all. This co-operation has already been largely employed in manu- facturing, especially in the making of the things required for the Army, both masters and workmen have co-operated. But the same spirit should find expression in all work, and in all the ordinary affairs of life. 'lt will all be required before this war is finished. All should realise that in so far As we save work we thereby save wealth, we contribute to- wards the end the nation has in view, namely, that of defeating Germany and restoring order, . freedom and . peace to distracted Europe.—Yours, &c., CHAS. E. PARKER. June 1, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
RECRUITING WEEK-END.

Bands And Speakers Appeal To The Patriotism Of The Young Men.

MANY OPEN-AIR MEETINGS.

This week-end the call to arms will be sounded loudly in Warrington and district, and it is earnestly hoped that many of the eligible. young men, who have not already done so, will hear it, and, -hearing, respond, for recruits are most urgently needed if the war is to be brought to a speedy and successful conclusion, The meetings in Warrington, the first of which were held last night, form part of a vigorous recruiting campaign which is being prosecuted throughout South Lancashire by means of stir- ring martial music played by military bands and rousing speeches at open-air meetings. In the Warrington district the appeal is more specially being directed to obtaining four hun- dred more men as reserves to fill up gaps as they occur in the ranks of the 4th South Lan- cashires—the Warrington Territorials, who have been fighting so gallantly at the Front for over three months now, and of whom, of necessity, toll has ,been taken in the shape of killed and wounded. Whilst the primary idea of the campaign is to obtain recruits for the Terri- torial Battalions of the South Lancabliires, recruits will also be sought :Gl' tie Liverpool Scottish, whilst the Regular Army a•rliorities will also co-operate in the demonstrations with a view to enlisting more men for the ordinary line regiments. The central Territorial Recruiting Station in Warrington is, of course, the Drill Hall, in Bath-street, which is in .charge of Lieutenant Williamson., Recruits for the Regular Army are received, examined, passed and sworn-in at the Central Police Station.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE BANDS BUSY.

The bands, which are to form a feature of this special recruiting campaign, are Lord Derby's Band, a band of Scotch Pipers, and the band of the 4th South Lancs. Loyd Derby's musicians and the pipers left Southport—where they had been engaged in similar recruiting work—yesterday afternoon, and reached War- rington shortly before 4 o'clock, being met at the Central Station by the band of .the 4th South Lancs. All three bands then formed up, and to the music of the 4th South Lancs., marched to their respective billets. At 6 o'clock in the evening, the bandsmen and pipers and the full strength of the 3rd/ 4th South Lancs., paraded outside the Town Hall, and then diviried into three parties. • OPEN-AIR MEETINGS AND A CINEMA DISPLAY.

The first party—headed by the Scotch Pipers, under Captain McFarlane—marched to the Central Station and there embarked on tram- cars for Manchester-road. Alighting, they 'pro- ceeded .to the Oakwood-avenue Recreation Ground, where. at 7 o'clock a well-attended meeting was held, the special speakers being Messrs. Tom Smith and Bratherton. This meet- ing over, the band and a • detachment of the khaki-coated " Terriers " marched to the Orford-lane Recreation Ground—via Oakwood- avenue, Gorsey-lane, Marsh House-lane, Long- ford-street, Winwick-road and Bluecoat-street— where a second meeting was held. The second party, headed by the band of the 4th South Lancs., and under Captain G. H. Roberts, proceeded from the Drill Hall to vacant ground in Evelyn-sireet, making their way via the Park, Sankey-street and Liverpool- road. After the meeting, which was addressed by Messrs. A. Wynne-Jones and H. Ternent, the " Terriers " and their musical escort marched to the Lovely-lane Recreation Ground—via Liverpool-road, Powys-street and Green-street— where another meeting was held.

The third party, with Lord, Derby's Band at its head, marched to Victoria Park, under the command. of Captain Townroe. In the Park a particularly big and enthusiastic meeting was held, the principal speaker being Mr. James Parker, the Labour M.P. for Halifax. Prior to the meeting the band and the detachment of " Terriers " in attendance marched through Winwick-street, Horsemarket-street, Bridge- street and Knutsford-road; after the meeting they threaded their way------via Howley Foot- bridge, Egerton-street, Howley-lane, Church- street, Buttermarket-street and Bridge-street-- to Bridge Foot, where a. second successful meet- ing was held. This was followed at 9 o'clock by a display of living pictures by Mr. Arkwright (an ex-Unionist, M.P.) from his travelling cine- matograph van. Between the pictures. which raised the large crowd to a high pitch of enthusiasm, came brief speeches -by Mr. H. X. Harman and ex-Corporal Weir.. After the open-air meetings, visits were paid to. and recruiting speeches made in. the different places of amusement in the town.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S PROGRAMME.

A Big Military Tattoo in the Evening. To-day the band a the 4th South Lancs. parades at . the Drill Hall at 11.15 a.m., and proceeds at 11.30, accompanied by recruiters, to Bridge Foot. A meeting will be held there between 12 and 1 p.m., with Mr. J. Parker, M.P., and Mr. Tom Smith as speakers. Captain Gordon Roberts is expected to take the chair. Lord Derby's Band will parade at the Drill. Hall at 2.45 p.m., if fine, and will play selec- 'tions in the Park at 3 p.m. Major Monks will take the chair at 3.30, with Messrs. Wynne- Jones and Ternent as speakers. If wet, the band will play in the Drill Hall. The Pipers, if possible, will visit Stockton Heath during the afternoon. , At 6.15 the three bands will parade at the Drill Hall and make short route- marches—the Pipers down Bewsey-road; Lord Derby's Band down Liverpool-road, and the South Lancs. down Sankey-street, all three returning to the front of the Town Hall by 6.40. The band of the South Lancs. Regiment, from the Bararcks, will probably march down via Orford-lane at 6.15. At 7 p.m. the Mayor of Warrington will take the chair in front of the Town Hall. Mr. W. T. Tyson Wilson, Labour M.P. for West Houghton, will speak, and probably brief speeches will also be made by Major Monks, of the 3rd/4th South Lancs., Captain Gordon Roberts and Captain Townroe. At 7.30 Lord Derby's Band, assisted by the other three bands, will give a Military Tattoo. . At 8.30, by the Town Hall. the cinematograph will give a dis- play, the film to be shown under the charge of Private Bird and Mr. Jamieson. Messrs. Tom Smith, J. Parker, M.P., H. Ternent, A. Wynne- Jones and Bratherton have placed themselves at the disposal of Lieutenant Williamson, so that every picture Palace in the town can be visited during the evening. Each speaker will be ac- companied by an officer of the South Lancs. Regiment. Lord Derby's Band and Pipers return to Liverpool for the week-end.

MONDAY'S ARRANGEMENTS.

On Monday the band of the 2/4th South Laneashires will play at Bridge Foot at mid- day and speeches will be given by Messrs. Tom Smith and Ternent.

In the afternoon the Pipers will proceed to Sankey Bridges (Meeting at 2.45) ; Penketh (3.5); Farnworth (3.30) ; Crompton (4); return- ing via Tibbs Cross (4.20), and Great Sankey (4.45). Lord Derby's band, under Captain Townroe, will proceed to Martinscroft Green (Meeting at 2.50); Hollins Green (3.10); Glazebrook (3.30); Culcheth ((3.50); and Croft (4.20). Both bands and all speakers will parade at the Drill Hall, Warrington, at. 6.30. Lord Derby's band ivill proceed to Penketh Recreation Ground and hold a meeting at 7.15. They will leave for Great Sankey at 7.45, and then hold a meeting at 8 p.m. Returning to Warrington at 8.30, they will hold a meeting at Sankey Bridges at 8.40. The Pipers will proceed to Lower Walton for a meeting at 7 p.m. They leave for Stockton Heat at 7.15.

The band of the 4th South Lancashires will play at Stockton Heath from 7 p.m. for the purpose of assembling a crowd for the Pipers. The Pipers will leave for Grappenhall at 7.45, holding a meeting at 8 o'clock. At 8.15 they leave for Thelwall, holding a meeting at 8.30, and returning to Warrington via Latchford.

WORKS' MEETINGS ON TUESDAY. On Tuesday Lord Derby's hand will visit Winwick (6 p.m.), Burtonwood (6.30), Collins Green (7.15) and Haydock. The Pipers will go to Newton-le-Willows and _other places, and on Wednesday the scene of activities will be transferred to the St. Helens district. Mid-day meetings will be held outside the larger Warrington works on Tuesday.

SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

Congratulations From All Classes And All Parts.

Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield have been much gratified by the very many congratula- tory messages they have received at their London home, 41, West Hill, Highgate, N., from friends in Warrington and elsewhere, of all parties and classks, upon the bestowal of a baronetcy upon Mr. Crosfield last week. The messages were still flowing in yesterday and we postpone, the publication of the list we had prepared ftk to-day until next week in order that it may be as complete as possible.

A Wide-spread Family.

Mr. Arnold Nield, son of the late Councillor, Nield, sailed for Australia on the 29th ult., and his brother, Mr. Norman Nield, has heard of the safe arrival of his vessel, the Malwa, at successive points, such as Gibraltar and Malta. Mr, Nield expected to meet another brother, Mr. Morris Nield, at Malta. The latter, who has the temporary rank of Paymasyr on H.M.S. Sapphire, was slightly wounded in the forehead by shrapnel during the early part of the operations in the Dardanelles. and is con- valescing at Malta. Whether the brothers met is not yet known. Dr. Harry Nield, who was serving at the Front, is in Scotland recovering from enteric fever contracted, in France. Mr. Arnold Nield, who 'like his brother Norman, was engaged at Messrs. Ryland' Bros., has obtained an appointment at Sydney with a big firm of biscuit manufacturers.. For the present his wife (the ex-Mayoress of War- rington) and her two children remain at home.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Flag Day.

Next Saturday will be observed as Flag -Day in Warrington. That is, a large number of young ladies will be on duty in all parts of the town, whose object will be to raise money in aid of the hospital funds for our wounded soldiers and those of our Allies—just as the same willing workers raised money- by selling flowers on Alexandra Day. About 0,000 flags will be placed under the notice of the general public, who will have the privilege of. paying as much each for them as they please—but not less than one penny. The Mayor is presi- dent of the fund, which commands our very best support. A meeting of the workers and all willing to assist will be held at the Parr Hall at eight o'clock next Tuesday evening, the ,General Committee having met half-an- hour earlier.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Engineering Models.

Recently the newly-formed local Association of Engineers and General Craftsmen held a most interesting and successful exhibition of engineering models in the Junior Conservative Hall, Market-place. This was the first exhibi- tion the association had attempted and it is pleasing to record that it was such a success, from all standpoints, that it will undoubtedly encourage the society to persevere with the in- teresting and instructive work which it has set out to accomplish. As a result of the exhibition many new members have been enrolled, a fact which, of course, has given it a firmer founda- tion for its efforts. The exhibits were of a varied character and several of them displayed considerable engineering ingenuity, whilst all were interesting. The committee responsible for the organisation of the exhibition consisted of Messrs. L. Dale (chairman), H. B. Bell (sec- retary), H. T. Brown, A. Bridge and H. Lee.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Lewis Carrol's Birthplace.

The spring ramble and conference of the Sunday School Union took place on Saturday week. The afternoon was spent in a visit to Daresbury Church, and Mr. A. Mounfield gave a lecture on "Daresbury and its Associations," making interesting reference to the origin of the church and to some well-known people who had been connected with it. Many of those present' were surprised to learn that Charles L. Dodgson. better known as Lewis Carrol. and the author of "Alice in Wonderland," was born at the old parsonage, and spent the first eleven years of his life at Daresbury. Tea was served in the Wesleyan Methodist Schoolroom at Moore, and an evening meeting and conference was presided over by Mr. Appleton, headmaster of Evelyn-street Schools. Mr. Clare,' of Liverpool, gave a very fine address on the psychology of childhood and youth, which was much appreciated, and was followed by a useful conference. A first instalment. of Mr. Mounfield's lecture appears in this issue of "The Examiner."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Freemasons' War Honours.

The Hon. Arthur Stanley, M.P.' home tem- porarily from Red Cross work in France, pre- siding as Provincial Grand Master over the annual meeting of the Provincial Grand Lodge of West Lancashire Freemasons, held at South- port on Monday, pointed with pride to the fact that despite Masonic generosity towards the Forces, the four provincial institutions had each enjoyed a record year, though no special appeal was made. He congratulated the lodge on the report and emphasised the value of all forms of national service at this period of stress. He had attended a meeting of the Liverpool Cathedral Committee, and learned that the estimate fortthe four two-light windows to be erected in the Eathom Memorial Chapter- house, for which the province had subscribed, would cost about. £1,700. or 5..500 in excess of the estimate. A complete scheme. would, be put before the next Provincial Grand Lodge; meanwhile he would suggest that a pair of the windows should be dedicated to the memories of his two predecessors in the chair, the first and the second Earls of Latham, the remaining pair of windows being reserved as memorials of Masons of the province giving their lives-for their country in the war. One of the greatest encouragements which Red Cross and kindred workers had received came from those who, like the Masonic brethren, had shown their appreciation by generous financial aid. It would be the task of Freemasonry to aid in leading the nations of the earth back to the paths of peace and brotherhood. (Applause.) The Provincial Grand Secretary reported that the roll of lodges in the province had been increased by two. the Argosy (Liverpool) and the Paton (Southport), the total number of lodges being now 170, while the Masons reached 13,500. Scarcely a lodge was without repre- sentation at the Front. and they already had to mourn the loss of several brethren who had fallen in battle.

The Prov. G.M., who was assisted by the Deputy Prow. G.M. (Bro. Louis S. Winsloe), the veteran Bro. W. Goodacre, and other bre- thren of Grand rank, invested the new Provin- cial Grand officers.

Those upon whom Past rank was conferred included Bro. D. Tinnion (Warrington). P.P.G.S.D.; and Bro. T. Bowes (Warrington), P.P.G.'Asst. D.C.

A Successful Bazar. The total proceeds of the annual sale of work, in connection with the Golborne-street Baptist Church—held last week—were £127.

PRIVATE WM. NEWNS.

Second Of Three " Terrier " Brothers To GiVe His Life For His Country.

The second of three gallant brothers who at the outbreak of war offered their lives, if need be, in the cause of their country has been killed, in the person of Private William Newns, Whose home is at 16, Forshaw-street, Warring- ton. His brother, Private. John Newns, was .killed six weeks ago, and his second brother, Joseph, is still serving. News of Private Wm. Newn's death was first Conveyed to his father—he has no mother—in a letter written by Company Sergeant-Major Thomas S. Davies (B Company), in the follow- ing terms:—

A -Fatal Wound in the Head.

It is with deep sorrow that I have to in- form you that your son, Private W. Newns, has received a fatal wound in the head. He had not been away from me many minutes when someone brought word to me that he was bit. Death must have been almost in- stant. Words of mine cannot lighten this. second great sorrow which has fallen upon your home.. Your son acted as my "bat- man," and I always found him willing and trustworthy, and L feel that I have lost a personal friend. I may say that the acci- dent had only just happened, and it is the first casualty we have had since coming her,-? on §aturday night. Please 'accept my deepest sympathy and, believe me, I mourn, along with you, the loss of a loved one.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
Brother's Pathetic Letter.

Dated the same day, came a letter from the sole survivor of the three brothers, Private

===== 0002601_19150612_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
"ThsePh Newns, of No. 1 Platoon, A Company, 4th (Territorial) South Lane.

_ Just. a few lines," it ran, "to let you Icnow that our Willie has met with an accident. I cannot tell you yet whether or Ilot it is fatal, but he has been hit in the back of the head. The officer told me that he would let me know." Here the letter breaks off, and is 'resumed, appatently some time later :--" I liave just got to khow that he has gone to the One above, in Whose hands it is best to place ourselves. Dad, keep a good heart and rely on Him, as it is His will and not our will that must pre- vail."

A Painless Death.

Wound Apparently Not Serious.

"I was quite near him at the time, and assisted to carry him to the dressing station, wherp his wound wag found, and I am glad to sat. that it did not seem of a serious nature. I trust he will soon be well. It will be com- forting to you to know that he is receiving. good treatment," " As Well As can Be Expected."

Later, Mrs. Pemberton received a letter from her husband, who is in hospital at Chichester, stating that he is progressing as well as can be expected.,

_ Private Pemberton, who is now in his 28th year, as a boy attended the Wycliffe day school. He was formerly. employed as a boilermaker by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., 'Ltd. He served in the Territorials some time ago, and when war broke out he rejoined. He is a son of Mr. Joseph Pemberton; of 131, Dal- lam-lane. A brother—Gunner Joseph Pember- ton—is serving at the' Front with the Royal Garrison Artillery.

the upper left leg, received whilst taking rations to, the trenches. On Tuesday week a letter was received by his father, in which he said that he was making progress and was able to walk.

A Native of Grappenhall.

RIFLEMAN E. CLOUGH. Wounded.

says:—" I lost all my kit when I got wounded, for I had to walk two miles before I could get to a dressing station. I have written 'walked,' but that hardly accurately describes it, for part of the way I had to be dragged, and part of the way I crawled. It was awful! It was a good job I managed to get away somehowe or the Germans would have had me. Had they got me I don't suppose you would see me again, for 'they always shoot you now'! I only wish I could have got hold of one of the Prussians just then, for they are a dirty lot of pigs. I should have enjoyed cutting lumps off him! I reckon I managed to account for two before I got hit. . . . . The scenes at the place where I was hit were hellish. . I feel a bit better!"

===== 0002601_19150612_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
COURAGE, DEVOTION, ENDUR ANCE

Lance-Corporal E. Spruce AVill Be Missed By Battalion.

Lance-Corporal Edward Spruce, 499, Well- field-street#, who was in the list of wounded Territorials, published in "The Examiner" last week, is spoken very highly of by his com- rades.

'Sergeant 11. Goodwin, who is at present in Summerdown Convalescent Camp, has written to Mrs. Spruce: "I have just received a paper from home in which I find that your son, Ted, is reported wounded. I should like to express to you my sincere regret and tender to you my sympathy in your trouble. . -. . Ted was in my platoon while I was out there, and, if you remember, he went back to Tunbridge with me when we were returning from our furlough. . . . . I have known him a long time, and since I joined the battalion T have come in contact with him a great deal.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
Always Cheery and Reliable.

"I can assure you that he has always played the game, and was one of my best N.C.O.'s He always carried, out his duties in a cheery and light-hearted manner, and was always reliable. I will never forget the manner in which he carried out a very painful duty the !first night we were under fire. I had to leave him behind with Private Briscoe, who got shot. He was under heavy fire at the time and there being no stretcher- bearers about, he bandaged up poor Briscoe, and carried him on his back for a mile and a half to the dressing station. This proves his courage, devotion and endurance. lam sure such a character- will be missed in the battalion. I was quite shocked and upset when I read the news in the paper. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
"FEARLESSLY AND WELL."

Dr. Law's Praise Of Private Edwards.

Private George Edwards, news of whose having been wounded was, reported in last Saturday's "Examiner," was engaged at Messrs. J. crosfield and Sons. • In a letter, dated May 12th, lir. C. D. Law wrote' to his mother, who lives in Priestley- street :—" I am sorry to inform you that Private G. Edwards was wounded this evening, but not seriously. Whilst accompanying a carrying party—as a stretcher-bearer—he was strucleby a bullet, in the right foot. I regret exceedingly that he has had the ill-fortune to be wounded, and we all hope that it will not be long before he is able to be about again. It is most unfortunate that so soon after becoming a stretcher-bearer he should have been wounded. He performed his risky duty • fearlessly and well, and he will be much missed both by his comrades and me. He was sent down this eveining to the Field Ambu- lance Hospital (No. 8), and I expect that he will be transferred to the base. I feel sure that you have no cause to be 'anxious about him, although, naturally, you must be distressed to learn that he has been wounded."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE LITHERLAND'S WOUND.

A NarroA\ Escape, Then llit In The Leg

Private John Litherland, 9, Winwick-road, who was in our casualty list last week, writing home described how he received his in- jury, and giving numerous other de- tails of the recent fighting in which the Warrington " Terriers " have par- ticipated. "I started put along with the lines- man through the communication trench up to a reserve trench in a woad," he writes. "From the reserve trench we started to lay a line across the wood to a fire trench. We ran out a reel of wire and found we had not got enough, so we were making our way to the fire trench for more cable . when over came one of the Germans' highly-explosive shells. We dropped fiat and this time we had a lucky escape, for a .piece of the shell dropped be- tween ray head and my mate's foot. After that we thought it best to hurry out of the wood, and we were just getting in a communidation •trench, leading to the fire. trench, when over came another shell. No sooner did I hear the report than I felt as if some One had hit me on the calf of my right leg with a big stick.

" Half a Mo,' Jim."

"I looked down and saw a hole in the big boots I was wearing, so I said to a mate, who was only about a couple of feet in front of me, Half a " mo," Jim, I'm hit.' I told him to take the boot off. He did so straightaway, and then we saw that the bottom of my trouser's leg was soaked with blood. I lifted the trousers and then saw a gaping wound. . . . My mate did the best thing possible . . A he ran for assistance, for neither of us had field dress- ings with us.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
Interesting Musketry Competition,

At the Legh-street range on Wednesday even- ing, an interesting musketry competition con- chided, with the following result:— Best File (Classes 1 and 2, paired by ballot): F. Allman and A. Bailie; Class 1: T. Lowe; Class 2: G. Reeves.

Best -File (Class 3): C. H. Page and G. Pryor; Class 3: J. Norman. Best File (Class 4) : F. S. Rossiter and W. Freeman; Class 4: F. W. Swallow. Good scores were also made in Class 1 by : D. Plinston, J. Hinde, E. Cleeton, F. L. Gar- side, F. V. Mathias, H. Gaunt and W. Monks, and in Class 2 by T. Gorton, A. W. Nicholls, L. Cross and J. Langhorn. The donors of prizes were. Messrs. F. W. Wright, W. Litton, F. Fox, C. H. Hoult and P. Shakeshaft.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
IS IT NOTHING TO THEE?

A rabbit lay flying where bluebells were spread. And apple blooms hung on the boughs overhead; Its poor eyes were glazing, wlth labouring • breath Its pulses kept tira6 to the slow leet of Death; I gazed at the blossoms on every tree, _ . _

But each fluttering heart-beat was torture to me.

A child op his couch tossed in pain to and fro While the pear trees' white petals were falling like snow; His mother sat watching each movement—in

vain She looks at the landscape; the thought of his

• • pain Has palsied all pleasure—her firstborn is he; Theisight of their sorrow was anguish to me.

An old man was nearing 'the end of the road, And Death came, at last, and unloosened his load; His widow was lonely, his children bereft, And dark was the home his bright spirit had

left; Though long was the journey, and Death set him free, The pain of their parting was heart-break to me.

Out ,yonder dim millions are dying or dead; The grass is all gory, the daisies are red; The poor traropled faces look up at the sky. The wail of the wounded goes endlissly by, 'The dead rise in mountains for all men to see: 0, Father in Heaven, is it nothing to Thee? AiITIII7R. BENNETT.

Colonel Moggridge, of Orford Barracks, and Colonel ,Ireland Blackburne, of Hale Hall, up to-recently dispatched many parcels of food and clothing, but the list is growing, and funds are still urgently needed to extend the work, which has been taken over by the Mayor of Warring- ton, at the request of the gentlemen named, and is being carried out under the direction of the Town Clerk, at the Parr Hall.

Parcels of clothing and cast-off boots (which had been repaired) were sent to all men of the South Lancashire Regiment who/were known to be in need, but this call has, now ceased, as prisoners of war can obtain clothing on appli- cation to the commandant of the camp where they are interned.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE .I. \\. IN.

his Conduct After Being Wounded Was "Splendid."

Private James William Blain, of 8, Wind- sor-street, has been wounded. News to the effect that he had been hit reached his parents —Mr. and Mrs. David Blain—in a letter writ- ten by Second-Lieutenant J. H. Mandleburg (0.C.T.)., which ran as follows:— With the Transports When Hit.

"I regret to have to inform you that your son, Private J. Blain, has been wounded by the explosion of a shell, while doing his duty with the transport. He has been in- jured in one eye. He received medical at- tention immediately, and has "13en sent to hospital. His conduct after being wounded, as always, was splendid. I wish to convey my best wishes for his quick recovery, to- gether with my sympathy."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
How It Happened.

Another and later letter received from. Company-Sergeant-Major Davies, ran as fol- lows :

"I am writing to inform you that your son has been wounded. He was driving a pair of mules through the village towards the firing-line, when a shell burst, and a piece struck him off, or near, the right eye. The extent of the damage is not yet known, _ _

but he has gone into hospital, and probably he will be able to write you, and let you know the result of the medical 'examina- tion. • I saw him this morning, when he was quite cheerful. . . . I am sorry to be tit° sender of had news to you, but hope the wound will not be serious, and that your son will soon be restored to his usual health and strength."

Eight Years A "Terrier "—And More! _Private Blain is a single man of 28 years of age and makes his home with his parents. He was born and brought up, however, at Norley, near Frodsham, and it was while at that place that he served four- years with the 2nd Che- shire Regiment. Upon his parents coming to reside at Warrington, he joined the local Territorials, in which Force he has also served four years; in fact, his period of ser- vice was completed when war broke out. He is a member of "F " Company. He went to the Front in. August. He was formerly em- ployed by the London and North-Western Railway Company, in one of their goods departments. He is a member of the Lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity) which meets at the Mul- berry Tree Hotel, Stockton Heath. He was formerly .41 member of a church choir at

Norley

A -brother—Gunner . Arthur Blain—is in training with the R.F.A.

PRISONERS OF WAR.

How Their Scanty Fare Can

===== 0002601_19150612_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Be Supplemented.

A REALLY DESERVING OBJECT.

Letters frequently reach Warrington from prisoners in Germany saying how their lot could be 4nade easier by gifts, especially of food, one of the most recent haying come from Sergeant Hector Nash, who wi,as wounded early in the war and taken prisoner. Sergeant Nash, who is 23 years of age, and has been in the 2nd South Lancashires for nine years, comes of a soldier family, the members of which live' in Chorley-street, Warrington.

OFFICIAL ACTION.

Warrington is trying to meet this call for help by assisting the men of the South Lanca- shire Regiment and men of -other regiments who reside in the borough. 1

===== 0002601_19150612_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO HELP.

Gifts of food and tobacco may be handed in at the Parr Hall, and subscriptions paid to the Mayor or Borough Treasurer. A list of the, gifts already received will be published in next week's paper. -The cry is urgent: the need is great. One guinea will answer one soldier's appeal for over six weeks.

The articles most appreciated are: Tea. cocoa, milk (in tins). sugar, Lyle's Syrup. Oxo (tab- lets), soup tablets, bread (well-baked), biscuits, cheese, oats (rolled), tobacco, cigarettes.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
WHO WILL VOLUNTEER?

The' Town Clerk will be glad to receive the name of any citizen who will undertake to act as' a standby for one or more prisoners, and by sending a regular supply of necessaries, relieve to tat extent the Central Office, which will be sorely pressed' as time goes on.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
A JUVENILE PATRIOT.

The Use She Made Of Her Pocket- Money.

Little Miss May ffannan, who lives with her parents in' Parker-street, shows the right spirit. By saving her pocket-money she is able to buy tins of Oxo cubes; which are so appreciated in the trenches, and despatch them to "'Pommies" at the Front. Recently this young lady forwarded a supply of the cubes to Private N. Millington, of the Warrington Terri- torials, who writes back thanking her for her generosity. 'We are making the Germans repent," he says, "for their dastardly crimes, and hope to prove a credit to the 'good old town of Warrington.' We are having glorious weather at present, and hope to be shortly g,oing along Parker-street to the Town 'Hall again. . . . . . With, the best of luck to

you in your endeavours to brighten the lot of at least one British 'Tommy' barring the way to Calais."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ARTHUR SIN IN DELLS.

" Going On Fine" In A London Hospital.

A letter from Private Arthur Swindells, of the 2nd Battalion South Lancashire Regi- Metnt; to his mother, who lives in Ripley- street, contained the first intimation that he had been wounded, and he now lies in the King's Cottage Hospital, Denmark Hill, Lon- don, S.E., from whence he writes to say that he "is going on fine." He adds thai he was shot in the right hip whilst taking barbed- wire to the trenches on the 18th of last month, and says that his wound is now healed and that he can walk.

Private Swindells is only 18 years old, and, prior to 'listing, he was an iron-moulder's apprentice at Messrs. Smethurst's works in Fottndry-street. He was a regular 'attendant at the Working Men's MisSion at Bank Quay, and he "joined up" in August last and went to the Front in November.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN HEWITT.

Shot Through Left Arm And Gassed

Private John Hewitt, of the Royal Lancashire Fusiliers, who has many friends in Warrington, has been wounded in the left arm, and is in hos- pital recovering from his injuries and also from the effects of gas inhaled after the bursting of One of the German gas shells. • Prior to "joining up" in November last, Private Hewitt was employed by Messrs. Pendle- bury, furniture dealers, ST., Winwick-street. Born in Warrington he, as a boy, removed with his family to Yorkshire, and there spent most of his life, returning to Warrington about twelve months ago to take up the position which he left when he enlisted. Whilst working in Warring- ton he made his home with lils aunt, Airs. Sharples, at n, Laira-street, Orford-lane. He is 19 years of age. At present Private Hewitt is lying in the East- ern General Hospital at Cambridge. The first intimation that he had been hit was contained 1 in a field card and was received by his aunt on May 26th. On May 31st he wrote to the same lady

"Just a line to say that I am getting on all right at present. I have got up out of bed now, but I have to go under an operation, as a result of my wound. I am wounded in the left arm and I also got a dose of the poisonous gas. I was hit at . Going through Bel- gium was like going through hell! I hope soon to he home on sick furlough."

WAR ITEMS.

BRIGADrER NOEL-LEE.

Brigadier-General Noel-Lee, who is in the list of the wounded in Gallipoli, is a cousin of Mrs. Ziba Armitage, of Newton Bank, Preston Brook. :1e is second son of Sir Joseph Lee, and a partner of Tootal, Broadhurst and Lee, the celebrated shirt makers.

FIRST ST. PETER'S BIBLE CLASS BOY TO FALL.

Private David Kirkham, of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancs. Regiment, who—as was reported in "The Examiner "—was killed in ;action on• May 11th, was the first member of the Bible Class held in connection with St. Peter's Church to fall on the field of honour.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
" TOMMY " PROUD OF ARPLEY STREET GIRLS.

Letter From The Trenches.

Mary Spann, 24, Cairo-street, the little daughter of Mr. A. E. Spann, secretary of the Headquarters Division of the Warrington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, was one of the children attending Arpley-street School who recently sent out parcels of com- forts to the men at the Pront.

The soldier who got Miss Spann's gift. signs himself "Tommy Atkins," in a letter written from "Trenches," as follows :--

Trenches, 240115.

My Dear Little Gir/; I have jtrst had handed to me a small gift from you, which I appreciate very much. It is very kind of you to think of us Tommies out here who are fighting for our King and country, and we are doing out best to beat those naughty Germans as quickly as possible. It is a shame and pity to see •Ile small villages about here. they are simply in ruins, and all the little children are without any homes, and mothers and fathers.

Whilst writing this shells are bursting all about us, so you see we are in danger every- where we go. We saw seven aeroplanes up at once last night.

I must now thank you again for yow kind- ness, and must say that I am proud of the little girls at Arpley-street School for doing their share in this terrible war.

With heaps of love and kisses,

TOMMY ATKINS

Mr. Spann is on ambulance duty as Sergeant, 10th Co. R.A.M.C., at Aylesford Military Hospital, near Maidstone.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
MISSING.

PRIVATE JAMES GOUGH.

When Last Seen by Comrade Was Cheering Up " Pals."•

Private James Gough, 2nd Cheshire Regt., son of Mr. Owen Gough, 299, Liverpool-road, sawyer at Messrs. Clare and Ridgway's, and caretaker at the Wesleyal Chapel, Sankey. 13ridges, is unofficially reported to be missing. The War Office have no record of any casualty in this case, but Private Gough has ceased to write home, and a chum in the same regiment, Private W. H. Peck, whose home is also in Liverpool-road, has written to say that "Jim" is _missing. _

Private- Gough had been in India fire years with the Cheshires when at the end of last year he was sent to England and then to the Front. He was at home for a few days in the New Year before he sailed for France. He has a brother, Joseph Gough, in the Ist South bancashires in India.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
" My Best Churn—Your Son !"

Private Peck, writing to Mrs. Gough, May 12th, says :—" I don't know how to pluck up enough courage to write of you concerning Jim; still, on the whole, I consider it something of a duty to write of the man whom I regarded as my best chum—your son. He was in an advanced trench for days and had to face the worst possible fighting that ever man could face. We got pushed back—and again Jim was in the thick of it. . . . Jim and others of our chaps were cut off by the enemy. This happened about four days ago and Jim must have been taken a prisoner, for he has not turned up yet. . . . I suppose you will hear from the War Office I hope God spares rap to meet Jim after the war. . . . . Our chaps bumped up against 'a sock." ,

===== 0002601_19150612_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
Not Hit When Last Seen.

In a subsequent letter Private Peck writes "I made inquiries off two of the chaps who were in Jim's trench and they told me, that Jim was not hit when they last 4Sti IV him.' It was on the Bth of May that the enemy rushed us in swarms and we got the order 'Every man away!' But we did not run

My comp-any stopped, and ,Jim, I know, was one of them. . . . He cheered several men up by saying 'Buck up, So-and-So '—mention- ing their names. He had a chum with him— quite a little chap, who 'had not got much service in '—and Jim made him take his coat

off so that he could work his rifle more quickly. You see, Jim was in an advanced trench, and when the order came for the occupants to make tracks the Germans were on them. I still en- tertain hopes that Jim is safe On that day the Germans tried to take the place from us I am sorry to say that the

other two men who were in the smile part of the trench as Jim have since gone under. it is awful. . . . . I cannot get the scenes 'off

my mind Tim and fwere together in India for years, and we were great chums be- 'fore we went there. It seems strange without one's chum—with nobody to talk to." In conclusion the writer tenders thanks to Mrs. Gough for a parcel which she had for- warded him and makes reference to the fact that a parcel came for the missing Jim, and adds, but "somehow I could not touch it. I gave it away 1"

===== 0002601_19150612_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
A Comrade's Fears.

A letter has also been received from a com- rade—Rifleman Thomas Waring, whose home is

in Hoyle-street, and who is now in hospital—in which the writer states that he believes Rifle- man Woodey to be either killed or wounded. "If he is not," he adds, "he is a lucky one. I hope he is lucky. He is the only ' pal ' I had in the trenches."

Rifleman Woodey, who is 21 years of age, is a single man. As a boy he attended the Heath. side day school. On the outbreak of war he left the Warrington Wire Rope Works, Bewsey-road; and enlisted in the Rifle Brigade. In pre-war days he frequently attended ,the services at the All Saints' Mission, at which place of worship he was very well known. He was at one time a member of the football club connected with the Mission.

LAY MM

"Tommy's "Bit of a Rash."—"l am not up to the mark at present; got a rash all over m 3- face and arms," writes Private J. Clare from the Front. "But I think it will be all right in a day or two?!

"Living Pictures" Visit School.—On Thurs- day evening a good number of Lymm children and adults gathered in the village school to wit- ness a very fine war exhibition of living pictures. This is a rare event in the life of the township.

Three Marriages.—The June issue of the Parish Magazine records three marriages May Bth, John Taylor to Nellie Blease, both of Lymm; May 12th, Albert Heathcote, Lymm, to Florrie Talbot,. Oughtrington; and May 29th, George Ditchfield to Nellie Ravenscroft, both of Lymm.

Gentle Reminder.—There is still plenty of unfilled space on the subscription lists for the Urban District Council Roll of Honour. The lists will be found at the Council Offices, Banks, Drill Hail and other public buildings; and contributions, however small or great, will be welcomed.

Postmaster Wedded.—At the Warrington Wesleyan Church on Tuesday, took plaee the wedding of the Lymm postmaster—Mr. James Arnell, now of Grove-avenue, Booth's Hill—and Miss Elsie Miller, of the Causeway-avenue, War- rington.

Correction.—At last week's meeting of the Lymm Education Administrative Sub-commit- tee the resolution of sympathy with Mr. and Mrs. T. .T. Ridgway was, of course, seconded by Mr. S. W. Gould, not Mr. Groves—as we inad- vertently stated the name of the Vice-Chair- man.

Dead Warriors: Rector's Sympathy.--In the June Parish Magazine, the Rector writes : Our deepest sympathy goes out to Mrs. Wal- ter Jackson on the death of her husband in Flanders; and to Mr. and Mrs. Ridgway, of Wildersmair, whose second son has died of, wounds received while fighting with the Aus- tralian contingent in the Dardanelles."

Wesley Guild.—The monthly devotional Meet- ing of the Wesley Guild was held on Monday, and was presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. A very interesting address on "A great Old Tes- tament Prayer" was given by the Rev. H. T. Dixon, of Penketh. Miss Stewart gave a sweet rendition of the solo, "Rock of Ages." There was a moderate attendance.

Heatley Train Mishap.—At mid-day on Wed- nesday railway traffic was suspended for some hours in consequence of a slight mishap. A goods train went off the line at some points, and ran on to the goods yard. No serious damage was done, however, and the crane of the "break-down engine .!':—which was promptly run up from Warrington—soon lifted the wayward locomotive on to the lines again.

Red Cross Funds.—At present there are many heavy calls upon the resources of the Red Cross Hospital Working Men's Committee, in conse- quence of recent alterations, extensions, and im- provements to-meet the needs of the many wounded warriors now stationed there. Conse- quently it is the intention of the Working Men's Committee to make a special appeal and effort to augment the funds.

Sergeant Hit With Grenade.—A letter received from Sergeant 11. Clare, says:— " I have recovered from my illness after nine days' good treatment in hospital. I suffered terribly with pains in my head arid face about a month ago; I got hit just over my left eye with a piece of a rifle grenade, and I have not felt the same till going to hospital; but am glad to say I feel first rate now."

Italian Church Music.—Very appropriate is the decision of Mr. William Cooke, to play, at the Parish Church services through June, vol- untaries by Italian composers. The pieces selected are: Canzonetta (A. d'Ambrosio); Offertorio (Vincenzo A. Petrali); Marcia Reli- giosa (Edoardo Perelli); Pastorale (Core11i); Offertorio (D. Zipoli); Allegretto Villereccio (P. Fumagalli) ; Alla Marcia (V. A. Petrali); Sanc- tus ("Requiem ") (Verdi); Andante Religioso (Fran.cesco Sangelli); Fantasia (E. B. Pollen).

Pity the Poor Farmer!—The long drouaht is having a disastrous effect on Cheshire crops, and potatoes and other roots are completely spoiling through the absence of rain. Farmers state that the hay crops in the Cheshire dis- tricts will be the lightest known for many years. Clover so far is a complete failure, and cutting is supposed to commence in a fcw days. The pastures are now bare, and farmers are even complaining of shortage of water for cattle. The drought is having similar bad effects in fruit orchards.

Letter Read at Parish Church.—At thd Church of St. Mary-the-Virgin there was read on Sunday a pastoral letter—drawn up .by the Archbishops at the request of the Bishops of the English Dioceses at their Whitsuntide meeting.—ln this letter they said they have cause to fear that the power of these spiritual forces has slackened since the earlier weeks of the war, and that they are not as alert, as watchful,. and as persistent as they ought to be. In these tremendous weeks of tense con- flict, of crushing bereavement and continuous suspense and strain, the duty lies imperatively upon all who profess and call themselves Chris- tians to put into the high service of prayer all the energy and resolution, all the time and thought, of which they are capable.

Sergeant Compliments " Examiner."--Coun- calor P. Lockyer, of The Star Hotel, Statham, has received an interesting letter from Sergt. J. Hewitt, of the Ist Welsh Regiment, attached sth Leicesters. The Sergeant pays a gratifying compliment to "The Examiner" war-service, and continues: "As to the war, I can only admit that it is going to be a very long and tough job. The last concerning Jim was that he is quite well. Sammy [Private Sam Booth, also of the Ist Welsh] has been wounded again, but John was all right after having a rough time of it. . . . I am quite all right up to now. I have seen plenty of letters, ttc., in The Examiner' from Territorial. . . Re- member me to all the boys. . . ." High Legh Organist Dead.—With regret we record the death of Mr. Ralph Wheeler, at the age of about sixty—which sad event took place from pneumonia at his home, Cherry-lane, Lymm, on Thursday morning. Son of the late Mr. and Mrs'. John Wheeler, who also lived in Cherry-lane, he was by occupation-a wheel- wright: and also held the post of organist at St. -John's Chapel, High Leh. He leaves two sisters---/with whom he lived—to mourn their loss. '

He was unmarried, and had spent all his life in Lymm—coming of an old local family. Hi employer was Mr. John Holt. Previous to join- ing the High 4Legh Church, • he acted as organist at the Congregational Church (at which he had been brought up) under the ministry of the Rev. T. W. Pinn, now of Stock- port.

Primitive Methodist Quarterly Meeting.— There was a very good :attendance at the quar- terly meeting of the Primitive Methodists, held at -Broom Edge on Saturday. The ministers, the Rev. R. W. Ferguson and the Rev. W. Daw, were invited for another year, the vote being unaninions for them to stay. Tea was partaken of, about 50 being Present. After tea a concert was held, the Chairman being Mr. F. Keen. The artistes taking part were: Mrs. Thomas (song), Mr. unison (recitation), Mr. G. Okell (song), Miss Roberts (song), Mr. G. Okell (banjo), Mr. Biddulf (song), Mr. Stuart (song), Mr. Bradbury (gramophone). A vote of thanks to the following ladies for their help was passed: Mrs. Ford, Miss Cross, Mrs. Atkinson, Mrs. Moss and Miss Eva Perrin. A very hearty vote of thanks was passed te all the artistes. The singing by the Altrincham P.M. Quartet Party was a special feature.

RECTOR ON COALITION.

Duties, Aims and Prospects of New ,

Government.

The Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston) writes a long letter in this month's Parish Magazine, and says: "With the advent to power of a National Government, we enter as a country upon a new phase of the war. The fact of the establishment of such a Government, from which all trace of party spirit is eliminated, will in itself, bring relief to thousands upon thousands who for the past few weeks have been sadly perplexed and distressed. Many have felt, and felt rightly, that the serious issues at stake have not been fully grasped by the people as a whole to anything like the same extent as is the case both among our Allies and our enemies; any definite step that will bring home to all the gravity of the situation will not only be warmlY welcomed, but will mark the path to certain victory. " It would be no exaggeration to say that we could win if we liked, but if we allow time

..to go by while we live on in a fool's paradise the result may be such that all will bitterly regret when it is too late to provide a remedy.

Be warned in time ' is not only a sound motto, but a fundamental maxim for every- thing in life."

After referring to the "willing service" of the country's "best and noblest sons in num- bers that the most sanguine could hardly have anticipated,". the Rector continues: "But this war is proving to be a more serious matter than could have been imagined ten months ago, and there is little donbt but :that some compulsory-, system will need to be instituted. This is no question of forcing What some are 'too 'ready to call slackers and 'cowards to enlist, but it will concern a very large class of men who are both brave and patriotic, Whose way is not clear, hut who are quite Willing to serve if their country needs theft, and WM send for them. It is this class that has coin- manded our sympathy more than any other during the past few months.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
Provision of Munitions.

"Of the provision of munitions it is hardly necessary to. speak, as everybody will agree that the Government, by the creation of a State department for this purpose and placing at the head of it one of the foremost statesmen in the country, has secured that the short- comings in the past in this respect will cer- tainly be soon made good.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
Women and Church.

"But the organisation of a nation at war cannot stop here; we must all be made to feel that we have a part to play. Especially does this concern the women, whose ability and patriotism to help their country in its hour of need are above. reproach. . . But there is a matter over and above all these by which we can not only assist the cause of righteous- ness . but weld the whole Empire • and inspire it with the proper spirit. This is the essential work of the Church of Christ—to quicken and to guide the spiritual forces on which the strength, the steadfastness and the nobility of the national spirit depend. . ."
