VOLUNTEER ACTIVITIES.

Church Parade On Sunday; lianceuvres On Monday.

To-morrow morning at the Lymm Parish Church there is to be a parade of the local de- tachment of the Cheshire Volunteers. The Rec- tor (th'e Rev. G. B. Thurston) is hoped to preach. The Volunteers fall in at the Drill Hall at 10 a.m., and, accompanied by the Heatley Boy 'Scouts' Bugle Band, march round the village en route for the church.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
At Grappenhall

At Grappenhall on Monday evening the Lvmm detachment—under the 'command of Captain George R. -Williamson and Sergeants J. Lister- Farrar and S. D. Moston—is to meet the .War- rington Corps for the purpose of manceuvres. The Ly-mm Volunteers are now in a particu- larly sound and active position, making splen- did progress, thanks to the able services of its leaders and the enthusiasm of the men. All de- siring and eligible to join may be assured of a cordial welcome into the ranks.

The local corps were, particularly busy on Saturday, for then they marched to Altrincham. where, in the Market Square, the Cheshire Regiment assembled. The object was a recruit- ing parade, and the' regimental band was in fine form for the function. There was a good mus- ter, and many military drills were gone through. The Lymm platoon was under Captain William- son and Sergeants Farrar and Moston.

Sir Arthur Howarth's and Major Palmer's Visit.

On Thursday evening the Lytum Corps were just about to commence manoeuvring on the Golf Links when. they were as surprised as delighted by the sudden appearance of Sir Arthur Howarth and Major Palmer (adjutant) —neither of whom, wearing mufti—was at first recognised. Major Palmer, seeing Sergeant Sid D. Moston in charge at the time, at once introduced him- self; Whereupon the Sergeant passed the word along and gave the recognised salute. The distinguished visitors then proceeded to inspect the Corps and their rifle range, and ex- pressed themselves "very well pleased" by the men's appearance and efficiency. Twenty minutes afterwards they took their leave as unceremoniously as they had come.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. AND MRS. B. ELLIOTT.

Fine Army Record Of 125 Years.

Mr. and Mrs. B. Elliott, of Canterbury Cot- tage, Lymm, have three sons in the Army. They are:— Bombardier Wmixtm ELLIOTT, Royal Field • Artillery; Sergeant OTHO ELLIOTT, 2nd Cheshires; and Corporal BEN EI.LIOTT, Scots Guards. In addition to these, their other two sons are engaged on munition work : Tom ELLIOTT, draughtsman; and JOHN ELLIOTT, shell-maker. Before giving all his time to munition work, the first of these two belonged to the Man- chester Home Defence Corps. _ It may be noted that Mr. B. Elliott,gsen., has had many other fighting relatives. Two of his brothers served right through the Crimean War, one (Otho Christopher) being a sergeant in the 63rd Regiment; during feats of bravery he was three times shot, and after recovering under the personal care of Miss Florence Nightingale, was awarded the Legion of Hon- our, with a life-penSion of .-tIO a year. The second • brother (Torn) was one of the. Marines, and he also went through the Egyptian War— for which he has the medal. • ,

Mr. Elliott's grandfather also served as Pay- master-General Viles in the 3rd Dragoon Guards during the Napoleonic Wars, and went through sixteen engagements—including Water- loo, where he was with the Duke of Welling- ton. lie in his turn had five sons in the Army. Mr. Elliott has had, in addition, 60 many cousins and nephews in the 'Army that he has last eount.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
General Havelock.

Apart from this record, Mrs. Elliott's family has a long Army record. They come of the family of General Sir Henry Havelock, who was killed at Lucknow. Mrs., Elliott's grand- father and only uncle were both soldiers; and she had a nephew who last his life in the Boer War. Moreover, she has a nephew now engaged on munition work. Probably there are few families which can beat this very fine record.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 12, 1915.

LYM M TERRITORIALS.

BACK IN NOW QUIET TRENCHES

AFTER REST.

The Lymm "Terriers," having now con- cluded their rest in the pretty little French village, are back again in the trenches once more—" cleaned up," refreshed and in- vigorated. Writing on Saturday, June 4th, Private Joseph Clare says: "Just a line or two from the trench, which we have been in for four days [since Tuesday] and stopping in a few.more yet. We are better off in the trenches than out. " I think it will not be long before we shall all meet in our own land. We are miles away now from where Richard Shaw left us." _ _

Private Percy Clare's word runs: "We don't know how long we are in the trenches for; we might be out again by the ,time you get this. We might be still in them, and I don't mind if we are—as it is very quiet here now. I don't think we shall have much more trench work now. . . . We are, giving lesson; we have them in the trenches with us now. . . .

"We are having grand weather. It is grand this morning [the sth], and I am sat in a corner of the trench writing this letter. Now we might get a soft job. We have lost about a dozen while we have been in the trenches this time, and one was an officer. "Some ,of the other lads got wounded, and one killed while going to fetch water in the day time. So you see you cannot be -too' careful. But I am pleased to say that we [Lymm men] are all keeping in the best of health. . . . We can only send one of these a week from. the trenches. . . . I don't think we shall be in long before we go for a tgood rest. . . . • Remember me to R. Shaw; tell him to 'keep on doing it.' " Yesterday Private H. Cadman's communica- tion ran: "We have been in the trenches for seven *days now. and I do not know when we are going out. Some of the men saY that when we do come out we are going down the line for a bit. Ido not know if it is a true tale or not. for we have heard so many stories that we-do not take much notice of them now. But we will live in hopes:" . Yesterday word was also received of thesrapid death from wounds of Private Jack Guest, who was shot in the head whilst .on outpost duty.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE PARTICULARS OF REST.

There was apparently Some difficulty in the prompt despatch of the " Terriers' " letters from the village in which they were resting, and several written from there have reached Lymm this week.

Well Away From Trenches For a Week.—To Mr. Sid Moston, "Newfield," Private Percy Clare writes on May 27th.:—" We have been well away from the trenches for about a week now. We Are in a very nice French village; it is a grand place, and we cannot tell that there is a war on. We have just stopped in a village, while having a,walk round. . . . I hope we are not going back yet. . .'." To his mothef the Private says.:—" We are well down the line now, living in a barn at a farmhouse. We are having very nice weather; it seems too good to last. I think we have earned a rest, and we are all hoping to have one. .. . We all had a good bath the other day, and changed. . . ." A week ago the Private's latest letter ran:— " We have had a nice week's rest; I think we shall be going back again at the beginning of the week—well, that is a rumour. But I hope not, as it is -fine where we are at present; everywhere looks so nice and calm; it is .very pretty round here. We might be here when you receive this, or we might be in did trenches."

Sergeant Clare writes to his mother at Booth's Hill:—" The change is doing us all an enormous amount of good. I am sorry to say Tom Kirkpatrick and Bob Leigh have not recovered from their illnesses yet, but I have iust inquired, and find they are doing very well. . . ." •

Private Joseph Clare says on May 25th:—"! think it will tie a long time before we go back yet. . . . We are a good way from the firing line, and are going further away still in a day or two. We .have seen enough of war if we never see any more. It has been a treat to have a proper night's rest, and if a man doesn't get his rest—well he can't stick out long with the work he has to do. . . ."

Private Henry Cadman writes to say they. are ten miles away from the firing line, and it is a treat! The weather, he adds, continues to be fine—which is very pleasant after the recent very wet days and nights. _ _

Apparently there is some difficulty in getting letters through from the Lymm "Terriers" at present. One to a young lady runs: "We are still in the little French village, and a pretty place it is, too! There are some walks round here that remind us of Lymm. It is Saturday night, and lam writing this letter in the fields. We shall be going to bed soon. We sleep in a barn. Sometimes we sleep in the field—as there are hens in the barn, and they perch just over us; it is not so nice. But we can put up with all these things as long as we keep living. . . "I hope we don't go back yet; but we have done-it once, and we can do. it again. We came down here because half of our men were sick. We are having some grand weather. It is (mite hot, and it will be fine if it keeps like this while We are having a well-earned rest. It is a treat to get the trenches out of our minds for a bit. We are living at a. farmhouse; . . . everything looks so nice and green."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JACK GUEST.

"The Country Has Lost A Good

Soldier."

June 7th.—Dear Mr. and Mrs. Guest,-1 deeply regret to have to inform you of the death of your son Jack. We are all anxious

===== 0002601_19150612_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
ing letter reach Lymm, with tragic suddenness. So is recorded the death at the Front of the second Lymm Territorial, and the third. mem- ber of the Lymm platoon. Yesterday morning also arrived three other letters bearing the news of Private Guest's heroic end. Of these the first ran:—

"7/6/15.—Dear Mr. and Mrs.. Guest,—l very much regret to inform you that your son Jack gave his life whilst bravely doing his duty at daybreak this morning. 'lie was at his post when unfortunately a bullet went through his head. He did not die instantly; in fact, I thought he would live, providing we took the risk of taking, him to the doctor at the dressing station—a distance 'of about a mile and a half •from the trenches.

" Although it was getting daylight, the Lymm men gladly volunteered to take the risk—Lance- Corporal Kay, Privates W. Jones, .L Read, H. Brookes, my brother Joe and myself. We took poor Jack as quickly as possible without mishap, but the doctor told me there was no hope.. He was unconscious all the time, dying a hero's death. We got back to the trenches safely, and we have just got to •know Jack has died. We did all we possibly could to save him. Private H. Brookes, our stretcher-bearer, worked splen- didly in putting on the bandages. " Our comrade's death has cast a deep gloom over us all. He was greatly respected by the officers and men alike—and particularly our Lymm men. He was such a bright and happy lad, and strongly devoted to his duty; always to be trusted, and always reliable. You have lost a good son; England has lost a good soldier. I beg to express to you, on behalf of his Lymm comrades, our deepest sympathy 'in this your great sorrow. It pains me to write this letter, but, I thought it would be some little comfort: for you need it. Try to realise that poor Jack has given his life for the King and country he loved so much.

He will be buried by our chaplain in a pretty spot, and I shall apply for us to attend his funeral. The place no doubt will be communicated to you in due course.

"If there is anything I can do for you I shall be pleased. I trust you will bear your great loss bravely; I know how very hard it will be, but Jack himself was so very brave.—Yours faith- fully, H. CLARE, Sergeant." Private Henry Cadman—a half-cousin and friend of the dead Territorial—writes to his sister, Mrs. S. Owen : " We have not had a very bad time in the trenches this time, but I am sorry to say we have had rather a good many wounded, and four or five killed. lam very sorry to say that Jack Guest was badly wounded in the head this morn- ing [June Bth], and the doctor said .that there was no hope for him. He was next to me in the trench when he got hit; he and I have been together on outpost duty while we have been in the trenches this last time, and have been in the same dug-out, had meals together, and shared our food with one another.

"We had been on this outpost duty, in front of our trenches and on a canal bank, for three nights; and it was riot a very nice job, I can tell you. For we have to keep quiet and lie down in one place for two hours, and then someone else takes our place and stops there for the same time."

Private J. Clare writes to his mother: "This is our sixth day in the trench, and I have very bad news to tell you. . . . We have had the misfortune to lose Jack Guest—killed by a bullet. His poor head was in an awful state. It was at about 2.30 this morning (Monday) that he got hit. Word came up for stretcher-bearers, and off went Henry Brookes. He found it was Jack! Well, he bandaged him up and then put him on a stretcher; and off went six of us to the dressing station .513 as to get him attended to straight away-. But as soon as the doctor had seen him he said he could do nothing with him. So we left- the poor lad to pass away in peace. We are all thinking this moment of his' parents, but he died a soldier's death and did his duty:"

===== 0002601_19150612_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM •BORN AND BRED.

The fifth and youngest son of Mr. and Mrs. John Guest—of Legh-street, off Bridgewater- street—Private Guest was born in Lymm twenty years ago, and received his education at the village school. He attended the Congregational Church, and worked as an anprentice plumber for Mr. Henry Thomason. builder. Also he had belonged to the Saturday Football and the Lymm Lads' Clubs.

He was unmarried, and previous to the out- break of war, for some months had belonged to the Territorials. Lost summer he attended his first camp; when the call to arms sounded wail he entered upon rigorous training in different parts of the South. eventually leaving for the FroTA in February with the other Lymm "Terriers."

"The Exsminer " has on qeversl °cessions the Drivilege of publishing the private's letters home.

The dennnst svmnathv is felt throughout fhn village with his relatives and fiancee.

Last Letter: "Do Your-Best."

Private Guest's last letter was sent on May 30th to Jack Guest, his little nephew. It was written "in the fields," and ran:

_ "Dear Jack,—Just a line to let you know I am going on all right, hoping you are the same. I've been a long time sending the Belgian penny and halfpenny, but I just found time to write, and now it's not much. We are going in the trenches again. We shall be in, I think, when you get this. Dear Jack, the last time / heard about Mr. Oddie.he Was ill. Has he got better? I hope he has. How are you getting on at school? Do your best there; you veil' find it is the best for you. I used to wish I could leave, but I wish was there now. Just remember me to Mr. Oddie [the headmaster], will you? I've got a photo of Statham May Queen; I sup- pose you saw it? How have you enjoyed your- self at the fair? Give my love to all. . . .

Tell them I am all right, *hoping to see you all soon. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE STANLEY GIBBS.

Private Stanley Gibbs, who was recently wounded, is, we are glad to state, now making good progress towards recovery at the Toxteth Park Hospital Liverpool. He is shortly to undergo an operation for the extraction from his left hand of other parts of the bullet which caused him to be placed on the casualty list.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
I'o •1\ D FRO' TRENCHES.

Corporal J. Mosion Fills In A Few

Details.

Corporal Jack 11. Moston, a well-known mem- ber of the 1/sth Cheshires—whose home is at "Newfield "—sends a graphic letter ,home to .his brother, Mr. Syd. 11. Moston (who holds the rank of sergeant in the Lymm detachment of the Cheshire Volunteers). This letter gives many of the Lynn "Terrier?" experiences since going abroad:— " We are all going on very nicely, and are having a good time at present. We are stay- ing at a pretty village in France; the scenery is lovely. But I don't think that we shall stop here for long.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
With Pick and Spade. re'

"We came out here voluntarily to the call, after six months' hard training in England. At first our spirits were damped, because when .we passed the Regulars they would say: tt's only the Territorials!' But when they saw the Cheshire boys work with pick and. spade they began to have a respect, for us Saturday afternoon soldiers.' We are attached to the - Brigade, and, although I say it myself, we are looked upon as the crack Territorial Corps.

- . "I will now try to give you an insight as to what we are doing, or have done. As a rule we go in the trenches for four days, and then rest for four days. Picture yourself with me in the ranks. It is at night we go in the trenches' and each man is carrying full kit and two days' ratiOns. We get the word' Fall in'; and the ,Fall-in is done in silence. We then move off three or four miles to relieve our comrades.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
Through Town and Country.

"On our way we pass through the deserted streets of a town, with buildings blown half- down—some with great boles in the sides, where shells have made their entrance. We then pass oat on to a country lane leading to our' destination. Here ,and there the man in

front will say: 'Hole on the right.' Then you see a big 'Jack Johnson' hole.

Battered Farmhouse.

"Then; when a mile and a half from the trenches, we halt at some: battered farmhouse for a short .rest—which is welcome, as your Pack gradually feels to become a ton-weight. "We next leave the road and make our way across fields and ditches; and all about are great hqles—which we carefully avoid. We are now in single-file and under rifle-fire. We forget our weight, as our thoughts are centred on getting behind those sand-bags safely. As W 8 move slowly along, the Germans fire rockets high in the air; then all of is fall flat on the ground, regardless of mud or water—because we realise the -value of our lives.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
"Crawl Out."

"So with a few'. more struggling efforts we reach the trenches 'absolutely exhausted.' We are then posted on sentry, and the men whom we have relieved crawl out in the same manner as we came in. " Can.our friends at home realise a time like this? I should say not: 'Tommy' has no sleep at night—as you never know when the Germans are going to make an attack. Those men in England who have hung back perhaps don't think, when snug in bed, that poor 'Tommy' is tramping up and down the trenches, with back bent, facing death every minute.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
Artillery Duels.

"It is generally quiet in the day-time, except when the artillery start their duels. When shells .start bursting in the trenches, and bul- lets are flying, and you see your pals killed or wounded, it makes even the hardest-hearted ask their Maker to spare them. I have seen men, myself included, on their knees in silent prayer, with the fear of God in their hearts. "The. shells are terrible omens of death—. more so as we have to crouch down in the bottom of the trench. It is a terrific Way of fighting. I assure you I witnessed a bombard- ment a short time .ago, and if pretty little Lymm had only been a mile off it would have been shaken down. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
SEEN THROUGH FIELD-GLASSES.

How Territorials Met Transport

Friends.

. . On June Ist word from Private S. Clare ran: " We have seen Reggie Smith [one of the two soldier-sons of Mr. George Smith. master painter, Booth's Hill]. He said he had been out here ten days, and that he had got a ' cushy ' job. We were very lucky to drop ffn him; for we were., in a field about two hundred yards from the road, and this transport came past and stopped just facing us. "When they had been there about one hour I happened to get Harold's [Sergeant Clare's] field-glasses to see who they were, and my eyes dropped on this fellow. I could not make him out properly, although I thought I knew him; but to make sure I went to see—and\ there it was. R. Smith! Well,. I was more surprised than he was! . . . "I believe G. Appleton is out here, so per- haps some of these fine days we shall have the luck to drop on him."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST, STOLEN OR STRAYED. Letters And Gifts Get Lost To And

Fro' The Front.

Private Henry Cadman , has written from the Front to his sister, Mrs. S. Owen, Brook- field Cottages. After assuring her of his good health, he adds: " You would think I was done for altogether when you received word that I was wounded a second time. But it is good that it was only a mistake. I have sent you two letters since we came up the line, but we can't help them getting astray. . . . I am very sorry I did not get the song-book that Henry sent me. . . "I hope things are going on, all right at home; I often think of being there. It makes us think a bit when we are .out here and see the homes of people over here in ruins. I would not believe it before, and you would not; but it is quite true what you see in the papers. And then, they do not put all in the papers. It would open your eyes if you had seen some of the towns and villages in heaps of ruins. "

===== 0002601_19150612_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE SAMUEL BOOTH.

Private Samuel Booth, of the Ist Battalion Welsh Regiment—whose home is at Statham has again been wounded. He is at present in Leeds Infirmary, where he has been visited by his sister. His leg has been severely injured by a shell. Private Booth was first wounded in October during the fighting around Ypres, so his experience is no new one.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPL. REG. lIILLIER.

Progressing Well After OperatioA At

Malta.

This week a notion has gained currency in Lymm that Lance-Corporal Reg. Hillier has died of wounds received whilst serving with the Manchester Territorials in the Dardanelles. This .is not so. Its origin was from one of the Manchester evening papers, which inadver- tently included the Lance-Corporal's name amongst the "Died of Wounds" instead of the "Wounded" in. the casualty lists. -Those re- sponsible have, however, since communicated with Mr. W. E. Hillier, of the Village, ex- plaining that it was quite a mistake. • The people of Lymm, seeing the newspaper list, tendered Many expressions of sympathy to the wounded soldier's parents; for which they de-. sire heartily to thank them, although it has now transpired that there Was no ground for such; _ _ .

===== 0002601_19150612_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
"Feeling More Himself Again."

From Lance-Corporal Hillier arrived, on Saturday, a letter dispatched from the Malta Hospital on May 29th. This said that the writer was feeling more himself again after the chloroform and operation of extracting the bullet. He was going on well, and would be quite himself again in a few weeks' time.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
LIF.I*T. XX . N. AIOt.ESWORTII.

Nlanchester Business Man AN ounded

In Dardanelles.

• On Thursday evening ,a telegram arrived at Darley House, Lymm, to -say that Lieutenant William Nassau Molesworth had been slightly wounded whilst engaged inland fighting in the Dardanelles. Apparently the telegram had been despatched by the wounded Lieutenant him- self. No further news is yet to hand.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
Rugby Footballer.

The only son of Mr. W. N. Molesworth, of Derby House, Lieutenant Molesworth is single, and has for the last six years made his home at Lymm with his father. Educated at Hailebury School, Hertford- shire, he afterwards entered business in Man- choker; and is there a member of the Calico Printers' Association.: He is also an enthusi- astic footballer, .and played as three-quarter back for the Manchester Rugby Football Club.

,•• . Upon the sudden outbreak of hostilities he deided to join H.M. Forces—and did so on the second day of the war! On August 6th he was gazetted Lieutenant • in the 6th Manchester Territorials, and a month after journeyed with his regiment to Cairo, Egypt. Here he remained until a month ago—when the regiment entered upon the land fighting, in the vicinity of the Dardanelles.

Red Cross Wounded.—Three new arrivals at the Lymm Red Cross Hospital this week are: Private A. R. Cordinglee, K.0.R.W.; Private T. Meaking. Ist Coldstreams; and Private Brewster, 16th Canadians. Early in the week the institution was bidden a reluctant good-bye by Privates Beatty, Robertson', Groves and McWilliams.

Seriously Wounded In France By

Poison-Bomb.

Lieutenant Alfred Edward Watkin, of the 3rd Royal Welsh Fusiliers, has been seriously wounded whilst serving in France, and now lies in considerable pain in hospital at Boulogne. Happily he makes good progress. In a War Office telegram to his parents, the news was first received on Monday. This tele- gram stated that the Lieutenant had received gunshot wounds in the thighs and was dan- gerously ill; it added that he might be visited. Immediately his wife and father set out for Boulogne, where they are now with him. On Thursday Mrs. Watkin was 'notified by further telegram that it was by a poisonous bomb that

LIUETENANT WATKIN.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
her son had been wounded, and that, though now suffering a great deal, he was progressing well.

Of Manchester Exchange.

Elder son of Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Watkin, of Danebank, Lymm, Lieutenant A. E. Watkin married the daughter of the late Mr. C. J. Miller, of Oughtrington House, and now resides at Knutsford. His father is head .of the firm of Absolom Watkin and Son, Manchester; and before the war the Lieutenant was a well known and exceedingly popular member of the Man- chester Exchange—where the news of his wound- ing has been received with general regret and deep sympathy. Upon the outbreak of hostilities Lieutenant Watkin volunteered, joining the 3rd Welsh Fusi- liers, and until about five weeks ago was train- ing at Wrexham—where his regiment had been stationed since October. In France he had only been for four weeks at the time of his casualty. The wounded Lieutenant, having been born and spent a considerable part of his life locally,. is highly respected in Lymm—by the residents of which much sympathy is felt with all the family. He has one little son.

AN TROBUS.

Without A Light—Ernest Wilkinson, of Pool Platt Farm, Antrobus, was fined 2s. 6(1. at the Daresbury Petty Sessions held at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, for riding a bicycle without : a light in Barber's-lane, at 10 p.m. oil 19th ult.

GRAPPENHA LL.

Removing the Pigs.—Thos. Chamberlain, (If Grappenhall, was summoned at the DaresburY Petty Sessions, held at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, for contravening a regulation of the Swine Fever Order, in the matter of the, removal of pigs, at Grappenhall, on the 7th of May. He was fined 20s.

CLUB HOUSE RIFLED.

Prisoner Who AVlshes To " The Game Up."

"I have broken into this place nine or tett times, and want to get it off my mind. I have had pounds worth of stuff out of it, and wish to give the game up, and try to lead a better life.'' Bailey, with breaking and entering the pavilion of the Mersey Golf Club, Penketh, he" tween May 7th and Bth. and stealing a pair of • trousers and a pair of boots, the property 01 George Smith, 67, Knutsford-road, Warring' ton, and Fred Joynson, 7, Museum-street, Wa)::". rington, respectively. Joseph Lloyd, a Penketh labourer, spoke tc:, seeing the prisoner loitering in the vicinity el• the clubhouse on May 7th.

Hard-up For Some Money." I was hard up for some money." Deteetive-Inspector Davidson said thn' prisoner voluntarily made a statement to hill?, ' in the course of which he said that, after being liberated from Knutsford Gaol, he went on , Warrington, and from there to Penketh, wher.,,° he broke into the golf club house, subsequentiY, walking to Liverpool. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 12, 1915.

DARESBURY AND SOME OF ITS ASSOCIATIONS.

A Lecture to the Sunday School Union.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
By ARTHUR

Daresbury is unique among English vil- lages in its name., and probably owes it, not to any natural features, but to an ancient family who may have held the land after . the Con- quest. The history of its church takes- us to " the years of the 12th. century, when the Augustinian monks were making themselves felt in the North of England. The Order of St. Augustine were called the White Monks to distinguish them from the Benedictines,' whose habit was black., They wore a five- pointed cap and took the name White Monk from a white tunic and linen gown, whichi showed under a. black 'cloak. Encouraged- by Henry I. and his Queen Maude, they had re- ceived a priory as a royal gift. Other bene- factors followed this example, and by the time Edward I. readied the throne there were more than 50 such houses in the land, at which the children .were taught, the poor fed, and the offices of the ell ureb celebrated.

The Origin of Norton Priory. Among those who followed the royal ex- ample was William fitz Nigel, the second baron of Halton, and the monks found a home at Runcorn with seven. carucates (i.e., 700 acres) of land for their sustenance. But a more tranquil area commended itself, and on the accession of William .fitz • William to the barony, the canons were removed to a fertile and promising spot under the shadow of the Castle of Halton. . It was thus that , Norton Priory was established, and the visitor may still- see a noble relic of -that distant day in the Norman doorway of the Priory. There were then extedsive woods waiting to be cleared, and a rtither wild country waited to be coaxed into fruitfulness; and we may in imagination see the monks, one Of whose rules bound them to inimual labour for a part of every day, setting themselves to the task, and thereby, it may be hoped, winning the ap- proval of the serf and rustic.

An Ancient Charter. • The Charter of A.D. 1135, by which the gift of the land at Norton is made, opens:. In the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy 'Ghost. I, William, the son of William, the son of do give and grant to the Holy Church of Mary of Norton, and to the canons regular serving God there, the viii of Norton in free alms, with all its appur- tenances in wood, with forests and warren and in plain, with lands, pastures and waters. . . 1 change the habitation of the said canons from Runcorn to Nor- ton, which Norton .1 give and grant the said canons in free alms. . . Also give and grant to them the Mill of Halton, with a moiety of all the fisheries which be- long to Halton; 1 also grant them and their tenants common in the Woods, waters, and pagtures which belong to Halton, and two oxgangs of land in Halton, with one dwelling' there, and one moiety of my whole fishery of Thelwall, With one oxgang of land and the fisherman. there. . . I also grant to them common of the woods. and pastures of Cuefdley, and the .Mill, of Barrow.

The gift of land, a mill and fishing rights was a common form of endowment, and secured corn, milling and fish. The right to hunt in the woods was also usually given. Fishing rights in the Mersey would not represent wealth in these days, but were then. a valuable asset.

The donor is at pains to say that he gives all these things to the priory, together with the tithes or part of the tithes from 13 churches and several mills, "for the health of the souls of may father and mother, my •brothers and sisters, and all my ancestors and successors."

The Charter has a vivid ending: .WhosQ, therefore, shall augment or main- tain this charitable foundation, may be, through the benefits of Holy Church, obtain the Kingdom of Heaven.; but whose shall infringe or violate them in any re- spect, may lw, unless he make restitution, be punished in hell with Judas and Pilate, _

Daihan and Abiram. A wooden house first rose among the trees at Norton, with a clearing of the trees to allow of the support of cattle, and afterwards of crops. 'Then came the priory, of which a few portions, including .a fine doorway and some vaulting Tomah'. ~ A church was completed in 1157. It stood to the cast .of the present mansion at Norton, and after some centuries of usefulness was 'destroyed at the dissolution of the Monasteries. Daresbury Church.

Following upon the eiection of the church, Henry, the second prior of Norton, put for- ward the project- of building a church at Daresbury, an outlying and. probably wild and neglected portion of the parish of' Run- corn, where the ordinances might be admin- istered by the canons of Norton. The build- ing, when it. arose at the crest of the wooded slo.pe, was probably a very modest structure. From the fact that it .was known tradition- ally as " the White Church of Cheshire," it may be conjectured that it was of wood and wattle coloured white. A church pf .stone succeeded it, and with various additions and

At the doss- house, too, may generally be found some per- son of superior \education, fallen through in- temperance, who for a small sum will supply an artful begging letter. It has, moreover, long been a common practice to borrow chil- dren to excite public sympathy. It is only right to add that .sine the outbreak of war this ancient profession has sensibly declined, and that such creatures as Nesbit, just on the point of completing his 14 days' " hard " for a local offence, are comparatively scarce at present.

Nick-names. 'These abound all over Lamoashire and York- shire, especially in the smaller country places, where many bear the same surname and are otherwise difficult of. distinction. Here are a few specimens that memory has yielded 'after, A few moments'. cudgelling of my brains : Scheedmer, Gimlet-heels, Sniddle, Knob- stick, Nooasy, Long Jimmy o' th' Loin-top, Carter Dick, Paper John, Muffin-chops,' Billy o' Junters, • Curran Tommy, Jooa o' th' oddlads' ' Dooaz o' -War 'Lijahs Daoaz o' Bettei 'Lijahs, Billy Beawt Brass (the foulest mouthed mortal I ever came across), Smodden, Pudding, ItTtwsty Nop, and Feawl-teawil.

MOUNFIELD.

alterations stood until 1870, when it was de- molisher). The present church, except ,the tower, dates from that year. The tower, though venerable in appearance and .bearing the misleading date 1110, belongs only to the 16th century.

A Misleading Date Stone. Concerning this date stone a warning may be given which will . apply to many other similar carvings. Without any intention to mislead, workmen and churchwardens 6ave often given us some wonderful dates to gape at. But antiquarians are seldom deceived. The date of a cutting can usually be told from its style. Just as a .dressmaker could tell the date of a magazine print by taking 'note of the fashions of the ladies' dresses, a stu- dent of antiquities can tell the date of an in- scription by the fashion of the letter. When, for instance, we are asked to believe that a stone in Hill Cliffe Graveyafd is of the date 1522,, it is only necessary to point out that the style of the letter in which it is cut only came into use in the 18th century. And when we are told that another' is of a date in the 12th century. it is enough to say that the numerals we now use were unknown in this country, as also were gravestones, at that early period. In the present instance the (laic 1110 is cut in figures of a 17th century. style. Had they been cut in the 12th century they would have been totally different in their form. The probability is that a date 1550 was cut upon the stone. At that period the figure five would be only slightly curved, and after being exposed to the weather for a century the in- scription might easily be mistaken for .1110. Someone has recut the stone in the 17th cen- tury and caused it to tell an astonishing story. Relics of the Past.

. Apart from the ,tower, little is left of the older church. • The font .and remnant of a sun-dial are in the churchyard, and there is the 17th century pulpit. Under the church, when the rebuilding waS taken in hand, were found two sepulchral crosses, One of them of We 12th century. There wbre also some tiles of early pattern,. which may have formed, the floor of the wooden church built by prior Henry, and some fragments of-painted glass. Perhaps before we leave these early times with its memories of things given for the good of the church and " the' health of the. souls " of those who gave them, we may re- call two other charters of that same early date and neighbourhood. The "Ship of Widnes."

• To John fits Richard, baron of Halton is due the first ferry at Runcorn. By a char- t6.r he granted to his son W'goon two bovates of •land in Roncover (Runcorn) and a toft and croft in . Widnesso (Widnes), which brought in yearly two shillings " to God and St. john, and the holy house of the hospital at Jerusalem on St. Mary's day " to find " half the necessaries for the passage of • the ship of Widnesse " (i.e., the ferry boat) • for ever "for all who should pass over. there in ftle love of God."

• This eight-hundred-year-old document with its provision of two shillings a year for the 4keep of a ferry li6at is very interesting, and is refreshingly different. from many charters of the period which reveal only the various and numerous moves in the game of grab which the Church played 'won the nobility. Another charter gives us another glimpse of this same ferry. Gamier de Naplouse, of, the Knights Hospitallers, granted lands in Platt and elsewhere to Richard de la More and his heirs on the condition that he paid. four shillings yearly at Michaelmas and kept in repair on the river Mersey at Runcorn the vessel which Baron John `.` for the love of God had provided to carry such persons as desire it across the stream and .that a third part of the chattels of Richard and his heirs in succession came back to the brotherhood for the good of his soul." The Patron Saint of the Ferry.

booking out across the waters of the Mer- sey and upon a crossing that was often stormy and dangerous, it was natural that Norton Priory should place itself under the care of St. Christopher, the saint- who is said to have carried a child across the., dangerous stream, to discover afterwards that it was the Saviour himself whom he had borne. A. gigantic figure of the saint was carved and stood under a canopy in front of the house. It is difficult to judge the age of this huge 'figure, but the canopy under which it stood appears. to have formed part Of the house and suggests that the figure is at least as old as this stonework.

The figure with the infant Christ upon his shoulder may still be studied, and those of us who are carried across the ferry upon the modern railway bridge or more modern trans- porter carriage may, perhaps, spare a kindly thought for the benefactors, of eight centuries ago who provided a " 'ship of \Aridness° with four shillings a year to keep it watertight, and raised to giant St. Christopher to watch over those who passed over in the fear and love of God.

_ CABINET MINISTERS POOL

SALARIES.

Each Member To Get £4,216.

Members of the Cabinet have decided to pool their salaries, so, in this respect, the Coalition is very real. It was felt that the re-arrangement of Offices involved certain invidious comparisons between Ministers who have -formerly been on an equality with regard to salaries. It was there- fore proposed by, we believe, Mr. Bonar Law, and Sir Edward .Carson, that the whole Cabinet, exclusive of the Prime Minister, should put their salaries in a pool and divide the amount equally. The average Conservative ministers' salary was .t,300 higher than the average of the Liberal Ministers' salaries. The average of the two works out at about £3,246 .a year. The chief sacrifices, by this arrangement, are made by the Lord Chancellor, whose Salary is £lO,OOO, and by Sir .Edward. Carson, whose salary, ex- clusive of fees, is .t 7,000. All Secretaries of State who receive uniform salaries of £5,000 give up over £r7oo, and mem- bers of the Cabinet who receive salaries of .t 2,000 including Mr. Churchill, Lord Curzon, Lord Crewe, Lord Selborne, Mr. Harcourt, and Mr. Henderson, will have their new salaries more than doubled.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
c THE PHYSICAL AND SPIRITUAL WORLDS.

Rev. W. Hendy Cock's New

Book.

The Rev. W. Hendy Cock, the Vice-Princi- pal of the Warrington Training College, is a scientist with a realised soul. He thinks scientifically, and, in some measure, the fetters of science, are about the diction in which he gives the results of his thinking to the, world. Tricks of expression (such as the frequent use of " hence " and " therefore ") serve to indicate the temper of the logical- minded rather than the freedom of the ten).- per poetical.. But while this trait hampers his flight and is a drag-rope on his soaring, it will to many readers appear in the character of a virtue, inasmuch as •while the beauty of the cosmos of God finds entrance only • after having been robed with the professor's call and gown, yet the conclusions which are come to in the laboratory will probably be more satisfying and seem the more sure than had thir been served up at a feast where the wed- ding-garment, instead of the student's gown, was the proper vesture. This being understood, the reader of this 'book will find it a. refreshing and stimulat- ing draught after turning from the iconoclas- tic atmosphere of modern theological thought, a breath of the clean fresh air of the heavens pf God. And if he come upon notes of in- terrogation rather freely interspersed, and with no answer to follow them, yet he will also be sensible Of :the fact that these ques- tionings are not those of gloomy doubt, hilt rather suggestive of further inquiry on the reader's own part. Indeed, the whole book is -suggestive, rather than conclusive, in spite of the many conclusions arrived at, and if the reader will but tread the way they paint, he .'ill find these questions each a very. reliable sign-post indicative of some vista of great beauty and of majesty not a little. Let him pursue the writer's thoughts as .far as they go, and when tliey end, let him con-, tinue straight ahead on his own behalf, and he will not, I think, be disappointed of some firmer assurance of the love of God enthusing our ;present life and illuminating, • with even gredter radiance, the life to which we are all predestinate. Mr. Hendy Cock has 'done well to give to the world his thoughts in permanent form.. The , book is the outcome of 'much -wide re- search, and yet it is original, inasmuch as the author retains to •himself the right to give his own opinion on the various questions raised. Frequently he is epigrammatic, and has .a decided gift of clear expression which at times breaks forth in some delightful sen- tence. As witness the following: " The chief aim of 'a man on the earth is the production of happiness in its various forms; and the curious. result is this—that, if the object be the creation of it for self alone the individual always fails to get it; while he who; en- deavours to produce it for others, invariably secures it for himself." "In science we ob- serve phenomena and deduce laws, but no man can discover a, power of causation in. law, which is simply our own statement of sequence and relation as to fact, and evidently expresses the operation of a will behind it, that originates the laws, which are always a product of mind." "The aim of science is the description of facts, the aim of philosophy their interpretation." "Ignorance is hardest to remove in the brutal and in the falsely scientific." " The .first verse of the Book of Genesis gives the .truth, that it took scien- tists ages to discover, viz., that the universe is one connected piece of mechanism and the product of one master hand." The caution of the exact scientist is admirably seen in this extract.: "Natural laws are the expres- sion of the will of this Creator as seen in operation on the matter.; True science is a partial description of His work and method of working in the physical universe. Partial it always must be, while man's vision and capabilities are limited." As to the schenie of the book, this developes in two phases. First is examined the material world and the 'life upon it and the forces which regulate both. 'Emerge from this two facts: First, God is, and God is good; second, God's goodness does not, and cannot, end with this present phase of existence. This much we get from reason.. Have we any evi- dence, then, pf the present existence of human beings, like ourselves, who have ceased to be so far as this material creation is concerned? Evidence in support of an affirmative reply to this question is examined in ,the second half of the book and is found to be satisfactory. His conclusion is stated in these words: " Hence the departed and those left _ on earth may communicate with and influence each other now. Since this is so, why cannot we make our petitions for them, and also talk to them as formerly ?"

The idea of a general -resurrection (old style) and the re-assumption of a material body, spiritualised (in which case it would cease to be matter in any real sense) dies hard. It crops up near the end of Mr. Hendy Cock's book, and although, personally, I refrain from following the author here, yet the idea he puts forth is new to me, and is expressed so clearly and so well, that I can- not refrain from giving it in his own word- ing. "Since the soul by its existenee in the body proves its power of uniting with and participating in the properties of matter, and since the condition of each is affected by and suitable for the other, it is probable that when the world of matter ceases its 'unction in the training of human beings a new com- bination of the human soul and matter will take place, in which a body of matter, spiri tualised, however, for existence purely spiritual ind corresponding to the soul's con- dition, will be given again to the individual so that there be no division or break in the Creator's work." So well is this expressed that I feel almost brutal in suggesting that this implies a long, long wait on the part Of the (shall I use the favourite old .phrase?) " disembodied soul." Nevertheless, there is food for thought here: For if the universe of God is one whole and undivided, it may be that the basal substance of the realm of spirit is ;the product of the sublimation of matter. I believe Hudson Tuttle says so, and Andrew Jackson Davies backs him up. But these are names to.be breathed softly in the public presence of to-day. In conclusion, J would, as one of Warring- ton's lovers, congratulate our old town on adding one more worthy production to that literature which adorns its annals. It has numbered among its townsmen a "goodly number of men who have shone fn the world of learning and of endeavour after 'the up- lifting of the people. The author of the boek of which I have given a very meagre and in- adequate description deserves our thanks and our appreciation. For his aim is a laudable and a high aim. It is the removing of the black curtain from the gate of death in order that the radiance of Paradise may stream through, and the voices of our loved ones may be heard, telling us that with them all is well. So I will leave it there, content to be- lieve with the author that " God's in His heaven, all's right with the world," and that Death, is no wraith of visage pale, But waits attendant on our birth Out of the darkened womb of earth, . • To lead us gently through the Veil: To Realms of radiance, broad and free, To Christ and immortality. - _ -

G.V.O.

"Life in the Physical and Spiritual Worlds. W. Hendy Cock, L.q.P., F.G.S., 13. Sc. (Lond.). London: J. and J. Bennett, Ltd., The Century Press. 58. net.

A FATAL BATH.

Lunatic Accidentally Scalded By Epileptic Patient.

TRACERY IN THE 'WORKHOUSE

Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, an a jury sat at the Warrington Pol;ce Station Dn. Tuesday morning, in order to inquire into the circum- stances attending the death of a certified lunatic, named George Wilmhurst, who died on Friday of last week in the Warrington Union Hospital. Mr. A. Bottomley Watched the pro- ceedings on behalf of the Warrington Board of Guardians, of which he is clerk.

Evidenc6 of identification was given by the widow, Martha Wilinhurst, of -35, Battersby- street, Leigh.

"DID IT TO HELP THE ATTENDANT."

Fred Topping, who was described as a sane epileptic patient under treatment in the special ward for imbeciles and epileptics at the Workhouse, said that at seven O'clock in the morning of Friday, May 28th, .he took Wilm- hurst to the bathroom and bathed him.

The Coroner: Were you told to do so by any- one?

Witness: Not exactly, sir! I did it in order to help the attendant, Mr. Ireathcote, who was then on duty and in another part of the premises.

- The Coroner: Did you receive instructions from Mr. lleathcote to bath -Wilmhurst?—Wit- ness: No, sir; I did not •receive .instructions.— You did it on your own initiative?—Yes sir. , The witness added that he prepared the both first and in order to do so, had to get two keys. which fitted the hot imid cold water taps. 11, found the keys in the male attendant's room, on top of ,a medical chest or cupbokrd. The attendant was not in the room when witness took the keys. The keys were not marked " hot " and " cold " respectively, but each fitted only its own tap. When he had got the bath ready witness went for Wilmhurst and took him to the bathroom and assisted him to bath. At the end and just as the deceased was about to leave the bath, witness noticed some soap on his head and shoulders, and thought lie would rinse it off with cold water from a bowl.

Poured Hot Water Over Him. Instead Of Cold. However, instead of filling the bowl with cold water he accidentally filled it from the hot water tap; it did not steam, and he did not notice it was hot before it was poured over the deceased's head and shoulders. The Coroner: Have you bathed these patients before? Witness: Only now and again. How long have you been in the Workhouse?— Ten months. How many times have you bathed them?— 'Not many. .How many times a week have you bathed them?—All the time I have been there I have only bathed them twice. Other patients, do you mean?—No; Wilm- hurst twice! The Coroner: With the knowledge and con- sent of the male attendant? Did he know you were doing it? . Witness: Yes. Have you seen the code of instructions which is posted up as to bathing inmates?—Yes. Was there a thermometer in the bathroom?— Yes; there was one hanging up.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
Used His Own Judgment As To The Bath.

Could you read it?—No. He added in answer to further questions that he used his own judg- ment as to the suitability of the temperature of the water in the bath. _ _

Did Mr. Heathcote know that you have pre- viously bathed a patient ?—I think so. Did the other attendant, Mr. Malone?—Yes. The „coroner said he presumed that Mr. Bottomley would reserve any examination of the witnesses until an inquiry ;was held by the Guardians or the Local Government Board. Mr. Bottotuley said that was his intention. The Coroner said that Topping had told the Court a straightforward story.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
EPILEPTIC HAD HELPED TO BATH THE

LUNATICS. The next witness called was Thomas Malone, one of the male attendants in the imbecile and epileptic wards at the Workhouse, and he said that ho was on duty up till midnight on the day before the accident. So far as' he knew, the bath keys were in the cupboard in the attend- ant's room when he went off duty. At any rate, they were there when he last saw them; he had not had occasion to use them for three or four days before the accident.

The Coroner: Is it true that during 'the time you have been a male attendant in the Work- house that you have allowed Topping to bath the lunatics?

'Witness: He has never bathed lunatics by my direction, but he has helped, when I have been there. He added, in answer to a further question, that neither of the keys to the bath- room taps had the words." Hot" or "Cold" on them, and that there was nothing on the taps either to show which was hot and which was cold.

You deny that Topping, with your knowledge and consent, has undertaken the sole responsi- bility of bathing patiehts?—l deny that.

• THE WHEREABOUTS OF THE BATH KEYS. Harold Heathcote, another xuale attendant in the imbecile and epileptic wards of the Work- house, said he had held his present position six months, prior to which he had had experience in an asylum in Montreal. When he went on duty at 12 midnight, on. the 28th ult., Malone gave him the key of the cupboard, but he did not know where the keys of the bath taps were. The Coroner: This epileptic, Topping, found them on top of the medicine cupboard. Witness: I-did not know they were there. It is a curious thing he should find them ' there. It seems to me to be a fair inference to draw' from the fact that he looked there for them, that he knew that they were likely to be there, else why Should he look on the top of a 'cupboard for the keys?—l did not know they were there, and I never had occasion to use them durin,, the night. I have used them, but whenever that has happened I have always found them in the medicine chest, where they have to be kept, and where I have always put 'them when I have finished with them. I don't know how they got on to the top of the Cup- board.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
"ALL THAT WAS WRONG!"

It's the last place in the world one would look for keys, even if one were searching for them. In the morning, in order to assist you, Top- ping got these keys, prepared a bath, took a patient into the bathroom and bathed him,. and then, by inadvertence, poured lot water on him instead of cold ! All.that was wrong?—Yes. Has he on any previous occasion bathed patients under similar conditions?—Not during my time on duty. He has assisted me, but I have never allowed him to bath a patient by himself. - •

You say that the man carried out duties he had no right to and against orders?—Yes, no right at all!

And not with your consent, expressed or izu- plied?—No.

A nurse in the Workhouse Hospital gave evi- dence to the effect that the man died at 4.20 on Friday morning last.

EXTENSIVELY SCALDED.

Dr. John Ratcliffe Jagger, resident medical officer at .the .Workhouse Infirmary, said that his attention was drawn to the case a few hours after the scalding. On examination he found that the deceased was extensively scalded over the back, neck and chest, rather less than one- third of the body's surface being affected. The patient's condition got worse and pneumonia set in, and he died, the cause of death being hyportatic pneumonia, accelerated by scalds. In answer to the Coroner, the doetor said that be was not supposed ..to order each separate bath, but was only responsible for saying when a patient was medically fit to be bathed.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
"A WEAK LINK SOMEWHERE."

In summing up, the Coroner said the jury were not there to manage the Warrington Workhouse, or to criticise it, except so far as they thought desirable in connection with the .manner in which Wilmhurst came by his death. Whether the bath that was given was.. the best form of bath to give a patient; whether the taps ought to be of a. different construction and indicate " lot" and "Cold," and whether the keys should bear a similar designation, no doubt the Guardians, in their good judgment, and assisted by their Clerk, would discuss at their Continued in Next Column.

BUYING STOLEN MACHINES

:Queries and correspondence should be addressed to "The Hub," do The Editor. Replies can- not be sent by post and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

To be accused of having purchased goods "well knolving the same to have been stolen" is a serious charge in the eyes of the law. Judging by events that have happened recently, cycle agents may very easily be brought up in such a charge. A case is reported which might have had very serious consequences. A well- known cycle agent was thus charged, but no one, even if conviction had followed, would have believed him guilty of having bought a. machine, knowing the • same to be stolen property: There is always a. possibility of cycle agents being caught napping by clever thieves, and every cycle agent who reads this should ask for the original . receipt when offered a " second-hand " by a stranger, and if possible " temporise " until the receipt can be verified. An apparently ordinary business transaction may develop into a very unpleasant affair if suitable precautions are not taken.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
A WAY OUT OF THE DIFFICULTY.

The agent in question, to prevent others from getting into the -same "corner," suggests the following stringent precaution :—My indis- cretion—it is extremely easy to recognise that fault afterwards very nearly landed me in gaol, for the reason that the magistrates had data to work upon while I possessed none until I created them. It takes but little wisdom to believe that a person standing in the dock, self- accused as a thief, looks like .a thief, but often a Sherlock Holmes to have the slightest sus- picion of a person against whom you know nothing whatever. However, I have learnt a lesson, and that is, never to buy a machine from a stranger, though he have the manner of a saint, or the attitude of an angel, except upon one condition—that he or she must bring to me a written authority from the nearest police station, signed by the officer in charge, that the seller is at liberty to dispose of his or her bicycle. If this method,was adopted by every cycle dealer and pawnbroker, it would practically free the whole trade from running risks with strangers and save loss, not only of money, but of •reputation." I think, however, that an agent who takes the precaution I have suggested would be held free from blame by any Court.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR CYCLISTS AND THE WAR.

It would be difficult to enumerate the many ways in which motor cyclists are serving their country at the Front, since their names appear iii such various branches of the Army. The daring deeds that have been performed by motor cyclists are known to a few; it is only a very small percentage that find their way into the public Press. The exploits of Second-Lieu- tenant Moorhouse .have been re2anled, every rider laments his untimely death. Another aviator, well-known as a prominent rider in many competitions, and a member cf the Oxford University M.C.C., has .also per- formed remarkable deeds, displaying coolness and resource under most trying circumstances. His last performance AVIA S mentioned in a dis- patch from " Eye-Witness " and his name was not given, but I am able to say that the refer- ence was to Flight Lieutenant L. P. Openshaw, R.N.A.S., who fell upwards of 5,000 feet in a spinning aeroplane.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEED DESCRIBED.

The deed constitutes a record even in a ser- vice where hairbreadth escapes are of daily occurrence. In the words of t, e dispatch from "Eye-Witness ":—" One of the British airmen was on May 10th alone in a single-seater aero- plane, in pursuit of a German machine. Whilst trying to re-load his machine-gun he lost con- trol of the steering gear, and the aeroplane turned upside down. The belt round his waist happened to be loose, and the jerk of the turn almost threw him out of the machine, but he saved himself by clutching hold of the rear centre strut, the belt slipping down round his legs. While he hung thus, head downwards, making desperate efforts to disengage his legs, the aeroplane fell from a height of 8,000 feet to about 2,500, spinning round and round like a falling leaf. At last he managed to free his legs and reach the control lever with his feet. He then succeeded in righting the machine, which turned slowly over, completely 'looping the loop,' 'whereupon he slid back into his seat." Even the feat of looping the loop, which may be deprecated in the past, has been found of value in war time.

MOTOR TRADE IN AUSTRALIA.

The value of the motor-car chasis imported into the Commonwealth during 1914 amounted to £1,349,785, an increase of 2.16,235 over 1913. Although the figures for the last five months of the year shows a big falling off, the increase during the early part of the year was more than sufficient to compensate it. New South Wales is still at the head of the list-, showing an increase of about eight per cent. In 1913 the United Kingdom accounted for chassis to the value of 5:185,262 as against the United States with a total of 037,214. During 1914 there was a slight drop in the importation of chassis from the United Kingdom, but a big increase from America. This was partially accounted for. by the war, but at the same time there is no doubt that the demand for the cheap American car is rising rapidly. British manu- facturers should note this point.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPRECIATION IN TYRES.

A number of correspondents have written to me with reference to the •keeping quality of tyres. One and all are actuated by the same desire, namely, to save tyre cost. They realise that the price of. tyres is likely to rise in the near future, and they are considering the advisability of purchasing a complete set at present rates and storing them until required. Their trouble, however, is that they cannot figure out whether the depreciation of the stored tyres will be such as to counteract any saving they may effect in the original pur- chase price. I do not think they need have any fear on this point. A new tyre kept in its original wrapping will maintain its quality for two or three years—a good deal longer than the war is likely to last. This does not apply to the keeping qualities of tyres that have been used, however, for they lose value much more rapidly. Keep the new tyres in a dark dry place at as even a temperature as possible. say 55 degrees F to 60 degrees F, and they will be as good as new when required for use.

THE HUE.

GERMAN SUBMARINE SUNK.

Pirate Prisoners To Be Treated Like

Oilier Prisoners.

A German submarine has been sunk. Six officers and 21 men are prisoners in British hands. Mr. Balfour, the new First Lord of the Admiralty, made the announcement in the House of Commons. Mr. Balfour also an- nounced that submarine prisoners were not now treated otherwise than were ordinary German captives.: Attacks on defenceless vessels were very far from being the only violation of the laws of humanity of which the Germans had been guilty. The Government were therefore of the opinion that the submarine problem could not be treated in. isolation, and that the general question of personal responsibility could be reserved until the end of the war.

SOME SAD CASES CITED.

At the Monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, held on Tuesday evening, Mr. Edward Plinston presented his report as the Council's representative on the lotal Education Committee, and dealt most exhaustively with many of the points raised in the annual report of .the .Scheol Medical Officer on clinic work, emphasising the importance of the work and the excellent effeets which it is having, in many directions on the children of school age in the town. He referred to the overcrowding of certain schools, and deprecated the idea that because of the war it should be allowed to continue so that the scholars and teachers should be penalised, which idea, to his mind, savoured of re,d tape, because he thought the Government were well able, if they Wsere so minded, to find the requisite money for the war without hindering the most necessary work of education. (Hear, hear.) He contended that the work done by the clinic in no sense con- stituted an interference with the liberties and rights of the people inasmuch as its primary purpose was to improve their condition, and ;he pointed to the marvellous improvements which had been effected in the matter of the per- sonal cleanliness of the children. The sugges- tion to provide a special residential open-air school for tuberculous children had Mr. Plin- ston's whole-hearted approval and, in summing up on the report generally, he thought there was ample justification for the work of the school clinic in the fact that, since the educational system had turned its attention to the body as well as to the mind, the general stamina of the children of the town had much improved; indeed, was vastly superior to-day to what it was in the old days. (Hear, hear.)

STILL ROOM FOR GREAT IMPROVEMENT.

The Chairman (Mr. Hamblett) thought that as Labour men, the delegates should be pre- pared to do all they could to support the sug- gestion to provide a special and suitable school for the tuberculous children. The lot of the child was being greatly improved, but there was still room for great improvement. He men- tioned the case of a consitmptive child who had not seen a blade of grass for seven years. Yet the doctors told them that it was only fresh air, plus good plain' food, that was required to stamp out consumption. Another case which had come under his notice was that of a widow, who had two consumptive children, and who had to go out working from morning to night in order to keep herself and them, working, of course, as most women had to, for what was little more than a mere pittance. And yet in the face of cases like this, there. were men on Education Committees who still squabbled round the point as to whether or not the chil- dren should have fair treatment and fair chances in life. It was very sad.

"A Home" Not Fit For Pigs.

If the children he had in mind had been fairly treated by society when they were younger they would, probably, never have had the disease which. was now eating their young lives away. It was only because, deprived of a father to work for,them, they were compelled to be herded together in a place that was not fit to keep pigs.in, that they were suffering to-day. " What we want to do as workers," concluded the speaker, "is to see that this proposal to provide a special school for these helpless and handicapped children is not allowed to be shelved. That open-air school is wanted— badly wanted—and it is up to us to do our best to see that it is supplied." Mr. Dukes thought that the best thanks the Council could give to Ile. Plinston for his work on the Education Committee was to pass a resolution that would assist him in the task he had undertaken—a resolution call- ing on the Education Committee to ,press on with the matter of the provision Of a school specially adapted for tuberculous children. The dread disease of consumption was, at root, a poverty disease. It was eventually decided to further discuss the matter at a future meeting, with a view to appointing a committee to offer assistance to the School Medical Officer in his efforts to get special school proyision for consumptive chil- dren.

OUR WAR LOSSES.

Quarter Of A Million To End Of May.

Mr. Asquith, in the House of COmmons on Wednesday, said the British losses from the beginning -of war until May 31 in France and Belgium and the Mediterranean were 258,059. These figures included Regulars, Territorials. Colonials, and Indians. The kaled numbered 50,300, and the missing nearly 54,000. More than half the casualties have, it would seem, been suffered since the beginning of February, for on the Bth of that month the Prime Minister disclosed an official return which put the total at 104,000.

BRITISH RED GROSS SOCIETY.

The Great Work Being %Done By

The Cheshire Branch.

In common with all other branches of the British Red Cross Society, the work of the Cheshire branch has increased since the out- break of war in August of last year by leaps' and bounds, until, at the present time, there is. hardly a village in the whole county which is not, in some way or another, interested in the work of helping the sick and wounded in the war.

Besides the very large' quantities of clothing and comforts supplied to the many Auxiliary Home Hospitals in the county, or sent up to the main depot in London, two additional depots have been formed, one. at 60, Sackville- street; Manchester, and one at St. Michael's- row, Bridge-street, 'Chester. both of which have done' excellent work. Some Of - the smaller places, where no hospital has been formeo, have been particularly conspicuous in this essential part of. the society's work. The Red Cross organisation has been of the very greatest assistance to the War Office and the country generally, and its main work in Cheshire has consisted in the prevision of three things which are, above all, essential to the proper treatment and comfort of the wounded —transport, hospital accommodation, and last, but perhaps most important of all, trained or partially-trained nursing help. The County of Chester is adjacent to two large military, hospitals, one being the Ist Western General Hospital at Fazakerley, Liv- erpool,.and the other the 2nd Western General Hospital at Whitworth-street, Manchester, and of the 57 auxiliary home hospitals in the county,- 15 are situated in the western half and are affiliated to the General Hospital at Liver- pool, and 42, situated in • the eastern half, to the General Hospital at Manchester.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
Motor Transport Provided.

It may not be generally known that the whole of the motor transport ,service in the Manchester area, which consists in the convey- ance of the sick and wounded from the railway stations to the General Hospitals, and from the General Hospitals to the various Auxiliary Hospitals and Convalescent Homes, is carried out entirely by the Cheshire Branch of the society, acting in conjunction with the East Lancashire Branch. The whole of this trans- port service Is, of course, given voluntarily, and from the eastern part of Cheshire alone some 150 cars and 20 ambulances are continuously engaged, often day and night, in this arduous and essential work. In the western half of the county very valuable assistance is also ren- dered in the same way, and it is not saying too much to state that without the help of the Red Cross the large military hospitals would have been gravely handicapped in the work which has been so admirably carried out by them in the treatment of our wounded soldiers.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
Beds and Nurses.

Since the commencement of the war 57 auxi- liary home hospitals, providing accommodation for about 1,400 beds, have been offered through the Cheshire Branch and accepted by the Military Authorities, and it is to these hospitals that the men are sent as soon as they can safely be moved from the General Hospitals. .According to the last return issued by the headquarters of the society, 4,252 beds have been provided by the society in the Western Command, which stretches from Carlisle to Cardiff, so that it will be seen that Cheshire has not been backward in this important respect.

Not long after the beginning of the war it became clear that the existing slimily of trained nursing help would have to be supple- mented, and an arrangement was made by the War Office with the society. that any member of, a Women's Voluntary Aid Detachment who is between 23 and 38 years of age and possesses the preliminary certificates in both first-aid and home nursing, could volunteer for special service either at home or abroad. If accepted, such member is sent to a military hospital for one month, on probation, and if approved is asked to sign on for special service for a fur- ther period of six, months. Cheshire has already sent forward over 100 names for this purpose, and it is hoped that many more will be available. The need is already very urgent and will increase as time goes on, but it should be borne in mind that candidates cannot be accepted without going through, the preliminary training provided by the society's lectures and examinations.

This branch of thi3 society's work is, perhaps, at the present time, the most important of all, and fresh detachments are still- being formed. There are at present in the county 58 women's and 12 men's detachments, with a total mem- bership of well over 2,200.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====
Help Wanted.

The Cheshire Branch has recently published a small handbook, which may be obtained from the headquarters 'of the tranch, at 56, Mosley- street, Manchester, or from the publishers, Messrs. W. Pearce, Ltd., 52, Corporation-street, Manchester, at the price of 6d., or 7d. post free. A list of donations appears on another page,' together with an appeal for further cOntribu- tiovs, as it is felt that the excellent work being done by the branalt only needs to be More widely known in order to ensure a ready re- sponse, especially from those not directly con- nected with the society. The donations above referred to are, of course, exclusive of the large BUMS contributed locally towards the upkeep of the various hos- pitals and other Red Cross objects.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The House of Savoy.

The flag of Italy has now to be added to our own, and that of our Allies. The national colours are red, white and,green, and these are worn by the Italian children in London. The House of Savoy has a romantic history, and it is the oldest reigning dynasty it): Europe'. " Forward Savoy" is its motto, but Orders of Knighthood are few in Italy, and are bestowed with great reserve. The Military Order of Savoy, which corresponds to our V.C., was founded in 1815, but the Order of the-Annuncia- tion is the highest, and ranks with the British Order of the 'Garter, in dignity. The Queen of Italy is a daughter of the King of Montenegeo, and was.educated.in Russia. She is wholly in sympathy with Italy's action in declaring war on Austria. The Queen of Greece, who is a sister of the Kaiser, has declared, on the other hand, that she will leave her adopted country, if Greece joins the Allies.

An Eloquent Woman Speaker. One of the most eloquent and finished of the younger women speakers of to-day, is Miss Agnes Boyden, daughter of Sir Thomas Boyden, Bart., of Frankly Hall, Cheshire. Before the war, Miss Royden was a popular lecturer, under the Oxford University Extension Movement, and. since the outbreak of hostilities she has devoted her talents to philanthropic work. Not long ago she addressed a thousand sailors in the Crystal Palace on True Manliness, and is as successful in addressing an intellectual audience of a University city, as one of working class men and women.

The Clubs for Girls and Soldiers. Quite an innovation is the Evening Club for Soldiers and Girls; it is an Outcome of :the war, but meets a national want, and will probably remain when peace reigns once more. The idea originated with the National Union of Women Workers, who .organised and carried out their plans with such success, that they have received official sanction and full military and civil sup- port. The scheme is also being taken up by the

===== 0002601_19150612_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes • and Useful Suggestions.

Making Meat Tender. Meat roasted in the oven, to be tender and juiry, should, if very leap meat, be put in a tin of hot dripping and well basted, the oven, after the •first half hour, cooled down. In addition to being well basted, the joint should be turned over frequently and given the full quarter of an hour to each pound of meat. Too often both ,boiling and roasting joints are put on to cook' and just allowed the bare time to the dinner hour, and then extra heat is put on to save time. When boiling meat, let it Merely simmer, not bubble; this method ensures its being tender and full of its own juice.

Rolled Bread. The best bread to use for bread and batten sandwiches is milk-and-water bread, in which no shortening- has been used. This kind will roll more easily. As soon as the bread has been taken from -the oven, wrap the loaf in a cloth wrung out of cold water, then cover with several thicknesses of dry cloth, thereby sub- jecting the bread to a kind Of steaming process. Let it stand from two to five hours. Then remove the crusts, and eut in as thin slices as !possible, spreading each slice with butter cream, before cutting it from the loaf. Then roll the slice like a jelly roll. Cheese Omelette.

Beat the yolks of three eggs light, add two tablespoonfuls Of milk and season with salt and white pepper; fold in the whites of the eggs beaten to a stiff froth, and pour into a hot buttered frying pan. When ready to fold, spread thinly with grated cheese, fold, and serve at once on a hot platter. . Banbury Tarts.

Stone. and chop a heaping cupful of raisins together with a piece of citmn the size of an egg; add the juice of a lemon and the grated rind, a cupful of granulated sugar and an egg beaten very light. Make a good piecrust, roll out as thin as possible, and cut out round n portion About the size of a saucer; drop a table- spoonful of the mixture on one half, wet the edges, fold the other half over, put a fancy edge on with fork, prick and bake a light brown.
