Young Women's Christian Association. Clubs are now being started all over the country in thn neighbourhood of 'camps, and here men in khaki can meet and spend their leisure hours in the company of their girl friends, who act as hostesses. A hall, or part of a house is taken, and arrangements made for dancing,, amateur acting, concerts, games, &A% Refreshments are provided .oa very low terms, and everything is done to render the club 'homelike and attractive.

A Girl Call-Boy.; Every day women enter on occupations hitherto sacred to the sterner sex. Only recently a lady was appointed manager of a London music hall, and is filling the post with the greatest success. At the Shaftesbury Theatre, a girl has taken the place of call-boy, who has joined the Army as a trumpeter. There are no fewer than two thousand women employed as police patrols, and still more are needed. They wear a blue and white arm badge, and carry a card with their name and number. They always Walk in couples, and must bo twenty-five years of age, but are not eligible .if over fifty. Women are also distinguishing thelnselvcs as inventors just now, and a remarkable proof of feminine in- genuity, is a new non-skidding motor-cycle. Naturally the war has called forth numerous devices for the comfort of soldiers and sailors, for Which patents have been granted to' the women, whose fertile brains orginated the ideas.

Feminine Fashions in \War Time. It; is a curious fact that women's dress in- variably becomes more feminine during war time, except perhaps in Germany. Frills, flounces and furbelows; petticoats and full skirts, have all returned since August of last year. The military influence which was so apParent last autumn has now considerably diminished, and fashions for the summer are distinctly womanly. Freakish and startling modes have been eliminated completely. Woollen materials are much dearer than last year, in consequence of the demand for soldiers' uniforms, -consequently manufacturers turned their attention -last autumn to cottons—a British speciality—and the result is that the cotton frock will this year take a very,_promi- nent position in the world sartorial. Dress- makers and designers have expended on its per: 'faction, the art and genius formerly devoted to the richest and Ulost costly materials, and cot- ton frocks, althdugh high in price, are to be

Modern Mourning. AS a result of the war so many young women, more especially the wives of officers, have been widowed, that .in their case the usual heavy style of 'mourning has been considerably lightened. Individuality has asserted itself, and many old-fashioned habiliments of woe, ,have vanished, possibly for •ever. Some of the 'most youthful of widows, wear the traditional white weeds, but very considerably modified, and in every case the cap forms a dainty and becoming headdress. Others wear neither cap nor veil. Considerable licence is allowed in the matter of collars, whilst the V-shaped decollet- age is permitted When a band of dull narrow black ribbon encircles the .throat. This band is often used to support a pretty white up and dowa collar, hat it is always worn high. Dull corded silks, black Georgette, and black crepe .de chine, are used for day had for 'evening wear. Lace of a filmy shadowy kind, is largely employed .for lighter mourning, and chantilly is preferred, where expense is not the first con- sideration.

Where Love Is Serious.

. Women in France are supreme, says Mme. De Perrott. They hold the purse strings. Often they assist their husbands in their business or profession. Their .practical sense and ability are respected. Their beauty and their talent for making themselves attractive ,are univer- sally admired. A Charming, well-dressed woman in Paris is looked at by everybody. Why do the men sit outside the cafes? To see the elegant toilets and the dainty\ figures and the pretty faces which pass. English women find this unpleasSnt, but the French woman looks for admiration. She would .be disap- pointed—and quite rightly—if she did not re- ceive it.

For the greatest power of a woman is ,to be beautiful. Yes, that has always been so. For a woman to be forced to work in competition with men is pitiful. • To 'a French woman it comes natural to be pleasant, amiable; to seek to put people at their ease and make them happy. It cemes natural to her to cook and to manage a ho. It comes natural to her te watch over• her children. And the reason? It is because she in turn has been watched over and taught and cherished by her own mother. No one in France Would jest about love. It is sacred, beautiful, the one reality of life.

Fashionable Summer Boots.

Dressmakers and designers insist that boots, and high ones too, must be worn with the new short skirts, and bootmakers ,have come to the rescue with novelties suitabl4 for wearing dur- ing the hot months. For wearing with the white dresses that are to have such a vogue this summer, boots are shown made of white linen and white canvas, and many women order their footgear of the same material as their summer gowns, when these are of linen, cotton, corduroy or any of the firm cotton fabries. High boots of thin beige cloth are made for wearing with navy serge suits, unless serge is used. In either case the toecaps are of patent leather. The heels of all fancy boots, when of the LOuis type, are now covered with cloth, &c., to match the uppers, instead of being made as heretofore of leather. Dark 'blue leather is employed also for high boots, but only sparingly, for all kinds of leather are veily high in price. The fashion of lacing high boots on the inside seam is not a commendable one, for it does not improve the shape of the foot. ' •

ON HEALTH.

1 send Information In Popular Language.

Flies and Typhoid. • It is interesting to note that the typhoid season corresponds very closely with the Hy season and that the height of the typhoid fever curve comes in early autumn, usually in Sep- tember or October. Good For The Teeth.

Eat hard food if you wish to keep the teeth clean and. preserve them is the advice of an eminent English dentist. He says the best of all bread was that provided in the prisons which was very hard. Mothers who had the interests of their children's teeth at heart should select a good crusty loaf made from "seconds" flour, and not from the white roller milled. The crusts should be cut off and given to the children to eat. .

===== 0002601_19150612_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
Too Many Meals.

Most peoplo eat too frequently,- taking four or five or even six meals in the day. Three are ample, and there is little doubt but that the vast majority of people w.uld find two moals.a day still more satisfactory from the stan:lpeint cf• health and efficiency TU.r.l should be an in- terval of • about five hours betwom meals so as to give the digestive organs ample dportunity to do their work and have a brief rest between meals. This is particularly 'true of the stomach, which should not be kept in a state of constant activity and dilatation during all the waking hours and part of the time allotted to sleep. It is well to make breakfast a fairly substantial meal, and, if possible,. it should be taken sufficiently early so that it is not necessary to rise from the table and rush off to work or to the tram or train. As far as possible the main meal of the day should be taken • between one and twb, and the third and last meal should be taken comparatively early, about six or seven. Supper should be the lightest meal of the day, and may consist of fresh or stewed fruit, with wholemeal bread and butter, with an added hot nourishing drink in' winter time if necessary. Drinking freely with Meals should be avoided. No provision has been made f r tea, which is both•an unnecessary and an unwholesome func- tion as far as health is concerned. If it be- comes desirable ta offer refreshments to friends calling in the afternoon, we would recommend fruit drinks, such as freshly prepared lemonade or orangeade, unfermented grape wine or fresh grapes, oranges or other juicy fruit.—Doctor Olsen.

Vicar Of St. Ann's Appeal.

In the current issue of his Parish Magazine, the Vicar of St. Anifs (the Rev. James Davies), under the heading " Pollow the King !—an ,eal," writes:—" At the recent Winwick Ruri-decanal Conference, held on Wednesday, May 19th, the following resolution was agreed to 'That in the opinion of this, meeting, every Churchman should be an abstainer during the war.' Some we know preceded the King—others we trust will respond to the appeal. . Yet another appeal. Let everyone do his or her very best—to help our sailors and soldiers to follow the King. Let not their 'homecoming be a backward, step. Let them return from their furlough strongly impressed and deeply in- spired, that we at home are thinking seriously, and acting soberly, that' is with self-restraint in pleasures and luxuries, and ready to forego anything if by so doing we can contribute some- thing to the overthrow of the enemy\ and to the hastening of a righteous peace, which we pray God may speedily come."

===== 0002601_19150612_art0271.txt | included=true | included =====
Rev. James Davies And An Idea IA (' Must Get Rid M.

Writing in the current issue of the St. Ann's Parish Magazine, the Vicar (the 'Rev. James Davies) says :—" All need to get rid of the idea that the war has not reached us. It has; but the majority, the large majority, have not gripped the fact. Even to those who have, it has not as yet burnt into our souls as it should and ought, yet and must if we are to ,do bur part in saying and ending this awful conflict. The war is not far off. The war is here and now. We see it and feel it The loaded ambu- lances of 'wounded men which pass and " pass again—they are of the war; the crippled and invalided from the. Front whom we see about the streets, they are, of the war; the _bereaved and mourning families—of men, women and children, bereaved of loved ones killed, drowned or lost—they are of the war. Other happenings of daily life are ,evidences that the war has reached us and is with us, but thank the Lord and let .us praise Him for His mercy, it has not reached us in its aWfulness as it has so many other parts of the world. Must we wait till the enemy is, within our borders, at our doors, with- in our homes, before we are roused to the danger, the need, our duty? Must we wait till we hear .the enemies' inhuman shout, see and feel their brutal deeds, before we are roused to action? Must we wait to have our homes brought down upon our heads and to witness, the terrible carnage of shell: and shot and sword, before we fall to penitence and prayer? This war calls for, claims, demands, without stint , our material, physical and spiritual help. Thank , God it has received from the very many un- stinted sacrifice and service.'

===== 0002601_19150612_art0272.txt | included=true | included =====
THINGS THAT ARE UNSEEN.

Materialism One Of The Perils Of The Present Day.

A contrast between the things that are seen and temporal and the things that are unseen and eternal was drawn at Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday evening, by the Rev. T. Walton Meredith (of Burnley), who uttered an impeachment of the materialistic tendency of the age. t The occasion was the anniversary of the Sunday school, which, was celebrated by the holding of three special services, the. Rev. T. W. Meredith 'preaching morning and even- ing, and the Rev. Wilfrid L. Hannam (of Man- chester) delivering an address at the afternoon flower service, The tuneful singing of the choir and scholars was .a feature of the day. In the afternoon, when Mr. Hedley IL Taylor presided, there Were excellent violin solos by Mr. Bradford Barton. The Rev. T. W. Meredith, in the course, of his evening sermon, pointed out that there were twO distinct kinds of knowledge. There was the kind that one could prove and the kind that could not be proved. The first was as naught beside the 'second. The one was of the world; the other was of God. Knowledgefof the former kind was constantly spreading and expanding; but, spread and expand as it might, if the other knowledge, the intuitive knowledge were lack- ing in a man, his education left . him. poor. Temporal knowledge could show how God had done things; but it could not show GOd. For that, religion was required—a revelation of the amazing. mystery- of the Glorious, Creator, who had sown the sky with stars.. -A man' might think lie knew all about the World, but he would still be •up against a .great mystery in the at- tempt to solve which science would not help him. There were, unfortunately, many people who Could think only in terms of things that could be measured. But .the best side -of human life was outside all Measurement. A house could be measured by cubits, hut not the home. There was a tendency *to try. to measure everything by superficial standards; it was one of the Perils of the time.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0273.txt | included=true | included =====
AIEN Olt METAL?

Shells And More Shells Will Save The

iSoldler.

" Men or Metal?" was the title of a sermon preached at Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday by the Rev. H. Ogden, who de- fended the workers against aspersions of slackness, but contended that a guiding power was necessary to effect a multiplication and re-multiplication of the output of shells, in a large supply of which lay the salvation of many a gallant soldier's life. Metal, not men, was the urgent need of the moment.

The text of the discourse was: "And it came to pass from that time forth that the half of my servants wrought in the work, and the 'other half of them held both the spears, the shields, and the bows, and the habergeons" (Nehemiah iv. 16). When Nehemiah, said the preacher, commenced to rebuild the walls of Jerusalem, his 'enemies gathered around him, and in order to complete his task, half of his men had to stand ready with their -spears, prepared for ay attack that might be made, while the other half went on with the work of re-building.

Britain's Justification.

That seemed to be an ancient setting for the modern cartoon, "We're both needed," in which a munition :worker and a soldier were seen shak- ing hands. One could not emphasise too • much what was uttered by one of our great statesmen, viz., that we were the worst organised nation in the world for war. Many honest men had been concerned about the responsibility for. this war. Even British 'diplomacy was not above suspicion. Diplomats had played with fire until the whole nation had been burned. Diplomacy had followed its usual course of backstairs intrigue. • Bilt, • however much diplomacy might be to blame, it was quite patent that our nation was. not organised for war. And truly had it been sAid that when the history of the great struggle came to be written, one of Britain's justifications of their part in the war would be the .fact of her un- preparedness. The menace to democracy could never be forgotten. It had been asked whether ,the average man would be any worse off under German rule than under British. One had but to turn to. the treatment of the leader of the Social Democrats in Germany at this time to find What would be the treatment of alien demo- crats. If Prussian Socialists were refused their franchise now, what would happen to 'British democracy, about which some Germans daily prayed "God mulish England," if it came under Prussian rule? The elementary duty of every than, rich or poor, was to do his best.'

AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

First Round Of Club Championship.

The return matches in the first round of the club championship contest in connection with the Warrington and District Amateur Bowling Association, and which were played on Satur- day, produced some interestiTlg results and one extremely close ,finish, viz., the game between Bylands's No. 1 and the Stag Hotel, victory ultimately resting with the Hotel combination from Walton by the narrow margin or two points. Details and results of the various matches will be found below.

The War Fund Handicap.

LAST SATURDAY'S WINNERS AND TO-DAY'S COMPETITORS.

Further progress was made on Saturday after- noon with the monster War Fund Handicap managed by the Warrington Works' Bowling Association, for which there was a most en- couraging entry and for prowess in which a number of tempting prizes are on offer. Sat- urday's games were played off on the splendid green at the association's headquarters—the Horse and Jockey, Winwick-road—and they evoked even greater interest than that aroused the previous Saturday, the weather being splen- did and the attendance of trundlers ditto.

Over.a hundred competitors met in friendly rivalry on Saturday:and the following qualified to proceed into the next round :—J. Timimns, Monks, Hall; J. Barnard, Naylor's; W. Wright. Walker's Brewery; H. Deakin, Monks, Hall; James Smith, Walker's Brewery; A. Twiss, Grappenhall Tannery; J. Bird, Firth Mattress Company; J. Dagnall, Walker's Brewery; J. Cooper, County Asylum; 3. RESIDENTIAL QUALIFICATION.

An Interesting Point Raised.

An interesting, point has reached me (sys "The Striker "' in the "Daily Dispatch '') on the 'question of residential qualification; and while, as I have said before', it is impossible to restrict any bowler tO a stated area, the com- plaint under notice ,raises a point certainly worthy of attention. It arose out of the game between Roebuck and ]Occleston, in the Lanca- shire championship third round ties, played over the green of the Warrington Bull's Read. Without wiSting to make any reflections, I may point out that the Roebuck team, one of the finest in the county, is most cosmopatan in its personnel, and includes two Warrington men, who know the Bull's Head green particu- larly *ell. Obviously those two held an unmis- takable advantage, and as they won respec- tively by 21--,9 and 217-8, a total of 25 points, whilst the Roebuck's final margin was fOur Points less, the cause of the complaint is obvious.

I am not suggesting that the green was chosen with deliberate intent to secure a "pull" —that could not be, so lere the whole of the delegates 'of the cOuntr, have. to approve the selection—but the fact remains that the win- ners, unwittingly, were given an advantage that turned the game in their favour, and for the future special precautions should be taken to avoid such happenings.

COUNTY AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

One Warrington Team Successful;

Other Beaten.

The two Warrington Cemetery Hotel teams were engaged in Lancashire County Amateur Competitions on Saturday. In the second round of the Association Competition the Cemetery first team• were pitted against the Leigh Rail- waymen, the match being played on the green of the Wigan Ltd. Club. The Leigh combina- tion proved too good for the Warringtonians, and ran out victorious by 51 points, claiming eight of the twelve sets. Three of the Cemetery men let their side down rather badly. WALKING DAY.

TRADES COUNCIL OPINION

Not Sought—But In Favour Of

Holiday.

At a meeting of the Warrinton Trades and Labour Council, held on Tues4ay .%,v ening, it was decided to write to the Mayor (Alderman Peter, Peacock) asking why that body, repre- senting practically the whole of the organised labour of the town, had not received an invita- tion to be represented at the talked-about con- ference with reference to Walking Day. The Secretary (Mr. Charles Dukes) said the delegates had no doubt seen from the local Press that some sort of d‘ciiion- had been ar- rived at that Walking Day was this year to be abandoned, or only to be observed in some very much emasculated form. But that Council had heard nothing from, the Mayor; and, in his (the speaker's) view he. did not think they had been treated in a manner which he might describe as 18-carat. (Hear, hear.) They were written to and promised' a conference on the subject, but they had heard nothing further of the matter and had not been afforded an opportunity of putting forward their views as representing the thousands of organised workers in the town. They went to the trouble of taking a ballot of the various trade unions, and sent the Town Clerk all the facts and figures relating to the same. Having done that much they had a right to expect that the projected conference should have been held, or that they should have been furnished with some adequate explana- tion as to why such conference had, been aban- doned. For himself, he always felt that the workers had too little holiday time, and that what little leisure they did. have did not come frequently enough. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) They had been told by the working men them- s,elves, who ought to know, that the holding of the Walking Day holiday, as usual, Would not interfere in the slightest, locally. with the work of producing munitions of war. If that.were so, he thought some explanation was due to the workers as to why they were not to be granted their holiday as usual.. The least that could have been. dolie was, to have let the Council know whether the conference on the subject was going to be;,held or not. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman (Mr. Hamblett) : We feel that the manner in which we have been treated is a direct insult, and one against which I pro- test yery, very strongly, in the name of this Council and the many workers who it repre- sents. It shows the spirit of those at the head of affairs. If it suits their purpose they just treat us off-handedly; if they -want us to do something for them they come to us cap in hand. If some people do not know what they are, we do; at least, we know what dignity and courtesy are.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0305.txt | included=true | included =====
PROFESSIONAL FOOTBALL POSTPONED.

The Northern Union's Patriotic

Proposal.

DECISION WORTHY OF THE GAME.

The question—momentous so far as all those who interest themselves in the game, and have heard the call for more men, is concerned— whether League football should be resumed "as usual" next season; or whether, in view of the need of the nation for the services of all able- bodied men, it should be suspended during the continuance of the war, came up for decision at the annual meeting of the Northern Rugby Football Union, held in Manchester on Tuesday. The result of their confabulations was awaited with much concern by the many thousands of supporters of the handling code, and now that the committee's decision in favour of discon- tinuance of competitive football is made known, the spirit of patriotism, which has always permeated players and spectators alike, will show, itself in a ready and a whole-hearted 'acquiescence by all concerned. It is quite true that there are many croakers and kill-joys who have failed utterly to discern any such trait as patriotism in, according to their expressed opinion, the irresponsible crowds of working men who waste their time in watching professional athletes jostling each other in a mad scramble to gain possession of a ball, "misled," according to the Poet Laureate, "by the small body of men who cater for them." Such critics of the bread-and-milk school have never been able to understand the desire of workers for recreation which did not fit in with their own ideas. They have taken every conceivable opportunity to denounce and, vilify professional players and those who have found pleasure in watching them play. And, latterly, have gone so far as to challenge their inte,lligence nnd patriotism. lam not an anabaptist, but I should like to see them immersed in the nearest duckpond, or given an antesthetie until the war is over. Probably. the action of the "small body of men" will convince them once and for all that their own little minds do not contain all the intelligence. and that football enthusiasts are not, and never have been, lacking in loyalty.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0306.txt | included=true | included =====
ANSWERING THE CALL.

• We have known all along what the answer would be. Last season, the opinion of the War Office was sought as to whether it was deemed advisable to discontinue the game, and although no precise instructions were received yet it was generally understood that, for the moment at any rate, there was no reason why the game should be interrupted. It is quite true that some of us were of opinion that it might be postponed, fearing that it would interfere with recruiting, but, in face of actual results, we were content to continue until the end of the season, althongh it meant a depletion of the elub's finances. But now, after the clear demand made by the Minister of Munitions for national .service, it is no longer a matter for conjecture. but a clear call for the abandon-

ment of all sPlfish interests and a whole-hearted resnonse is the result. Thousands of players and spectators have already donned the khaki, but the nation needs the services of still more men—nay, every man is called upon lo do his little bit.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0307.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR-TIME SUSPENSION.

The resolution which Mr. J. 11. Smith, the chairman of the Union moved, was as follows: , That except for schoolboys and inter- mediates under 19 years of age, competition football under Northern Union rules be sus- pended for the duration of the war.

.' Is there a single person present," he asked, "who can honestly say that he got any Satis- faction at all out of football last season? There may have been some distraction from ;the national trouble, if you like, but there certainly was no genuine pleasure or excitement to be obtained from it."

Mr. J. B. Cooke (chairman of the League), in seconding the proposition, asked whether they could seriously feel at home, at a time like this, when men were giving their lives for their country in continuing their gams as they did before the war. broke out.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0308.txt | included=true | included =====
JUNIORS ENCOURAGED.

Mr. Walkden (Wigan) said his club were opposed to the continuance of professional foot- ball during the war, but they thought schools and junior football should be encouraged, with the limit 18 years of age instead of 19, and he moved an amendment to that effect, which I seconded.

Mr. H. Lodge (Huddersfield), while agreeing with the remarks of the previous speakers, could not see that, any great harm would be done by deferring a decision. Football was a Saturday afternoon game, and persons engaged in making munitions of war and kindred work should have the opportunity either of playing or watching football. His amendment was not supported.

Mr. J. Lewthwaite (Hunslet) pointed out that one advantage of taking a decision on the whole question now was that clubs might proceed at once to curtail their financial responsibilities. The resolution was carried by 24 votes to 3, the, age being reduced from 19 to 18 years. A further resolution was adopted, on the motion of Mr. Watson (Rochdale Hornets), seconded by myself, to the effect that no form of payment for football playing and no regis- tration of football players should be permitted during the continuance of the war. It was explained that friendly programmes might be arranged at the discretion of clubs, but that no payment, or allowance for broken time, would be permitted. I pointed out that my club had already decided that they would not be parties to professionalism, even if it was decided to go on with the game. The remarks made at our annual meeting were prophetic. E. 0.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0309.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE.

The annual meeting of the Northern Rugby 'Union League was held the same afternoon, Mr. J. B. Cooke presiding. The Chairman presented the report of the League Management Committee for the year, which showed a balance in hand of .2107 13s. 7d. as compared with .277 9s. 10d. at the end of the Previous season. The final tie between the Huddersfield and Leeds Clubs had yielded a sum of .2757 58. 3d. The relief fund estab- lished in aid of clubs who suffered through the war amounted to .21,967 Bs. 2d. of Which' 021,000 was contributed by the Northern Union, .2764 12s. 4d. from various clubs, being percentage from gates; and contributions from players, .2185 9s. id. On this acCount there was a balance in hand of £776 7s. 4d.

The election of members to the Management ComAittee resulted in the following being chosen :—Lancashire—Messrs. Platt, Counsell, Houghton and Kennedy. Yorkshire—Messrs. Wood, Wilson, Foster and Cooke.

On the proposal of Mr. Counsell (Wigan), seconded by Mr. Foster (Halifax), a resolution was adopted varying the basis on which the list of referees will in future be drawn up. The official list of referees will now be compiled by the League Management Committee and not on the nomination of clubs, and all referees for League matches will be appointed by the com- mittee from such, list. The committee will have power to add to the list during the season at their discretion.

At a subsequent meeting of the committee of the League the following officers were appointed for the year: Mr. J. B. Cooke, president; Mr. J. Platt, vice-president; Mr. J. Wilson, hon. treasurer; and Mr. J. H. Houghton, hon. secretary.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0310.txt | included=true | included =====
CUP 11 INNERS' LOSSES

Sheffield United, the winners -of the Football Association challenge cup, lost ,£9OO on last season, but that amount includes ,E 307 paid to war and League relief funds. The whole loss is £2,050.

The cricket ground expenses at Bramhall Lane amounted to £3,349, of which £1,944 was for repairs, football wages absorbed £5,287, and bonuses for the cup victory £305, whilst the net proportion of cup receipts was £644.

SHOWROOMS-51, 53 & 55, BRIDGE ST., WARRINGTON. 'PHONE No. 79. 'PHONE No. 79.

WALKING DAY.

Should We Make The Most Of The Shorn Festival?

===== 0002601_19150612_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHILDREN'S BIRTH- RIGHT.

Official Church opinions as to the best course to follow in the matter of Walking Day differ. The Nonconformist and Church of England schools are compromising, but the Roman Catholics are acting drastically and abandoning the whole idea of a children's treat.

IS A WET BLANKET REQUIRED?

The Free Churches, instead of following the usual custom, are taking their children to fields in the district on the Saturday after- noon, instead of the regular Friday, and giving them a good time till dusk. This is generally understood to be the course the schools are expected to follow, but the plans of different schools may vary in detail. The Rev. H. Ogden (secretary of the Free Church Council), whom we interviewed on the subject, on Wed- nesday, urged at the Town Hall meeting the adoption of a plan whereby the whole of the children, of all denominations, should assemble and sing hymns outside the Town Hall. This idea, however, did not find general favour and was dropped. Mr. Ogden, and, indeed, most of the Nonconformists, are opposed to the idea of concentrating on impressing on the children, as well as the adult section, the gravity of the time, holding that a ) go. '1 end would be served by thus flinging a wet blanket over the Ai hole of the proceedings. Many of the children, it is contended—rightly, we think—have very few special pleasures to look forward to, and it would be wrong to curtail the already too brief list of their occasions of joy, on no other pre- text but that of wishing, with mistaken sin- cerity, to inculcate a seriousness that could only be superficial in the large majority of cases, and that would do no good to anyone, even in the minority of instances in which it would be of a more genuine, or less unnatural, character.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0315.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPRESSING YOUNG HEARTS.

It is to be said, in- fairness to a section of the Anglicans, that the Nonconformists are not the only people who take the view that this year's Walking Day festivities, such as they will be, should not be made the medium whereby efforts might be made to instil awe and melancholy into young hearts. In fact,we be- lieve that quite a large percentage of Churchmen tkike what is largely the Nonconformist atti- tude on the subject. But at the meeting, held some weeks ago, at the Patten Hall, of mini- sters and Sunday school superintendents of the Anglican denomination the suggestion that it was necessary to subdue both children and adults by a united display of funereal solemnity met with more support than we should have imagined it would. Still, we have reason to believe that there were many at that meet- ing who, though they did not actually speak against the suggestion, felt that the attitude it represented was hardly becoming and was cer- tainly lacking in insight.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0316.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT "TOMMY" IN THE TRENCH THINKS.

We liked the remark made at the meeting in the Patten Hall to the effect that the War- rington men in the trenches would remember Walking Day and look with the mind's far- seeing eye. over the war-marred plains of Flanders and across the waters towards the tight little isle they love, and picture with pride the children enjoying the festival as usual. "Tommy," the speaker truthfully pointed out, would be the last to wish for any curtailment of what one of them, in a letter we publish this week,- describes as "the kiddies' fun." The writer of the letter, a Stockton Heath youth, turns his good wishes to practical account, and, with a spirit that does one's heart gocid, sends home five days' pay for the purchase of delectables for the bairns. This is not an isolated case. It is fairly typical. It silences the guns of those who say: "Let us solemnise." Solemnity is a virtue we should be the last to deride—when kept in its right place; outside its legitimate sphere it becomes other than a virtuous quality, and it is outside its sphere when it interferes with the spontaneous gaiety that is the birthright of the child from the slum and the child from the lap of luxury.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0317.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE WE TOO FRIVOLOUS?

The-argument that we are a nation of frivols, incapable of taking anything seriously, is effete. The people of Warfington do not need to wit- ness a long procession of Perplexed children and glum-faced Sunday school workers to con- vince them that the nation is at war. Those who talk like that insult the mothers and the wives, the sweethearts and the sisters of the men who are hizarding their lives on land and sea, of who, maybe, have passed into the Beyond. They forget the mental agony; they forget the nights of sleeplessness; what they see is a smile in the morning, when the s,un is shining, and imparting hope as nothing else 'tan do like the sunshine. This they see, but because there is a smile, must there be thought- less irresponsibility, callousness or apathy? Britons do not wear their real worries on their sleeves. Just as we are said to take our plea- sures sadly, so we are noted for taking our medicine as if we liked it.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0318.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROMAN CATHOLIC ATTITUDE.

The Roman Catholics are abandoning the whole idea of a children's treat. Instead of Walking Day, they are holding on Sunday. July 4th, special services of supplication for a, successful and speedy end to the war. The reason given for this course of action is triple: Firstly, there should be no opportunity given for interference with or interruption orthe war work in the town; secondly, the present is a time for economy on the part of parents; and, thirdly, the children, or at least the older chil- dren of twelve and upwards, need a salutary lesson that all is not as usual. However, though the Catholic Children are to be disappointed, the majority of the juveniles of the town will be given a certain amount of pleasure, of which they will assuredly, with characteristic phil- osophy, make the utmost. The Church of Eng- land schools, as well as the Nonconformists, will hold field treats. Some of them may also have parish processions on Sunday. July 4th, but no united procession will take place.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0319.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR WORK ARGUMENT.

One of the most powerful arguments used against the bolding of the regular Walking Day this year was that the men would not work while the children played, and that work might be delayed. We under- stand that a stoppage is to be made to effect repairs, if not to take stock, as in previous years. One of the largest works in town is to be closed for a week, including the orthodox Walking Day Friday, and doubtless other works will do the same, so that reason for abandon- ing the great festival has gone by the board.

FAIRGROUND ORG AN.

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me a space in your paper to make an appeal to the repre- sentatives of Town Hall Ward to unite their efforts to stop the organ playing on the fair ground after 10 o'clock on Saturday nights? I am the last man in the world to interfere with anyone's amusements, but I do think, along with other residents in this neighbourhood, that to allow this organ to keep on until midnight on Saturday is a nuisance. ONE WHO LIVES IN THE NEIGHBOURHOOD.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0321.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPPORT THE COALITION

GOVERNMENT.

Sir,—l have read the letter signed " Britisher " in your issue of the sth, with peculiar interest and some appreciation. In- dividually, I too am glad Mr. Redmond did not join the Coalition Cabinet and, uninfluenced by either ambition or selfishness, remains con- sistent to the pledges and policy of a lifetime: But I don't think he will thereby be eubject to very much misrepresentation by the great majority of the British public. I trust also we shall 'not at this moment be led into any controversial examination of possible political gain or loss, by the inclusion or exclusion of this or that person, from the Government or otherwise.

I have a great and abiding faith in the generosity, honour and common sense of the British people, and I also believe that the greatest barrier to the realisation of Home Rule in the past has been suspicion. The grave crisis we are now passing through is purifying our thoughts and feelings towards each other, and eliminating a lot of the bitterness of the past. When in the near future, Great Britain, as I hope, will have had its voice in the dictation of the terms of peace, and we have settled down to re-adjust our domestic legisla- tion, the Home Rule question will be viewed and dealt with, influenced by feelings far different from those that have existed in the past. lam hoping there will be no pretention sio of glorification and victory for the one party, or humiliation or defeat for another, but a common agreement, satisfactory to both. May I quote Mr. T. P. O'Connor, M.P. ("Great Deeds of the Great War," June .Sth)?: "As to the new Government, it is our duty as citizens, and as patriots, to extend to them our most loyal support. It is not the least con- cern to me, that among their ranks there are men with whom I have been at political vari- ance all my life. I recognise no political differ- ence in this hour of my country's peril. If a political opponent will help my country to victory then I will applaud and support him. If he can do his job better than one of my political friends, then I am for him, and against my political friend. We have some- thing bigger, greater, nobler, to fight for to-day than even the most momentous questions of our domestic life. How poor and insignificant do most of these controversies of the past appear, when they stand under the shadow of our pre- sent struggle." This is the spirit which I think should animate us at the present nioment, and in War- rington some of the sermons of the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) and the speech of Mr. E. A. White, at the Junior Conservative Club. indi- cate that they too, as representing a line of thought and opinion, ofttimes differing from our own, are animated by the same lofty and patriotic spirit breathed in the above quota- tion.

PATRICK O'BRIEN.

125. Wilderspool-causeway.

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE —continued.

Sir Frank Boy‘den, Bart.

Cyclists throughout the length atid,breadth of the Empire will welcome the name of Sir Frank Bowden, Bart., head of the Raleigh Co., among the birthday honours, for probably, no man has done so much to place the British bicycle in the vanguard of the world. Perhaps as much of this as anything is due to the fact that Sir Frank is still to-day the keenest of cyclists. The Raleigh Cycle Co., Ltd., is the largest cycle manufacturing firm in the world, employing 1,700 people, and turning out, before the war, over 60,000 high-grade bicycles per annum.

STOCKTON H -MATH

Prcceeds of a Sale.—As the result of the sale of the fire box, the Surveyor has handed the Clerk of the Parochial Committee £2 2s. The fact was reported by the Clerk at the monthly meeting of the committee, at the Council Schools, on Tuesday evening.

Dearer Lighting?—A letter from the War- rington Corporation, giving twelve months' notice of their intention to terminate their street-lighting contracts, was read at Tuesday evening's meeting of the Parochial Committee. —The. Clerk said it presumably meant an in- Crease in prices.

The Parochial Committee.—Mr. Geo. Pilling (chairman) presided at Tuesday's meeting of the Parochial Committee, and there were also present: Messrs. J. H. Clarke, F. Warburton, H. J. Westbrook, J. MeDermid, J. Whitley, F. Pearson, A. Bottomley and J. Hallows, together with the Clerk (Mr. W. Hough) and the Ser- ver:ix. (Mr. Stanley Wright). New Street and Building Bylaws.—The Local Government Board asked the Parochial Com- mittee, in a letter that was read at Tuesday's meeting of the committee at the Council' School, whether they were prepared to adopt the new street and building bylaws in place of the old ones.--,-The Clerk said he had written, informing the Local Government Board that the matter was in the hands of the committee.

Dearer Pails and Ashbins.—Your pails and ashbins will cost you more! Mr. Pearson re- ported at Tuesday evening's meeting of the Parochial Committee, held at the Council Schools, that the Sanitary Committee (of which he is the chairman) had decided to pur- chase four, dozen pails and a like number of ashbins. The tender accepted was that of Messrs. J. and P. Round, of Stourbridge, whose first quotations of 9s. and 10s. were subsequently raised to 10s. ,and us. The prices of another firm who sent in a tender rose thirty per cent. The increases were due to the continued rapid advance in the prices of raw materials.

No News of a Member of Goliath's Crew.— No further news has come to hand of the son of Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Gerrard, of 16, Bed- ford-street, Stockton Heath, who was reported to have gone down with H.M.S. Goliath in the Mediterranean. The son, Arthur Owen Gerrard, who receivesl his early educa- tion at St Thomas' School, Stockton Heath, and was subsequently employed in the Warrington Post Office, was thirty-one years of age. Two of his brothers are in the services, one in the Navy and the other in the Army. As he was known to be .a very strong swimmer, there is just a possibility of him being still alive.

Equestrian Intelligence?—rnred Faben, of 9, Portland-street, Warrington, delivered some coal at a house in. Whitefield-road on the 18th of last. Month. He had twenty .sacks to empty at the house in question. Now whether the noble quadruped between the shafts 'listened carefully, and counted the number of journeys made by .the coalman between the house and the cart is a matter for question,•but certain it is that when that worthy came out with the last empty sack, the horse was a hundred and. fifty yards on its way home. All -would have been well had, not P.C. Tudor, in the execution of his duty, taken the matter in hand, with the result that the unfortunate coat-deliverer became a defendant at the Daresbury Petty Sessions at Stockton Heath, on Tuesday, and had to pay a fine of 2s. '6d.

Refuse Disposal Problem.—lt was stated at the monthly • meeting of the Parochial Com- mittee, held at the Council Schools on Tuesday evening, that no word had been received from Mr. Spensely, the sanitary contractor to the committee, as to his application to the War- rington Corporation for their assistance in the matter of the Stockton Heath sanitary refuse problem. It will be recalled that -at the last meeting of the committee Mr. Spensely gave notice of the fathComing expiration of his con- tract to dispose Of the refuse owing to the fact that the tenant of the land on which he had been dumping the refuse, objected to the process going on any longer. Mr. Spensely then wrote ,to the Warrington Corporation and asked them if they could see their way to deal with the Stockton Heath sanitary refuse as they (lid with that of Warrington. It was decided at last Tuesday's meeting to communicate with the tenant of the land with a view to inducing him to come to terms. A committee, consisting of the Chairman (Mr. George Pilling), the Vice- chairman (Mr. Mclntyre) and Mr. Pearson was appointed, with full powers to deal with the matter.

A Courageous Constable,--Before Mr. F. A. Frost (in the chair), Messrs. G. Dakin, W. Fletcher, 1. J. Whitley and H. Roberts, at Daresbury petty Sessions (Stockton Heath) on Tuesday, Joseph Harrison, of 73, London-road, was summoned for failing to have control of a horse of which he was supposed to be in charge, in London-road, on May sth.—P.C. Worthington said that he stopped the horse, which was drawing a cart, and when he taxed defendant with being responsible, he said that the animal had bolted while he.was in a shop telephoning.—Defendant said he was short- handed, one of his men having enlisted, whilst another had been taken over temporarily by the Corporatqn. He had been driving horses for 32 years in Warrington, and was never cautioned before. AA he was about to go into the shop to answer the telephone, he asked a boy of sixteen who was outside if he would look after the horse a minute or so. He said he would. While defendant was telephoning, somebody shouted out that the horse had started off.—The Superintendent said that he was in- formed by an eye-witness that the horse was galloping, and that it was a courageous thing for the constables to stop it as he did.—The Bench imposed a fine of ss.

Pretty Wedding at Trinity Church.

Last week a pretty wedding took place at Trinity Church, the contracting parties. being Miss B. Starkey, late of Lichfield, and Mr. W. A. Sankey, eldest son of Mr. and. Mrs. T. Sankey, of 60, West-street. The bride was given away by .Mr. F. Taylor, of Manchester (uncle of the bridegroom), whilst Mr. T. Sankey (brother of the bridegroom) performed the duties of best manb The bride, who wa., attired in a beautiful cream satin dress, with train attached—which was carried by two pretty little children, the Misses Madge and Phyllis Giland—was attended by two brides- maids, the Misses E. and G. Sankey (sisters of the bridegroom). Suitable music was played by Mr. J. L. Millett, After the ceremony the happycouple received between 30 and 40 guests at Atkinson's Cafe, where the breakfast was served. The honeymoon is being spent at Bourneville. Many handsome and beautiful presents were received.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0332.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Church Intercession Service.

A well-attended intercession service—one of the monthly, series of services arranged by the Warrington Free Church Council—was held in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday evening.—The Chairman was Mr. Law, who reminded those present that the primary reason for their gathering together was to intercede for God's mercy during the present awful time. They still believed that there were more things wrought by prayer than this world dreamed of. —The speaker was the Rev. H. Ogden, secretary of the Free Church Council, who drew a con- trast between the Intellectualism of Christ and intellectualism without Christ. No one could doubt that there was an abundance of intellectual life in Germany, but, without the sanctifying and controlling spirit of the Cross; it had developed into systematic and scientific barbarism. He reminded the meeting that the salvation of Great Britain and the world de- pended upon not returning hatred for hatred, but upon striving to manifest Christ in the world. He further expressed the hope that the President of the Free Church Council (the Rev. T. T. James), who has gone to France, would be the instrument, in GOd's hands, of great blessing to the troops.

FIVE FRANCS FROM THE FRO.N

Generous Local Warrior's Appeal To Young Men At Home.

In a letter to Mr. J. Hallows, of Lilac Bankj Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, Private Richard H. Hatton says "We are once more in rest billets, a little way behind the firing-line, close enough to be called upon if needed. . . . lam sure that if some of the boys who hang around the street- corners could just see the spirit of the wounded when they are on their way to the hospital, they would enlist without delay. Every Britisher out here is craving for the avenge- ment of his fellow-men who have fallen doing their duty. I want you to do me a favour by getting a few more of the fellows from our village to join the colours. . . . .

Pleased to Hear the Children Are to Have

Walking Day "As Usual."

"It gives me great pleasure to hear that the people of Stockton Heath are going to give the children their Walking Day treat as utUal. It would be a great sin, I think, to rob them of their enjoyment just because one nation wants to rule the world. It also gives me great plea- sure .to be able to help them out a little. I am sorry I can't be with them to enjoy the day, but of the two I would rather be over here trying to do my little bit for my country. There is one way in which I can help to make our children of the Band of Hope happy, and that is by providing a little money with which you can buy either nuts or sweets to scramble, or prizes for racing. I am sorry I cannot send you more than five francs; we are only being paid one franc a day, but the five francs in this letter are sent with a good heart and with the hope that you will all have a very, enjoyable day."

A MUT %RV FUNERAL.

Driver R. Walton Dies After Fall From A Horse.

There were impressive scenes in Stockton Heath on Wednesday afternoon last week, when the mortal remains of. Driver Robert Walton, 129th Co. A.S.C., were interred in St. Thomas' Churchyard with semi-military honours. Driver Walton, up to the time of his enlistment in Kitchener's Army in October, resided with his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Walton, at 17, Pike-street. He died in hospital at Pur- bright, Surrey, on Saturday week; as the result, it is. understood, of a fall from a horse. Mr. and Mrs. Walton received a telegram on Friday of last week, infoigning them that their

===== 0002601_19150612_art0335.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE DRIVER WALTON.

son was seriously ill, and visited him in hospital on the following day. He passed away before they left. Twenty years of age, Driver Walton was employed—before he answered Lord Kitchener's historic call—by Messrs. Hayes Bros., as van- man. As a boy he attended St. Thomas' School, Stockton Heath. He was a member of Warrington Pigeon Flying Club. He has three cousins in the Army, viz.: Pte. Thos. Walton, of the Warrington Territorials, and Ptes. Jas. Walton and Wm. Withington. The funeral, which was largely attended, was conducted by the Rev. E. W. Clarke (curate). The Coffin was draped with the Union Jack, and there was a guard of honour. A large collec- tion of floral tributes included tokens from the officers, N.C.O.'s and men of deceased's com- pany; the Warrington Pigeon Flying Club; Messrs. Hayes Bros., and the neighbours. The chief mourners were the bereaved parents (with whom deep sympathy is felt), the Misses Annie, Charlotte and Frances Walton (sisters), Mr. and Mrs. Meredith Thomas, and Mr. and Mrs. Henry Withington (uncles and aunts), Pte. Tom Walton (cousin), Mrs. W. Thomas and the Misses Frances and ,Harriett Withington (aunts), and Miss Elsie Roughsedge.

A MANURE HEAP.

Alleged Nuisance Near Walton-road :

Residents' Complaint.

A letter was read at the monthly meeting of the Parochial Committee, in the Council Schools, on Tuesday evening, from Mr. W. Thompson, on behalf of the residents of Hawthorne-terrace, thaking a complaint in regard to a manure heap on ground occupied by Mr. John Taylor, greengrocer and fish merchant, situated in the vicinity of Walton- road. The writer alleged that the smell from decayed fish and stable refuse was unbearable, tind added that it was felt that this nuisance might well be the cause of the prevalence of fever and other forms of sickness. Mr. Farrington (sanitary inspector to the Rural District Council) was communicated with about a week ago, but nothing had yet been done.

The Clerk stated that he forwarded the letter to the Assistant Sanitary Inspector, Mr. Farrington being ill, and he had furnished a report, Mr. Farrington's report on the original complaint had also come to hand. In had investigated the matter, and been in- formed by a representative of Mr. Taylor that just before the complaint was made a small quantity of manure had been removed to a plot of ground for planting. As the plot was near the houses, the manure would doubtless smell for a time, but it had been "dug under." Fish had not been mixed with the manure, but buried. The report concluded by saying that at the time Of ihspection there was no ground for complaint, and that the premises would be kept under observation with a view to further reports being made, should the necessity arise. Not Fever-causing, But Unpleasant.

The Assistant Inspector (Mr. G. W. Lloyd) said that the manure heap, which was fifty yards to the rear of the terrace, was not offensive when he inspected it that morning. There .was no trace of the deposit of any fish. The owner promised to have the manure removed within the next few days. Mr. Warburton: It's all very well to talk of fifty yards, but you can smell it a mile away. (Laughter.) One of my tenants made the com- plaint, and I strongly object to the state of affairs. _ _ _

Mr. Westbrook said he thought it would, be a goad plan for the Sanitary Inspector to pay surprise visits to the place. Mr. Clarke expressed the opinion that the place should be repeatedly examined, adding that decayed fish, though it did not cause fever, was very unpleasant. Mr. Westbrook's suggestion was approved by the committee.

RADDON COURT HOSPITAL.

Many Gifts And Some Pressing

Needs.

FORTY-FOUR WOUNDED PATIENTS

Another highly successful " round Day" was held in connection with the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital on Thursday, resulting in the receipt of goods of various kinds to the value of £ll 195., the articles received including groceries, fruit (fresh strawberries from Kent amongst the other varieties), cigarettes, sweets, jam, butter, eggs, biscuits, flowers, notepaper, Sz.c. In addition, £3 igs. in cash was forwarded by Miss Gertrude Brown, the proceeds of an entertainment recently given at Stockton Heath, whilst Mrs. Henry Lyon gave a large ham. The chief needs at the hospital at the moment are night shirts for the patients, plants for the garden and greenhouse, screens, wastepaper baskets, day shirts, socks, slippers, soap and flower vases.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0337.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO WOUNDED MEN FROM THE DARDANELLES.

There are at the present time 4.4 patients in the Raddon Court Hospital, two of them being seriously hurt, though, happily, both are pro- gressing satisfactorily. Twelve of the patients are confined to their beds. The wounded men mostly met with their injuries in France and Belgium, but two of them were hit in the fight- ing in the neighbourhood of the Dardanelles. Included among the patients are two Canadians and two Australians.

During the present week a number of artistes from the Hippodrome kindly gave the inmates a most enjoyable entertainment in the gardens of the Court, the " turns " including one which specially fascinated the wounded warriors, viz., crack rifle-shooting by Miss Lilian. Jack Lane. "the Yorkshire Rustic," was also a great favourite.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0338.txt | included=true | included =====
MANY ENTERTAINMENTS.

On another evening a number of young men of the town gave a boxing display, and later Mr. Grounds' orchestra entertained the con- valescent soldiers, whilst on yet another even- ing the band of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancs., under Bandmaster Cahill, gave a pleasing concert. Last evening the Mayor and Mayoress (Alder- an and Mrs. Peter Peacock) visited the hospital for the purpose of presenting Red Cross certificates lately gained by Itarrington candidates, preparatory to the formtng of a new Voluntary Aid Detachment. The same evening Mr: Connor kindly provided a large 'bus to take the patients to the recruiting parade at the Drill Nall and to the subsequent open-air meeting in Victoria Park. ,

HOW THE PATIENTS HELP.

The patients, when sufficiently recovered, do light duty daily in the hospital, and also tend the gardens and greenhouse. Occasionally, too, they lend a hand with the clerical work. For their amusement such outdoor games as cricket, quoits and bowls are provided, and walks, drives and motor trips are arranged. Miss M. Broadbent, as commandant of the local Red Cross V.A.D., exercises general super- vision over the hospital, ilbf which Miss gadg e Evans is the lady superintendent. The medical officers are Drs. Bowes and Hutton, and in addition to three trained nurses, eight Red Cross nurses do duty, whilst there is a large voluntary staff of Red Cross helpers who act as cooks, waitresses and wkrd-maids.

RESULTS OF DRINK-ABUSE.

Would Drastic Government Action Be Justifiable?

The first of a series of open-air meetings was held by the Warrington Citizens' Temperance League in Bewsey-street last night, when an address was delivered by the Rev. W. Thomp- son Derry, and an appeal was made to a rather disappointing audience to sign the King's pledge. Mr. A. Lumb, who presided, said that alcohol was damaging the morale of the nation. This was recognised by the King when he promised to abstain from drinking intoxicants during the war, and gave orders that no alcoholic beverages were to be .consumed in his house- hold. The speaker appealed to his hearers to follow the King's example. If the Govern- ment would take its courage in its hands and prohibit the manufacture and sale of intoxi- cants during the war, it would be for the country's good. The Rev. W. Thompson Derry, in the course of his address, pointed out some of the evil effects of the abuse of alcohol. Sir Andrew Clark, the famous Royal physician, said that seven out or every ten people in the hospitals and infirmaries of this country were fhere through over-indulgence in strong drink. More than 180,000 people in Great Britain were annually hurried to their graves through a similar cause. Twenty-two per cent. of the men and nine per cent. of the women in British asylums" were there as the result of the abuse of alcohol. The young fellow of thirty who signed the pledge and kept it, would live three years and one month longer than if he drank intoxicants moderately. That was a fact derived by an expert from a careful study of statistics. The League proposes to hold open-air meet- ings every alternate Friday until the middle or September. The chief aim will he to obtain pledges for abstinence during the war

===== 0002601_19150612_art0343.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

EFFECTIVE DRAMA AT THE ,ROYAL COURT.

"The Sin of Her Childhood," which is this week being • presented at the Royal Court Theatre by Mr. Frank Listpn and his com- pany, is a powerful melodrama' that gets a real grip of the audiences foregathered to see There is much about it that is original, as mai he judged from the fact that it has a murder- ess, though not a wilful one, for its heroine. A mere slip of a girl, she :is persuaded by a masterful and ambitious elder brother to place poison in the baby's food, and the scene which he overcomes her scruples and 18-arils her judgment is among the strong things in contemporary melodrama. For ter crime the erring girl is sent to a reformatory, and the bulk of the remainder of the play is concerned with the hardships and temptations which befal her. there, and the many incidents in the working out of her salvation. The strict regime of the reformatory is justified for when, on, her •release, her ehameless ,brother again attempts to make.a catspaw of her, the morn/ training she has received stands her in gee stead, and she emerges triumphant from temptation. Of course, it is all highly 191- probable, but nevertheless it is most effective, the author, Mr. J. A. Campbell, having done his work with the touch of a master mele" dramatist.

The piece is forcibly acted. Miss Maud War- burton makes an appealing and convincing heroine, and Mr. J. li. Edwin is most effective as the selfish, designing brother, who stops at no villainy to gain his ends.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0344.txt | included=true | included =====
New War Play Next Week.

Next week, Royal Cot-irt patrons are to have the opportunity of witnessing an entirely nor war play, from the pen of that clever melo- dramatist, Sheila Walsh, and entitled "VP Boys and At 'Em!" The play is spoken of as being full of human interest and excitement, and seeing the subject + with which it deals, it should command packed " houses " twic° nightly.

===== 0002601_19150612_art0345.txt | included=true | included =====
.1.1( :I: LANE. TAKES HIPPODROME PATRONS BY STORM.

Such a "tarn" as that given by Jack Lane the famous Yorkshire rustic, does one good. From the moment he appears on the stage till the time when he retires amidst tumultuous applause, he keeps his audience laughing. The impression is left that the "house "—a crowded one on the occasion of our visit—would like to be amused by the irresistible rustic for the whole of the evening. The turn is certainlY one of the healthiest and best on the varietY stage, and, by the way, includes some clever pianoforte work. A contribution of an en- tirely different kind, but extremely meritorious in its way, is that of the Three Royal Dread- noughts,. who accomplish wonderful feats of marksmanship, including shooting round Or" ners, scoring bullseyes with fire-irons upside down, and shooting through a swinging 'awl)" glass. We do not remember to have see) better shooting. Novelty Revue Next Week.

The big line in next week's bill is given over to the novelty revue "Something Doing!" which was invented and arranged by George Ray, the producer of many other successful productions of similar character. The Two Tomboys, wh° claim to be the only lady-performers in their particular line of business, will present CalnledY " Humsti-Bumst " act, and the Carpatti Bros., who are Roumanians, will Pre' sent a novel and daring gymnastic turn on the parallel bars. Pynkie Whyte, "California 'Star' Comedy Girl" will appear in "Studie6 From Life."

President: HIS WORSHIP THE MAYOR (Alderman Peter Peacock). -- COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. JUNIOR CLERKSHIPS IN CORPORATION DEPARTMENTS. An EXAMINATION for the purpose of form- ing a " Waiting List" for appointments to Junior Clerkships in Corporation Offices during the twelve months commencing Ist August, 1915, will be held in .the month of July next. Vacan- cies, not filled by promotion, occurring after the date mentioned, will be filled from the Waiting List of candidates who have obtained not less than 60 per cent, of marks. Before appoint- ment Candidates will be required to submit themselves to an examination by the Medical Officer of Health. I Candidates obtaining' not less than 60 per cent. of marks will be furnished with a certificate to that. effect. Applicants must be the sons of ratepayers of the Borough, and must not be less than 14 or more than 15/ years of age on the Ist August, 1915. Forms of application may be obtained from the Town Clerk's Office, and no Candidate will be admitted for examination unless he has returned the form, duly completed, not later than noon on Thursday, the 17th day o. June, 1915. Dated this 4th day of June, 1915.. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk.

JJVERPOOL SUMMER ASSIZES, 1915. The. Commissions for holding these Assizes will be opened at Liverpool, on SATURDAY, the 12th June. Business, both Criminal and Civil, will com- mence on MONDAY .MORNING, the 14th JUNE, at 11 o'clock. The Prosecutors and Witnesses in the after- mentioned cases are required to be in attend- ance at the times specified in the following List,, and not earlier; and on all subsequent days, at the sitting of the Court; and the trials are to proceed in the order in which the cases are arranged in the List, unless the Court shall otherwise direct, viz.:— , On MONDAY, the 14th JUNE, at 11 o'clock a.m . The King versus Gebhard Phillip Brack, bigamy James Joseph Lamb, manslaughter; William Henry Smith, criminal assault; Charles Reginald Bell, criminal assault; George nirner, bigamy; John Robert Rowland, abduction; Philip Andrew Maughan, criminal assault; And all other cases. All Indictments settled by Counsel must be forwarded to me Engrossed • and Abstracted. The foregoing Regulations will be strictly enforced; and persons making default in the observance of them will be liable to have their costs disallowed. HERBERT STEPHEN, 'Clerk of Assize. CIOUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. 1-1 NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE ,OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 28th day of June, 1915, at Half- past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals -arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton? Chester Castle, Daresbury, Eddisburv, Nantwich, Run- corn, Winsford, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from the last-mentioned Divisions will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 28th day of June, 1915, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be adjourned to the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, on WEDNES- DAY, the 30th day of June, 1915, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Buck- low, Dukinfield, Middlewich, Sandbach. Congle- ton, Northwith, Prestbury, and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 30th day of June, 1915, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon. TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

intimation was conveyed through the medium of a letter„written by Sergeant Allcock himself, and dated June Bth. " Nothing To Get Alarmed At" He wrote I am quite well as regards - health, but I have got a slight wound in the calf of the leg. It is nothing to get alarmed at. Do not write until you hear. from me 'again, as I do not know which hospital I shall be sent to. I may come to England." A Liberal Worker. Sergeant Allcock, who belongs to D Company, has been in the old Volunteers and the Terri- torials for the long period of twenty-one years. After doing the necessary war training at Tun- bridge Wells and Edinburgh, he was drafted to the Front in February. He is a member of the Liberal Club, and is on the party's Town Hall Ward Committee. KILLED. CORPL. CHARLES 'WOOLDRIDGE KILLED. Time-Expired Man Who Rejoined On The Outbreak Of War. News reached his parents at 11, Brooklands- street, on Thursday evening of last week that Corp. Chas. Wooldridge of "A" Company, Ist Battalion Lancashire Fusiliers, had been

killed in action in the fighting near the Dar- danelles on or about May 27th. Corporal Wooldridge was a single man, 34 years of age, and made his home with his parents. • As a boy he attended the Hamilton- street School, and he worshipped at St. Peter's Church. His time of service in the Army ex- pired on March 2nd, but he rejoined on the outbreak of the war, and was drafted to the Front on March 30th. ' He was employed, prior to the war, as a straightener by Messrs. Ryland& Bros., Ltd. ••••••••••=••••••••••••••• DIED OF WOUNDS. SERGEANT ERNEST CLAYTON. • Keen Follower, Of The Warrington Football Team Dies Of Wounds. Last- week-end word came ihrough that Sergiant Ernest Clayton, son of Mr. Samuel Clayton, of 15, Guardian-street, Warrington, had died of wounds. • It appears that he was wounded on the 24th May, and died on the 27th. Prior to joining the 2nd Seaforth Highlanders four years ago, Sergt. Clayton was employed by Mr. Carter, baker, of Bridge-street. He was well-known in Warrington, where lie had many friends. He saw practically all the fighting

FEARNHEAD• Disastrous Fire at Corn Merchant's.—A dis- astrous fire took place on the premises of Mr. 0. G. Litton, corn merchant, Pearnhead, at about 7.30 on Wednesday evening, the place being completely burnt out. There was a quantity of hay and straw stored in the mill and it burned furiously when once alight. The Warrington Fire Brigade, with the motor fire engine, were soon on the spot, and succeeded in saving the stables adjoining the mill. The horses had previously been, removed to a place of safety by some of the neighbours. Mr. 'Litton, who was away from home at the time, was sent for, but the fire had got such a hold that all attempts to cope with it proved un- successful. The property is owned by Mr. Bennett, Padgate, and the damage is covered by insurance. APPLETON• , A Lightless Motor-cycle.--At the Daresbury Petty Sessions, held at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, Alfred Sinclair Allbright was fined 20s. for riding a motor-bicycle without a light, in Tarporley-road, Appleton, on the 30th of last. month.
